# 1. INTRODUCTION AND OVERVIEW

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|Part B: Thoracic Surgery]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|Part C: PC]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#3. Professionalism|Part C: Professionalism]]

> [!info]- Exam Context
> **Exam Weighting:** High (Core subspecialty area with significant clinical complexity)

---

## 1.1. HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT AND CURRENT PRACTICE

**Modern thoracic anesthesia represents over 100 years of continuous innovation in surgical and anesthetic techniques.**

Thanks to more than 100 years of improvements in anesthesia and surgical techniques, complex thoracic procedures are now routinely performed in patients who would not have been considered candidates in the past [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. One-lung ventilation (OLV) is a critical part of thoracic anesthesia practice, and is essential for lung, esophageal, mediastinal, spinal, and cardiac procedures performed via thoracic approaches [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. 

* **Evolution of techniques**: Minimally invasive intrathoracic procedures rely heavily on OLV for adequate surgical exposure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Equipment advancement**: The wide variety of double-lumen endotracheal tubes (DLTs) and endobronchial blockers that are currently available enables OLV to be provided safely and reliably for virtually all patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Expanded patient population**: Procedures now performed in patients previously considered too high-risk for surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

## 1.2. LUNG CANCER EPIDEMIOLOGY AND SURGICAL CONSIDERATIONS
**Lung cancer remains the leading cause of cancer death globally, driving increased demand for thoracic surgical procedures.**

* **Global impact**: Lung cancer is currently estimated to be responsible for 1.59 million deaths annually worldwide [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Leading cause of cancer death**: Responsible for almost 25% of all cancer deaths [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Comparative mortality**: Each year, more people die from lung cancer than from colon, breast, and prostate cancers combined [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 1.2.1. UNITED STATES STATISTICS

* **New cases (2022)**: 236,740 new cases (117,910 men, 118,830 women) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Deaths (2022)**: Approximately 130,180 deaths (68,820 men, 61,360 women) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Gender risk**: Overall risk of developing lung cancer is greater in women (1 in 13) than men (1 in 16) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Age demographics**: Average age at diagnosis is about 70 years; less than 2% occur in those aged 45 years or less [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Positive trend**: The number of new lung cancer cases continues to decrease due to people stopping smoking and advances in early detection and treatment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 1.2.2. IMPACT ON THORACIC SURGERY VOLUME

The increased incidence of lung cancer has led to an increase in the amount of noncardiac thoracic surgery performed in the United States [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Lung cancer's status as the leading cancer killer globally, combined with an aging population and evolving surgical techniques, makes thoracic anesthesia an increasingly important subspecialty requiring specialized knowledge and skills.

# 2. PREOPERATIVE EVALUATION AND ASSESSMENT

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#Preoperative Management|Part B: Perioperative Medicine]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|Part B: Thoracic Surgery]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|Part C: PC]]

> [!info]- Exam Context
> **Exam Weighting:** High (Critical foundation for safe thoracic surgery)

---

## 2.1. COMPREHENSIVE RISK ASSESSMENT FRAMEWORK
**All patients undergoing thoracic surgery require thorough evaluation for comorbid risk factors regardless of age, procedure type, or disease extent.**

All patients undergoing thoracic surgery, regardless of age, type of planned surgery, or extent of underlying disease, should be thoroughly evaluated for comorbid risk factors [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. These include but are not limited to the severity of their underlying pulmonary disease, the presence of cardiovascular disease, nutritional status, and any or all medical conditions whose modification could potentially improve outcome [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 2.1.1. MAIN PURPOSES OF PREOPERATIVE EVALUATION

* **Risk assessment**: Assess the risk associated with anesthesia and surgery throughout the perioperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Comorbidity optimization**: Identify, modify, and optimize the treatment of comorbidities that may affect perioperative outcome [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Anesthetic planning**: Provide the basis for the design of an appropriate, individually tailored, anesthetic plan [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Critical determination**: ⚠️ Determine whether the patient will be able to tolerate the planned lung resection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 2.1.2. ADDITIONAL BENEFITS

* **Patient education**: Patient education and informed consent [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Preparation optimization**: Providing advanced knowledge to clinicians when preparation needs vary from the usual routine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Schedule efficiency**: Avoiding delay or cancellation on the day of surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 2.1.3. COMMON POSTOPERATIVE COMPLICATIONS

The most common complications following thoracic surgery are pulmonary in nature; the most frequent are pneumonia and atelectasis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It is more difficult to predict postoperative pulmonary complications following elective cardiothoracic surgery compared with noncardiothoracic surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 2.1.4. HIGH-RISK FACTORS

Thoracic surgery is known to be high risk, and patient factors that have been associated with increased risk include [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Advanced age
* Poor general health status
* Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD)
* Body mass index greater than 30 kg/m²
* Low forced expiratory volume in 1 second (FEV₁)
* Low predicted postoperative FEV₁

## 2.2. PATIENT HISTORY AND CLINICAL EVALUATION
### 2.2.1. AGE AS RISK FACTOR

Age has repeatedly been shown to be an independent predictor of operative risk and postoperative complications, resulting in increased morbidity and mortality [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In addition, age is one of the more prominent risk factors for the development of atrial fibrillation in the postoperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Pulmonary complications following surgery increase after the fifth decade of life [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

💡 **Clinical pearl**: Although there is no upper age limit that precludes a patient from surgery, physiologic age is likely a better predictor of outcome than chronologic age [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Elderly patients with adequate cardiopulmonary reserve and without additional significant risk factors may still have an acceptable level of risk for surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 2.2.2. DYSPNEA ASSESSMENT

**Dyspnea occurs when the requirement for ventilation is greater than the patient's ability to respond appropriately.**

* **Quantification methods**:
  * Degree of physical activity required to produce dyspnea [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
  * Level of activity possible (ability to walk on level ground or climb stairs) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
  * Management of daily activities [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Clinical significance**: Severe exertional dyspnea usually implies a significantly diminished ventilatory reserve and an FEV₁ less than 1,500 mL, with possible need for postoperative ventilatory support [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 2.2.3. COUGH EVALUATION

* **Chronic bronchitis definition**: Recurrent productive cough for 3 months of the year for 2 consecutive years is necessary to make the diagnosis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Clinical impact**: Cough indirectly increases airway irritability [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Sputum assessment**: Volume, consistency, and color should be assessed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Diagnostic workup**: Sputum should be cultured to rule out infection and establish need for preoperative antibiotic therapy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ **Warning signs**: Blood-stained sputum or episodes of gross hemoptysis should alert the anesthesiologist to the possibility of a tumor invading the respiratory tract (e.g., the main stem bronchus), which might interfere with endobronchial intubation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 2.2.4. CIGARETTE SMOKING IMPACT

**Cigarette smoking is the main risk factor for developing lung cancer and dramatically increases perioperative complications.**

* **Primary risk factor**: Cigarette smoking is the main risk factor for developing lung cancer and, in the United States, is linked to about 90% of lung cancers [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Toxic exposure**: Tobacco smoke is a toxic mix of more than 7,000 chemicals [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Risk magnitude**: Cigarette smokers have a 15 to 30 times greater likelihood of developing and/or dying from lung cancer than nonsmokers [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Former smokers**: Have a lower risk than active smokers but still greater than people who never smoked [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.2.4.1. PERIOPERATIVE IMPLICATIONS

* **Multisystem risk**: Well-established risk factor for COPD and heart disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Cancer risk**: Dramatically increases, up to 20-fold, the risk of lung and esophageal cancers [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Postoperative complications**: Smokers have increased postoperative morbidity with positive correlation between frequency of complications and number of pack-years [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.2.4.2. SMOKING CESSATION EVIDENCE

A large retrospective study by Matsuoka et al. assessed the relationship between preoperative smoking habits and postoperative pulmonary complications in 1,248 thoracic surgical patients who underwent lobectomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Key findings:
* Higher incidence of postoperative complications in smokers than nonsmokers [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Frequency of respiratory-related complications increased with pack-years [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **No relationship found** between length of preoperative smoking cessation period and frequency of complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Clinical practice variation**: Some thoracic surgeons require 2-week minimum cessation, but majority don't require cessation if it would delay surgery due to concerns about disease progression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 2.2.5. CHRONIC OBSTRUCTIVE PULMONARY DISEASE

**COPD is the most common comorbid condition in the thoracic surgical population.**

* **Epidemiology**: Third leading cause of death in the United States [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Current prevalence**: 3.8 million adults currently diagnosed with COPD in the United States [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Pathophysiology**: Associated with abnormal and permanent enlargement of airspaces with destruction of alveolar walls, resulting in [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
  * Loss of gas exchange surface area
  * Air-trapping
  * Hyperinflation of airspaces distal to terminal bronchioles

#### 2.2.5.1. CLINICAL CONSEQUENCES

* Compromised lung mechanics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Hypercapnia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Dyspnea [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Hypoxia often requiring oxygen therapy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Progressive and debilitating disease impacting quality of life and healthcare utilization [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.2.5.2. RISK ASSESSMENT

COPD severity can be categorized based on PFT-predicted values using Global Initiative for Obstructive Lung Disease criteria [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. COPD is a major patient-related risk factor predictive of [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Postoperative pneumonia
* Prolonged mechanical ventilation
* Respiratory failure requiring reintubation
* Right ventricular dysfunction or cor pulmonale (complicating intraoperative management)

### 2.2.6. EXERCISE TOLERANCE ASSESSMENT

* **Stair climbing test**: Patients who can walk up three or more flights of stairs are at reduced risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **High-risk indicator**: Those unable to climb two flights are generally at increased risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Best evaluation**: Actually the history of the patient's quality of life [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Screening principle**: Otherwise healthy patient with good exercise tolerance generally does not require additional screening tests [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

⚠️ **Important limitation**: Quality of life measures alone have been shown to correlate poorly with FEV₁, DLCO, and exercise testing, and should not substitute for actual testing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 2.2.7. RISK FACTORS FOR ACUTE LUNG INJURY

In some cases, thoracic surgery may lead to acute lung injury (ALI) postoperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. 

#### 2.2.7.1. PERIOPERATIVE RISK FACTORS

* **Preoperative**: Alcohol abuse, patients undergoing pneumonectomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Intraoperative**: High ventilatory pressures, excessive fluid administration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Additional factors**: Poor preoperative pulmonary function and positive fluid balance on postoperative day 1 have been identified as independent risk factors for lung injury in patients undergoing open thoracotomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Comprehensive preoperative evaluation must assess multiple risk factors including age, smoking history, COPD severity, exercise tolerance, and ALI risk factors to develop an appropriate anesthetic plan and optimize patient outcomes.

## 2.3. PHYSICAL EXAMINATION TECHNIQUES
**The physical examination should systematically assess respiratory pattern, cardiovascular status, and signs of underlying disease.**

### 2.3.1. RESPIRATORY PATTERN ASSESSMENT

#### 2.3.1.1. CYANOSIS RECOGNITION

* **Peripheral cyanosis**: Present in fingers, toes, or ears - distinguish from poor circulation (acrocyanosis) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Central cyanosis**: Present in buccal mucosa, usually secondary to arterial hypoxemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ **Clinical significance**: If cyanosis is present, arterial hemoglobin saturation with oxygen is 80% or less (PaO₂ <50 to 52 mmHg), indicating limited margin of respiratory reserve [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.3.1.2. CLUBBING ASSESSMENT

Clubbing of fingers and toes is often seen in patients with chronic lung disease, malignancies, or congenital heart disease associated with right-to-left shunt [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 2.3.1.3. RESPIRATORY RATE AND PATTERN ANALYSIS

* **Severe dyspnea indicator**: Patient's inability to complete a normal sentence without pausing for breath [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Inspiratory paradox**: Abdomen moving in while chest moves out, suggests diaphragmatic fatigue and respiratory dysfunction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Physical signs to assess**:
  * Paroxysmal retraction (Hoover sign) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
  * Limited diaphragmatic movement due to hyperinflation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
  * Asymmetry of chest movement secondary to phrenic nerve involvement, hemothorax, pleural effusion, and pneumothorax [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.3.1.4. BREATHING PATTERN PHYSIOLOGY

* **Obstructive disease strategy**: For constant minute ventilation, work done against airflow resistance decreases when breathing is slow and deep [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Restrictive disease strategy**: Work done against elastic resistance decreases when breathing is rapid and shallow (e.g., pulmonary infarct or pulmonary fibrosis) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.3.1.5. BREATH SOUNDS INTERPRETATION

* **Wet sounds (crackles)**: Usually caused by excessive fluid in airways and indicate sputum retention or edema [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Dry sounds (wheezes)**: Produced by high-velocity gas flow through bronchi and are a sign of airway obstruction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Distant sounds**: Indication of emphysema and possibly bullae [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Tracheal position**: Should be in midline [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

⚠️ **Critical assessment**: Displacement of the trachea may be secondary to a number of causes, including mediastinal mass, and should alert the anesthesiologist to a potentially difficult intubation or airway obstruction on induction of anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 2.4. CARDIOVASCULAR SYSTEM ASSESSMENT
**Cardiac complications are the second most common cause of perioperative morbidity and mortality after pulmonary complications.**

### 2.4.1. INCIDENCE AND RISK FACTORS

* **Complication rate**: Reported incidence of adverse cardiac event following major pulmonary surgery is approximately 10% to 15% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Smoking connection**: Since smoking increases cardiac disease incidence by over 60% and causes lung cancer, this correlation is not surprising [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Procedure-related risk**: Intrathoracic surgery itself is associated with increased risk of cardiac complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 2.4.2. DIAGNOSTIC RECOMMENDATIONS

* **Basic screening**: Electrocardiogram before surgery is appropriate even in otherwise asymptomatic individuals [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Age-based requirement**: Recommended in all patients above age 65 years [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Symptomatic patients**: Anginal symptoms, suspected valvular disease, arrhythmias, shortness of breath, or history of cardiac disease warrant appropriate evaluation and noninvasive cardiac testing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 2.4.3. PULMONARY VASCULAR RESISTANCE EVALUATION

**One of the most important factors in evaluation is the presence of increased pulmonary vascular resistance (PVR).**

#### 2.4.3.1. NORMAL PULMONARY CIRCULATION

The pulmonary circulation is normally a low-pressure, high-compliance system capable of handling an increase in blood flow by recruitment of normally underperfused vessels [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This acts as a compensatory mechanism that normally prevents an increase in pulmonary arterial pressure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 2.4.3.2. COPD-RELATED CHANGES

In COPD, there is distention of the pulmonary capillary bed with decreased ability to tolerate an increase in blood flow (decreased compliance) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Such patients demonstrate an increase in PVR when cardiac output increases due to decreased ability to compensate for increased pulmonary blood flow [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 2.4.3.3. CLINICAL SIGNS OF PULMONARY HYPERTENSION

* Narrowly split second heart sound [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Increased intensity of pulmonary component of second heart sound [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Right ventricular and atrial hypertrophy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.4.3.4. ANESTHETIC IMPLICATIONS

⚠️ **Critical consideration**: An increase in PVR is significant during anesthesia because several factors further increase PVR and likelihood of right ventricular failure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Acidosis
* Sepsis
* Hypoxia
* Application of positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP)

### 2.4.4. LEFT HEART FUNCTION ASSESSMENT

In patients with ischemic or valvular heart disease, the function of the left side of the heart should also be carefully evaluated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 2.4.5. ELECTROCARDIOGRAM FINDINGS

A patient with COPD may present with electrocardiographic features of right atrial and ventricular hypertrophy and strain [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Low-voltage QRS complex**: Due to lung hyperinflation and poor R-wave progression across precordial leads [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **"P pulmonale"**: Enlarged P-wave in standard lead II diagnostic of right atrial hypertrophy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Right ventricular hypertrophy**: R/S ratio greater than 1 in lead V1 (R-wave voltage exceeds S-wave voltage) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 2.4.6. CHEST RADIOGRAPHY FINDINGS

* **COPD changes**: Hyperinflation and increased vascular markings usually present [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Bronchitis pattern**: Prominent lung markings often occur [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Emphysema pattern**: Lung markings decreased, particularly at bases where actual bullae may be present in severe cases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Hyperinflation signs**: Increased anteroposterior chest diameter, enlarged retrosternal air space >2 cm diameter on lateral chest radiograph [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.4.6.1. TUMOR ASSESSMENT

* **Location determination**: Lung lesion location should be assessed by posteroanterior and lateral projections [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Complication indicators**: Tracheal or carinal shift, mediastinal mass may indicate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
  * Difficulty with ventilation
  * Difficult and bloody dissection
  * Difficulty placing double-lumen tube (due to main stem bronchus deviation)
  * Collapsed lobe owing to bronchial obstruction with possible sepsis
* **Advanced imaging**: Review of computed tomography (CT) study is useful and often provides more information about tumor size and location than chest radiograph [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 2.4.7. ARTERIAL BLOOD GAS ANALYSIS

#### 2.4.7.1. COPD PATIENT PATTERNS

* **Common finding**: Hypoventilation and CO₂ retention [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**"Blue Bloaters" (Chronic Bronchitis)** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Cyanotic, hypercarbic, hypoxemic, usually overweight
* Chronic respiratory failure state
* Decreased ventilatory response to CO₂
* High PaCO₂ increases cerebrospinal fluid bicarbonate concentration
* Medullary chemoreceptors reset to higher CO₂ level with decreased sensitivity
* Hypoventilate when given high oxygen concentrations due to decreased hypoxic drive

**"Pink Puffers" (Emphysema)** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Typically thin, dyspneic, and pink
* Essentially normal arterial blood gas values
* Increased minute ventilation to maintain normal PaCO₂
* Explains increased work of breathing and dyspnea

#### 2.4.7.2. CORRELATION WITH INTRAOPERATIVE VALUES

The preoperative PaO₂ correlates with the intraoperative PaO₂ during OLV, but the intraoperative PaO₂ during two-lung ventilation correlates more closely [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Physical examination must systematically evaluate respiratory patterns, cardiovascular status, and signs of underlying disease, with particular attention to indicators of pulmonary hypertension, right heart strain, and airway compromise that may complicate anesthetic management.

## 2.5. PULMONARY FUNCTION TESTING AND RESECTABILITY EVALUATION
**Pulmonary function testing serves three critical goals in evaluating patients scheduled for lung resection.**

There are three goals in performing pulmonary function tests in a patient scheduled for lung resection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
1. **Risk identification**: Identify the patient at risk of increased postoperative morbidity and mortality
2. **Ventilation requirements**: Identify the patient who will need shortor long-term postoperative ventilatory support
3. **Bronchodilator response**: Evaluate the beneficial effect and reversibility of airway obstruction with bronchodilators

### 2.5.1. FUNDAMENTAL CLINICAL QUESTION

In thoracic surgery for lung cancer, the specific question is: How much lung tissue may be safely removed without making the patient a pulmonary cripple? [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This should be weighed against the 1-year mean survival rate of the patient with surgically untreated lung carcinoma [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 2.5.2. EFFECTS OF ANESTHESIA AND SURGERY ON LUNG VOLUMES

**Anesthesia and postoperative medications cause significant changes in lung volumes and ventilatory patterns.**

* **Total lung capacity (TLC)**: Decreases after abdominal surgery but not after surgery on an extremity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Vital capacity**: Decreased by 25% to 50% within 1 to 2 days after surgery and generally returns to normal after 1 to 2 weeks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Residual volume (RV)**: Increases by 13% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Expiratory reserve volume**: Decreases by 25% after lower abdominal surgery and 60% after upper abdominal and thoracic surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Tidal volume (VT)**: Decreases by 20% within 24 hours after surgery and gradually returns to normal after 2 weeks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Pulmonary compliance**: Decreases by 33% with similar reductions in functional residual capacity (FRC) secondary to small airway closure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 2.5.3. PATIENT POPULATION CHARACTERISTICS

Most patients who undergo lung resection are smokers with a certain degree of COPD [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. They are prone to postoperative complications in direct relation to the amount of lung to be resected (lobectomy or pneumonectomy) and to the severity of the preoperative lung disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 2.5.4. SPIROMETRY ASSESSMENT

#### 2.5.4.1. KEY PARAMETERS AND CLINICAL SIGNIFICANCE

**Forced vital capacity (FVC), FEV₁, maximum voluntary ventilation (MVV), and RV/TLC correlate with outcome following thoracic surgery.**

* **Abnormal vital capacity**: Can be identified in 30% to 40% of postoperative deaths [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Complication correlation**: Patient with abnormal vital capacity has 33% likelihood of complications and 10% risk of postoperative mortality [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.5.4.2. FEV₁ ASSESSMENT

* **Clinical significance**: FEV₁ is a more direct indication of airway obstruction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Historical contraindication**: Previously, FEV₁ <800 mL in a 70-kg man was considered absolute contraindication to lung resection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Modern practice**: With advent of thoracoscopic surgery and improved postoperative pain management, patients with smaller lung volumes are now successfully undergoing surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Preferred reporting**: Better to indicate FEV₁ as percentage of predicted value rather than absolute results in liters, as implications may differ depending on patient and percentage accounts for age and size [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.5.4.3. FEV₁/FVC RATIO

* **Diagnostic utility**: Useful in differentiating between restrictive and obstructive pulmonary diseases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Restrictive disease**: Ratio is normal because both FEV₁ and FVC decrease proportionally [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Obstructive disease**: Ratio is usually low because FEV₁ is markedly decreased compared to FVC [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.5.4.4. MAXIMUM VOLUNTARY VENTILATION (MVV)

* **Interpretation**: Nonspecific test and indicator of both restriction and obstruction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Risk indicator**: MVV <50% of predicted value is generally accepted as indication of high risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Systematic evaluation**: Has not been systematically evaluated as predictor of morbidity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.5.4.5. RV/TLC RATIO

* **High-risk threshold**: Ratio >50% is generally indicative of high-risk patient for pulmonary resection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.5.4.6. PREDICTED POSTOPERATIVE FEV₁

**Calculation**: By multiplying preoperative FEV₁ by percentage of lung tissue expected to remain following resection, a predicted postoperative FEV₁ can be calculated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

* **Reduced risk**: Patients with predicted postoperative FEV₁ value above 40% are at reduced risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Increased risk**: Those with predicted postoperative FEV₁ below 30% are at increased risk and more likely to need postoperative ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 2.5.5. FLOW-VOLUME LOOPS ANALYSIS

**The flow-volume loop displays essentially the same information as spirometer but is more convenient for specific flow rate measurement.**

#### 2.5.5.1. NORMAL FLOW-VOLUME LOOP CHARACTERISTICS

The shape and peak airflow rates during expiration at high lung volumes are effort dependent, but indicate the patency of larger airways [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Effort-independent expiration occurs at low lung volumes and usually reflects small airway resistance, best measured by forced expiratory flow (FEF) during middle half of FVC (FEF₂₅₋₇₅%) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 2.5.5.2. OBSTRUCTIVE DISEASE PATTERN

In patients with obstructive airway disease (asthma, bronchitis, emphysema) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Grossly decreased FEV₁/FVC ratios due to increased airway resistance and decreased FEV₁
* Peak expiratory flow rate and MVV usually decreased
* TLC increases secondary to increases in RV
* Effort-independent portion of flow-volume curve is markedly depressed inward
* Reduction of flow rate at 25% to 75% of FVC

#### 2.5.5.3. RESTRICTIVE DISEASE PATTERN

In patients with restrictive disease (pulmonary fibrosis, scoliosis) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Decrease in FVC with relatively normal FEV₁
* Normal airway resistance, so FEV₁/FVC is also normal
* TLC markedly decreased
* MVV and FEF₂₅₋₇₅% usually normal
* Flow-volume curves normal in shape but with decreased lung volumes and peak flow rates

#### 2.5.5.4. SIGNIFICANCE OF BRONCHODILATOR THERAPY

Pulmonary function tests are usually performed before and after bronchodilator therapy to assess reversibility of airway obstruction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

* **Assessment value**: Useful in evaluating degree of airway obstruction and patient's effort ability [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Positive response**: 15% improvement in pulmonary function tests may be considered positive response and indicates therapy should be initiated before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Prognostic significance**: Overall prognosis of COPD is better related to level of spirometric function after bronchodilator therapy than to baseline function [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 2.5.6. SPLIT-LUNG FUNCTION TESTS

**Regional lung function studies serve to predict the function of lung tissue that would remain after lung resection.**

#### 2.5.6.1. RATIONALE

A whole (two)-lung test may fail to estimate whether the amount of postresection lung tissue will allow the patient to function at reasonable level of activity without disabling dyspnea or cor pulmonale [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 2.5.6.2. REGIONAL PERFUSION TEST

Involves intravenous injection of insoluble radioactive xenon (¹³³Xe) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The peak radioactivity of each lung is proportional to the degree of perfusion of each lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 2.5.6.3. REGIONAL VENTILATION TEST

Using an inhaled, insoluble radioactive gas, the peak radioactivity over each lung is proportional to the degree of ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Combining radiospirometry with whole-lung testing (FEV₁, FVC, maximal breathing capacity) has resulted in fair degree of correlation between predicted volumes and pulmonary function tests measured after pneumonectomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 2.5.6.4. ADVANCED IMAGING STUDIES

**Computed Tomography and Positron Emission Tomography Scans**:
* **CT scan**: Provides anatomic sections through chest and can delineate tumor size [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Can also reveal if there is airway or cardiovascular compression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **PET scan**: Uses glucose analog labeled with radionuclide positron emitter [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Can detect tumor based on metabolic activity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Tumor detection principle**: Malignant tumors grow at fast rate compared to healthy tissue, so tumor cells use up more sugar with radionuclide attached [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Lymph node assessment**: Greater uptake by malignant mediastinal lymph nodes than benign nodes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Combined PET-CT**: Mass seen on CT scan is more likely malignant if it also demonstrates enhanced glucose uptake on PET scan [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Clinical applications**: PET may be more accurate than CT for mediastinal staging and can be used to follow results of lung cancer treatments [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 2.5.7. DIFFUSING CAPACITY FOR CARBON MONOXIDE

**The ability of the lung to perform gas exchange is reflected by the diffusing capacity for carbon monoxide (DLCO).**

#### 2.5.7.1. CLINICAL SIGNIFICANCE

* **Impairment conditions**: Impaired in disorders such as interstitial lung disease, which affects alveolar-capillary site [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Risk threshold**: Predicted postoperative DLCO below 40% is associated with increased risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Strongest predictor**: Predicted postoperative diffusing capacity percent is the strongest single predictor of risk of complications and mortality after lung resection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Independence from FEV₁**: Little interrelationship between predicted postoperative diffusing capacity percent and predicted postoperative FEV₁, indicating these values should be assessed independently when estimating operative risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.5.7.2. RESEARCH EVIDENCE

* **Study of 956 patients**: Lower DLCOPPO (diffusing capacity for carbon monoxide, predicted postoperative) and preoperative chemotherapy were predictive of postoperative complications. FEV₁ was not found to be predictive [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Strongest factor**: DLCOPPO was the most predictive factor for postoperative morbidity and mortality [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Both procedures**: PPO FEV₁ and DLCO are predictive for postoperative pulmonary complications in both minimally invasive and open lobectomies [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Risk stratification**: If PPO FEV₁ and DLCO are greater than 60%, the patient is at low risk, and no further testing is needed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 2.5.8. MAXIMAL OXYGEN CONSUMPTION

**Cardiopulmonary exercise testing (CPET) is a safe and reliable examination that objectively evaluates the function of both the heart and lungs under load.**

#### 2.5.8.1. CLINICAL UTILITY

The inability to perform preoperative CPET suggests that the patient has limited aerobic capacity and may not be suitable for thoracic surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The most critical index in assessing suitability for lung resection is the peak oxygen consumption (VO₂ max), which is defined as the maximum oxygen uptake by the body despite increasing workload [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. VO₂ max reflects the upper limit of the cardiovascular response and reflects overall cardiorespiratory fitness [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 2.5.8.2. RISK STRATIFICATION BY VO₂ MAX

**High Risk (Good Prognosis)**:
* VO₂ max >20 mL/kg/min or >75% predicted can safely undergo planned lung resection, including pneumonectomy with good prognosis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Intermediate Risk**:
* VO₂ max between 10 and 15 mL/kg/min or between 35% and 75% predicted have increased risk of perioperative morbidity and mortality compared with patients with VO₂ max >75% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**High Risk (Poor Prognosis)**:
* VO₂ max <10 mL/kg/min or <35% predicted are extremely high risk for postoperative morbidity and mortality and often regarded as relative contraindication to major anatomic resections [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* May require reduction of originally planned resection or nonoperative treatment modality [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.5.8.3. ALTERNATIVE TESTING METHODS

**Exercise Oximetry**:
* Decrease of 4% during exercise is associated with increased risk [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**6-Minute Walk Test**:
* <2,000 feet has been correlated with VO₂ max below 15 mL/kg/min and with decrease in oximetry reading during exercise [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Percentage Thresholds**:
* Percentage of predicted VO₂ max may be better indicator for risk
* Threshold of 50% to 60% could be established without increase in surgical mortality [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.5.8.4. STAIR CLIMBING TESTS

Multiple studies have evaluated stair climbing as alternative assessment:
* **Brunelli and Fianchini**: Patients climbing >14 m could safely undergo surgery, while those climbing <12 m with predicted postoperative FEV₁ below 35% were not considered suitable candidates for major lung resection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Correlation with mortality**: Inability to do maximal stair climbing has been correlated with increased mortality following major lung resection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Recent criteria**: Climbing to 20 m with speed of at least 15 m/min correlated with meeting qualifying criteria for pneumonectomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **VO₂ correlation**: Climbing 22 m has been correlated with VO₂ of at least 15 mL/kg/min, although stair climbing testing is not standardized [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.5.8.5. PREDICTIVE SCORING SYSTEMS

An 11-point scoring scale has been developed for predicting postoperative pulmonary complications: ASA physical status of 3 or higher, emergency or high-risk surgery, and history of CHF or chronic lung disease were independent predictors for reintubation following extubation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Pulmonary function testing provides essential data for risk stratification and surgical decision-making, with DLCO and VO₂ max being the strongest predictors of postoperative complications and mortality in lung resection patients.

## 2.6. ADVANCED IMAGING AND DIAGNOSTIC STUDIES
**Advanced imaging provides crucial anatomical and functional information for surgical planning and risk assessment.**

### 2.6.1. COMPUTED TOMOGRAPHY APPLICATIONS

* **Anatomic detail**: Provides anatomic sections through chest and can delineate tumor size [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Compression assessment**: Can reveal if there is airway or cardiovascular compression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Superior information**: Often provides more information about tumor size and location than chest radiograph [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 2.6.2. POSITRON EMISSION TOMOGRAPHY (PET) SCANNING

#### 2.6.2.1. TECHNICAL PRINCIPLES

* **Tracer mechanism**: Uses glucose analog labeled with radionuclide positron emitter [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Detection method**: Can detect tumor based on metabolic activity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Physiological basis**: Malignant tumors grow at fast rate compared with healthy tissue, so tumor cells use up more sugar that has radionuclide attached [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 2.6.2.2. CLINICAL APPLICATIONS

* **Lymph node assessment**: Greater uptake by malignant mediastinal lymph nodes than benign nodes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Staging accuracy**: PET may be more accurate than CT for mediastinal staging [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Treatment monitoring**: Can be used to follow results of lung cancer treatments [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Combined imaging**: PET-CT scans can be done simultaneously to produce combined scan [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Enhanced accuracy**: Mass seen on CT scan is more likely malignant if it also demonstrates enhanced glucose uptake on PET scan [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** Comprehensive preoperative evaluation combines clinical assessment, pulmonary function testing, and advanced imaging to accurately stratify risk and optimize patient selection for thoracic surgery.

---

# 3. PREOPERATIVE PREPARATION AND OPTIMIZATION

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#Preoperative Management|Part B: Perioperative Medicine]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|Part B: Thoracic Surgery]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#6. Systems-Based Practice|Part C: SBP]]

> [!info]- Exam Context
> **Exam Weighting:** High (Critical for reducing perioperative complications)

---

## 3.1. ENHANCED RECOVERY AND PREHABILITATION PROTOCOLS
**Enhanced recovery and prehabilitation represent evolving paradigms in perioperative care optimization.**

Enhanced recovery after surgery protocols and postoperative pulmonary rehabilitation are now ingrained in perioperative pathways, but preoperative rehabilitation, or prehabilitation, is a relatively new concept [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The aim of thoracic prehabilitation is to reduce perioperative risk for patients who are suitable for surgery or, in some cases, to move a patient who was previously unfit for surgery into a group which is suitable for surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. For both these cohorts, the chance of survival may be increased [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 3.1.1. PHYSIOLOGICAL RATIONALE

The wide spectrum of physiologic changes that occur during thoracic surgery puts patients at great risk of developing postoperative complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Morbidity and mortality increase when these changes are superimposed on an acutely or chronically compromised patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 3.1.2. CONDITIONS REQUIRING AGGRESSIVE TREATMENT

Several conditions show particular correlations with postoperative complications and require vigorous preoperative preparation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Infection
* Dehydration
* Electrolyte imbalance
* Wheezing
* Obesity
* Cigarette smoking
* Cor pulmonale
* Malnutrition

💡 **Key principle**: Proper, vigorous preoperative preparation can improve the patient's ability to face surgery with decreased risk of morbidity and mortality [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It is important that conditions predisposing to postoperative complications be rigorously treated before surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 3.2. SMOKING CESSATION STRATEGIES
**There is extensive evidence that smoking patients are at increased risk for postoperative respiratory complications, but cessation strategies remain controversial.**

### 3.2.1. PREVALENCE AND IMPACT

There is a high prevalence of smoking among patients presenting for surgery, and there is extensive evidence that these patients are at increased risk for development of postoperative respiratory complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 3.2.2. PHYSIOLOGICAL EFFECTS OF SMOKING

Smoking increases airway irritability, decreases mucociliary transport, decreases FVC and FEF₂₅₋₇₅%, and increases secretions, thereby increasing the incidence of postoperative pulmonary complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 3.2.3. BENEFITS OF CESSATION

#### 3.2.3.1. SHORT-TERM BENEFITS (48 HOURS)

Cessation of smoking 48 hours before surgery has been shown to [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Decrease percentage of carboxyhemoglobin
* Shift oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve to the right
* Increase oxygen availability

#### 3.2.3.2. LONG-TERM BENEFITS (4-6 WEEKS)

Cessation of smoking for a period longer than 4 to 6 weeks before surgery is associated with decreased incidence of postoperative complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 3.2.3.3. OPTIMAL BENEFITS (2-3 MONTHS)

Most beneficial effects of cessation of smoking usually occur 2 to 3 months after smoking has ceased [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Improvement in ciliary function
* Improvement in closing volume
* Increase in FEF₂₅₋₇₅%
* Reduction in sputum production

### 3.2.4. EVIDENCE-BASED RECOMMENDATIONS

#### 3.2.4.1. STUDY FINDINGS

* **No paradoxical increase**: One study showed no evidence of paradoxical increase in postoperative complications in patients who stopped smoking within 2 months before undergoing thoracic resection for lung tumor [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Mortality and complications**: Smoking is associated with increased mortality and pulmonary complications, but these can be decreased by preoperative cessation; the risk decreases with longer cessation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **8-week threshold**: Recent study indicated that discontinuation of smoking for more than 8 weeks prior to surgery can help improve postoperative pulmonary function [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 3.2.4.2. META-ANALYSIS RESULTS

* **<8 weeks**: One meta-analysis indicated that there was no improvement with cessation of smoking for less than 8 weeks [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Progressive benefit**: Another meta-analysis indicated progressive improvement with each week of abstinence from smoking [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

## 3.3. NUTRITIONAL ASSESSMENT AND OPTIMIZATION
**Nutritional status is a modifiable risk factor that significantly impacts surgical outcomes.**

### 3.3.1. RISK FACTOR ASSESSMENT

Low BMI is a known risk factor for patients undergoing lung resection surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This is a modifiable risk factor, and several scoring systems exist to detect malnutrition [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 3.3.2. CLINICAL APPROACH

Every patient interaction is an opportunity to reinforce the importance of optimizing the nutritional state [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. There is no accepted definition for perioperative malnutrition, which should encompass both underand overnourished states [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 3.3.2.1. RISK CATEGORIES

* **Overnourished patients**: At risk of postoperative complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Undernourished patients**: With lung cancer may be sarcopenic and have limited reserve to cope with surgical insult [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 3.3.3. INTERVENTION STRATEGIES

#### 3.3.3.1. BASIC GOALS

* Avoiding malnutrition [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Supporting anabolism [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 3.3.3.2. IMPLEMENTATION METHODS

* Screening [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Referral to dietetics [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Nutritional prehabilitation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 3.3.3.3. TREATMENT OPTIONS

* Oral nutritional supplements [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Vitamins [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Immunonutrition [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Protein [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Carbohydrate loading [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 3.3.4. PERIOPERATIVE NUTRITION SCREEN (PONS)

The American Society for Enhanced Recovery and Perioperative Quality Initiative published a Joint Consensus Statement on Nutrition Screening and Therapy Within a Surgical Enhanced Recovery Pathway [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 3.3.4.1. PONS COMPONENTS

The PONS determines presence of nutrition risk based on [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Patient's BMI
* Recent changes in weight
* Reported recent decrease in dietary intake
* Preoperative albumin level

#### 3.3.4.2. ALBUMIN AS PREDICTOR

The PONS includes evaluation of preoperative albumin level, as this is a predictor of postoperative complications, including morbidity/mortality [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 3.4. INFECTION MANAGEMENT AND ANTIBIOTIC PROPHYLAXIS
**Aggressive treatment of existing infections and appropriate prophylaxis are essential for optimal outcomes.**

### 3.4.1. TREATMENT PHILOSOPHY

Although not all surgeons routinely administer antibiotics prophylactically to their patients, any infection present before surgery should be vigorously treated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 3.4.2. ACUTE ILLNESS MANAGEMENT

Treatment of the acutely ill patient depends on the results of the Gram stain of the sputum and blood cultures, and broad-spectrum antibiotics are commonly used [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 3.4.3. PROPHYLACTIC ANTIBIOTIC PROTOCOL

* **Standard agent**: Cefazolin is routinely administered perioperatively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Exceptions**: Unless the patient is already receiving antibiotics or has modifying circumstances such as allergic history [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Timing**: To be most effective, antibiotics need to be given prior to skin incision [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 3.4.4. EVIDENCE FOR EFFECTIVENESS

In one prospective study, the incidence of mortality was lower in the group treated with prophylactic antibiotics compared with the untreated group (9% vs. 17%), and a lower incidence of postoperative pulmonary infection was also found [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 3.5. RESPIRATORY OPTIMIZATION AND BRONCHODILATION
### 3.5.1. HYDRATION AND SECRETION MANAGEMENT

**Correction of hypovolemia and electrolyte imbalance should be accomplished before surgery.**

#### 3.5.1.1. RATIONALE

Adequate hydration decreases the viscosity of bronchial secretions and facilitates their removal from the bronchial tree [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Humidification of inspired gas is extremely useful [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 3.5.1.2. PHARMACOLOGICAL ADJUNCTS

* **Mucolytic drugs**: Such as acetylcysteine (Mucomyst) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Oral expectorants**: Potassium iodide [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 3.5.1.3. NON-PHARMACOLOGICAL METHODS

Commonly used methods for removing secretions from the bronchial tree include [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Postural drainage
* Vigorous coughing
* Chest percussion
* Deep breathing
* Use of incentive spirometer

💡 **Patient cooperation**: These modalities often require patient cooperation and frequent verbal encouragement to maximize benefit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 3.5.2. WHEEZING MANAGEMENT

#### 3.5.2.1. EMERGENCY APPROACH

⚠️ **Medical emergency**: The presence of acute wheezing represents a medical emergency, and elective surgery should be postponed until effective treatment has been instituted [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 3.5.2.2. PATHOPHYSIOLOGY OF CHRONIC WHEEZING

Chronic wheezing is often seen in patients with COPD and is attributable to the presence of gas flow obstruction secondary to [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Smooth muscle contraction
* Accumulation of secretions
* Mucosal edema

#### 3.5.2.3. ASSESSMENT OF OBSTRUCTION

* **Localized obstruction**: Smooth muscle contraction may occur in small airways only (detectable by changes in FEF₂₅₋₇₅%) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Widespread obstruction**: May cause large reduction of FEV₁ and FVC [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 3.5.2.4. BRONCHODILATOR TRIAL

⚠️ **Critical evaluation**: The efficacy of bronchodilators in reversing the bronchospastic component is extremely important [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. A trial of bronchodilators and measurement of their effects on pulmonary function should be performed in any patient who shows evidence of airflow obstruction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 3.5.3. BRONCHODILATOR CLASSES AND MECHANISMS

#### 3.5.3.1. SYMPATHOMIMETIC DRUGS

**Mechanism**: Sympathomimetic drugs increase the formation of 3′, 5′-cyclic adenosine monophosphate (cAMP) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The balance between cAMP (which produces bronchodilation) and cyclic guanosine monophosphate (cGMP, which produces bronchoconstriction) determines the state of contraction of the bronchial smooth muscle [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Increasing cAMP production, therefore, causes relaxation of the bronchial tree [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Mixed β₁/β₂ Agonists**:
* Epinephrine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Isoproterenol [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Isoetharine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Ephedrine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Limitation**: β₁ (cardiac effects) of these drugs are often undesirable in patients with COPD [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Selective β₂ Agonists** (Preferred):
* Albuterol [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Terbutaline [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Metaproterenol [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Administration**: Given as inhaled aerosols, are preferred drugs for treatment of bronchospasm, particularly in patients with cardiac disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 3.5.3.2. PHOSPHODIESTERASE INHIBITORS

**Mechanism**: Phosphodiesterase inhibitors inhibit the breakdown of cAMP by cytoplasmic phosphodiesterase [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The methylxanthines, such as aminophylline, increase the level of cAMP, resulting in bronchodilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Additional Benefits**:
* Improves diaphragmatic contractility [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Increases the patient's resistance to fatigue [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Dosing Protocol**:
* **Therapeutic levels**: 5 to 20 μg/mL [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Loading dose**: 5 to 7 mg/kg over 20 minutes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Maintenance**: Continuous intravenous infusion of 0.5 to 0.7 mg/kg/h [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

⚠️ **Side effects**: Aminophylline may cause ventricular dysrhythmias, and this side effect should be borne in mind when treating patients who have myocardial ischemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Because newer medications have fewer side effects, aminophylline is now rarely used [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 3.5.3.3. STEROIDS

**Mechanism**: Although not true bronchodilators, steroids are traditionally considered to decrease mucosal edema and may prevent the release of bronchoconstricting substances [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. They are of questionable benefit in acute bronchospasm [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Administration routes**:
* Oral [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Parenteral [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Aerosol form (such as beclomethasone by inhaler) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 3.5.3.4. CROMOLYN SODIUM

**Mechanism**: Stabilizes mast cells and inhibits degranulation and histamine release [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Clinical use**: Useful in prevention of bronchospastic attacks but is of little value in treatment of acute situation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 3.5.3.5. PARASYMPATHOLYTIC DRUGS

**Agents**: Include atropine and ipratropium [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Historical concerns**: In the past, atropine has been avoided in patients with COPD and bronchitis because of concern regarding increases in viscosity of mucus produced by this agent [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Mechanism**: Atropine blocks the formation of cGMP and therefore has a bronchodilator effect [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 3.6. PULMONARY REHABILITATION PROGRAMS
**Structured pulmonary rehabilitation programs provide measurable benefits in thoracic surgical patients.**

### 3.6.1. EVIDENCE BASE

Sekine et al. reported that pulmonary rehabilitation led to reduced hospital stay and improved postoperative FEV₁, compared with a historical control group [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 3.6.1.1. PROGRAM COMPONENTS

The pulmonary rehabilitation included education in a variety of areas such as [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Breathing techniques
* Exercise programs
* Nutrition education

### 3.6.2. CLINICAL BENEFITS

Preoperative physical therapy in patients undergoing surgery for lung cancer led to better oxygenation and shortened hospital stay in the treatment group [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 3.6.3. MULTIMODAL APPROACH REQUIREMENTS

Prehabilitation must be multimodal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It is the sum of marginal gains from several interventions that are themselves complementary `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It requires [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Invested resources
* Infrastructure
* Organization
* Acceptance of longer-term benefits for surgical outcomes, value for money, and public health

### 3.6.4. IMPLEMENTATION CHALLENGES

Thoracic surgical patients awaiting resection of cancer have limited time available between diagnosis and surgery, and other related hospital appointments or treatments might place both a physical and psychological burden on the patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Comprehensive preoperative preparation including smoking cessation, nutritional optimization, infection management, respiratory preparation, and structured rehabilitation programs can significantly reduce perioperative morbidity and mortality in thoracic surgical patients.

---

# 4. INTRAOPERATIVE MONITORING SYSTEMS

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#3.3 Clinical Monitoring|Part A: Clinical Monitoring]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|Part B: Thoracic Surgery]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|Part C: PC]]

> [!info]- Exam Context
> **Exam Weighting:** High (Essential for safe thoracic anesthesia)

---

## 4.1. STANDARD MONITORING REQUIREMENTS
**All patients undergoing thoracic surgery require adherence to ASA standards plus specialized monitoring considerations.**

All patients undergoing anesthesia for thoracic surgical procedures require adherence to the Standards of Basic Anesthetic Monitoring of the American Society of Anesthesiologists (ASA) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In particular, these include [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Electrocardiogram**: Lead II and, if possible, V5 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Chest or esophageal stethoscopes**: For heart and breath sound auscultation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Temperature probe**: Continuous temperature monitoring [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 4.1.1. SPECIALIZED THORACIC CONSIDERATIONS

* **Chest stethoscope placement**: May be placed over the dependent hemithorax to assess dependent lung ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Pulse oximetry importance**: Standard of care, especially valuable during thoracic surgery because hypoxemia may occur during OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 4.1.2. DYSRHYTHMIA MONITORING RATIONALE

Dysrhythmias occur commonly both during and after thoracic surgery, making the usual need for continuous electrocardiographic monitoring even more important [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.1.2.1. INTRAOPERATIVE DYSRHYTHMIAS

* **Supraventricular tachyarrhythmias**: May be caused by cardiac manipulation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **OLV-related dysrhythmias**: May be a sign of inadequate oxygenation or ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 4.1.2.2. POSTOPERATIVE DYSRHYTHMIAS

May be related to [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Sympathetic nervous system stimulation from pain
* Decreased pulmonary vascular bed following lung resection

#### 4.1.2.3. PATIENT POPULATION CONSIDERATIONS

Patients who present for lung resection often have COPD due to cigarette smoking, have right-sided heart strain, and are prone to multifocal atrial tachyarrhythmias [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 4.1.3. LEAD SELECTION STRATEGY

* **Lead II**: The axis parallels that of the P-wave, making this lead useful for dysrhythmia detection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Lead V5**: Simultaneous monitoring allows for monitoring of anterolateral wall myocardial ischemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Multiple leads**: Use increases the sensitivity for ischemia detection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

## 4.2. DIRECT ARTERIAL CATHETERIZATION
**Peripheral arterial catheterization has become an essential tool for thoracic anesthesiologists managing major procedures.**

### 4.2.1. CLINICAL INDICATIONS

Arterial catheterization allows for [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Continuous beat-to-beat blood pressure measurement**: Critical during thoracic surgery because surgical manipulations may result in cardiac compression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Frequent arterial blood gas sampling**: Essential for managing patients undergoing one-lung anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Immediate recognition**: Of sudden bleeding or hemodynamic changes allowing time for proper identification and appropriate treatment [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 4.2.2. PHYSIOLOGICAL RATIONALE FOR BLOOD GAS ANALYSIS

* **Hypoxemia mechanism**: Arterial hypoxemia may occur because of inadequate hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction (HPV) response and shunting of mixed venous blood through the collapsed lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Acid-base monitoring**: Significant changes in acid-base status and hyperventilation or hypoventilation can be identified [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 4.2.3. CATHETER PLACEMENT STRATEGIES

#### 4.2.3.1. FOR MEDIASTINOSCOPY

**Two approaches**:

1. **Right radial approach**: Place catheter in right arm and use to monitor for possible compression of innominate artery by mediastinoscope. Helps avoid central nervous system complications from inadequate cerebral blood flow via right carotid artery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

2. **Left radial approach**: Allows continuous blood pressure measurements uninterrupted by innominate artery compression. If this approach is used, pulse oximeter probe should be placed on right upper extremity to monitor for innominate artery compression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 4.2.3.2. FOR THORACOTOMY

* **Dependent arm placement**: Placement in dependent arm can monitor for possible axillary artery compression, which may occur if patient is not properly positioned [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 4.2.3.3. FOR BRIEF PROCEDURES

For brief thoracoscopy case in relatively healthy patient, it would be acceptable to proceed without arterial catheter, as long as pulse oximeter is functioning reliably [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Example: healthy patient presenting for bilateral VATS sympathectomy for hyperhidrosis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 4.2.4. FLUID MANAGEMENT CONSIDERATIONS

The patient undergoing pulmonary resection, and especially right pneumonectomy, is at risk for postoperative pulmonary edema [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It is especially important to not fluid overload such patients, as likelihood of postoperative edema is greater with increased intraoperative fluid administration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.2.4.1. FLUID RESPONSIVENESS ASSESSMENT

It would be preferable to identify whether patient would be likely to respond favorably to fluid bolus prior to its administration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Traditional CVP limitations**: The central venous pressure (CVP) may not accurately reflect intravascular volume status, and is no longer recommended as guide for fluid responsiveness [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Dynamic Parameters**:
* **Systolic pressure variation (SPV)** and **pulse pressure variation (PPV)** have been reported as being able to predict fluid responsiveness [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **PPV thresholds**: Cannesson et al. found PPV >13% predicted fluid responsiveness, <9% predicted non-responsiveness, and 9% to 13% reflected gray zone [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Stroke volume variation**: Has been reported to predict fluid responsiveness specifically in patients undergoing thoracic surgery during OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

## 4.3. CENTRAL VENOUS AND PULMONARY ARTERY MONITORING
### 4.3.1. CENTRAL VENOUS PRESSURE MONITORING

#### 4.3.1.1. CLINICAL APPLICATIONS

The CVP may reflect patient's blood volume, venous tone, and right ventricular performance; however, it is also affected by central venous obstructions and alterations of intrathoracic pressure such as PEEP [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The CVP reflects right-sided heart function, not left ventricular performance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.3.1.2. INDICATIONS FOR PLACEMENT

Catheters for measuring CVP may be placed for thoracotomies, and in particular, patients undergoing pneumonectomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Uses of CVP catheters or large-bore introducers**:
1. Insertion of transvenous pacemaker where necessary [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
2. Infusion of vasoactive drugs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
3. Insertion of pulmonary artery (PA) catheter, which may subsequently be required during surgery or in postoperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 4.3.1.3. ACCESS SITE SELECTION

* **External jugular vein**: From external jugular vein [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Internal jugular vein**: From internal jugular vein (highest success rate using right internal jugular vein) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Subclavian veins**: From subclavian veins [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Arm veins**: From one of the arm veins [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Preferred approach**: Success rate is highest using right internal jugular vein, and pacemaker or PA catheter can be inserted most easily from this vein [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.3.1.4. POSITIONING COMPLICATIONS

* **External jugular limitation**: Major disadvantage during thoracotomy is that catheter often kinks when patient is turned to lateral decubitus position [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Subclavian risk**: Technique leads to higher incidence of pneumothorax, which can be disastrous if it occurs in dependent lung during OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Safety consideration**: If necessary and if possible, subclavian catheter should be placed ipsilateral to surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 4.3.1.5. CURRENT CLINICAL PRACTICE

The CVP is no longer considered accurate guide for fluid responsiveness [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. However, it is common practice among thoracic anesthesiologists to place CVP catheter for certain thoracic cases such as esophagectomy and pneumonectomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 4.3.2. PULMONARY ARTERY CATHETERIZATION

#### 4.3.2.1. INSERTION TECHNIQUE

The PA catheter is most reliably inserted through the right internal jugular vein using a modified Seldinger technique [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Insertion through either external jugular vein or subclavian vein often leads to obstruction of catheter when patient is placed in lateral decubitus position [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.3.2.2. INTERPRETATION CHALLENGES

⚠️ **Critical limitation**: Misinterpretation of data from PA catheter is real risk in patient with cardiac and pulmonary diseases undergoing thoracic surgery with OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. These errors can be produced by [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Altered ventilatory modes
* Location of PA catheter tip
* Ventricular compliance changes
* Ventricular interdependence

#### 4.3.2.3. PCWP LIMITATIONS

A major limitation of the PA catheter is the assumption that pulmonary capillary wedge pressure (PCWP) provides good approximation of left ventricular end-diastolic volume [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Pressure-volume relationship assumptions**:
* Use of PCWP directly to assess preload assumes linear relationship between ventricular end-diastolic volume and ventricular end-diastolic pressure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Compliance changes**: Alterations in ventricular compliance affect this pressure-volume relationship during surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Factors causing decreased ventricular compliance**:
* Myocardial ischemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Shock [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Right ventricular overload [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Pericardial effusion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 4.3.2.4. RESEARCH EVIDENCE ON LIMITATIONS

Numerous investigators have demonstrated poor correlation between PCWP and left ventricular end-diastolic volume in acutely ill patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This correlation is further worsened by application of PEEP [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.3.2.5. VENTRICULAR INTERDEPENDENCE

Ventricular interdependence can cause misdiagnosis when interventricular septum encroaches on left ventricular cavity, leading to increased values of PCWP [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].
