A PCWP associated with decreased cardiac output can be interpreted as left ventricular failure, when in fact, left ventricular end-diastolic volume may not be increased but decreased because of compression of left ventricle by distended right ventricle [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This situation can occur with acute respiratory failure and high levels of PEEP [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

💡 **Alternative techniques**: Techniques such as echocardiography, which directly measure ventricular dimensions, may facilitate resolution of this complex situation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.3.2.6. CATHETER TIP POSITION CONSIDERATIONS

Because most pulmonary blood flow is to right lower lobe, tip of flow-directed PA catheter is usually located in right lower lobe [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Left thoracotomy with OLV**: Catheter tip would be in dependent lung and should provide accurate hemodynamic measurements [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Right thoracotomy with OLV**: Catheter tip would most likely be in nondependent lung, and may not be accurate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 4.3.2.7. CLINICAL APPLICATIONS

* **Safety indicator**: Use of intraoperative mean PA pressure has been reported to be indicator of safety for lung resection under thoracotomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Safety limits**: Following occlusion of main PA, upper safety limits of 33 mmHg for right, and 35 mmHg for left thoracotomy could be used (difference between sides was minimal) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Mixed venous oxygen saturation**: Monitoring has been evaluated in patients undergoing one-lung anesthesia; changes were mainly dependent on changes in arterial oxygen saturation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 4.3.2.8. CURRENT RECOMMENDATIONS

Currently, use of PA catheter for monitoring during thoracic surgery is generally unnecessary, and may be reserved for patients with pulmonary hypertension [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 4.4. TRANSESOPHAGEAL ECHOCARDIOGRAPHY APPLICATIONS
**Transesophageal echocardiography (TEE) is a useful intraoperative monitor with specific applications in thoracic surgery.**

### 4.4.1. GENERAL CLINICAL APPLICATIONS

TEE is useful intraoperative monitor for [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Ventricular function
* Valvular function
* Wall motion changes that might reflect ischemia

### 4.4.2. THORACIC SURGERY APPLICATIONS

Its use in thoracic surgical patients has been limited, but it is widely used in patients undergoing lung transplant [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The use of TEE requires special training, and may not be available at all centers [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.4.2.1. ROUTINE USE RECOMMENDATIONS

A recent review concluded that although intraoperative use of TEE is not routinely indicated, it may be useful for diagnosing right ventricular dysfunction in the setting of hypotension or arrhythmias following lung resection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.4.2.2. RIGHT VENTRICULAR DYSFUNCTION DETECTION

Right ventricular dysfunction may occur during [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* OLV
* Clamping of PA for pneumonectomy
* Lung transplantation

TEE may be used to help determine if it is necessary to initiate cardiopulmonary bypass during lung transplantation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 4.4.3. TUMOR ASSESSMENT APPLICATIONS

#### 4.4.3.1. HILAR LUNG TUMORS

TEE may be useful in visualizing hilar lung tumors, and evaluating possible extension into the heart [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In one study [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Central lung tumors were able to be seen with TEE in 9 of 9 patients
* Peripheral lung tumors in 1 of 3 patients
* Anterior mediastinal mass in 1 of 1 patient

**Additional findings**: TEE also revealed PA compression in 5 patients and PA infiltration in 2 patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.4.3.2. CARDIAC INVOLVEMENT ASSESSMENT

In another study investigating echocardiographic recognition of mediastinal tumors, TEE revealed that these tumors were often adjacent to the heart and identified those patients in whom there was compression of innominate vein or PA, or infiltration of heart [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 4.4.4. SURGICAL DECISION-MAKING APPLICATIONS

Intraoperative TEE has also been used to reveal tumor invasion of the heart, which indicates that a resection by thoracotomy without cardiopulmonary bypass is not feasible [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.4.4.1. COMPLICATION DETECTION

* **Tumor embolization**: In one case report, TEE monitoring during attempted resection of tumor invading left atrium showed embolization of tumor. Fragments were seen to pass through aortic valve. Patient subsequently died of disseminated metastases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Aortic dissection**: In exploratory thoracotomy for hemothorax, intraoperative TEE revealed presence of subacute aortic dissection, which was believed to be cause of hemothorax [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 4.4.5. MEDIASTINAL MASS APPLICATIONS

* **Hemodynamic assessment**: TEE was used intraoperatively to evaluate large anterior mediastinal mass, providing data on right ventricular outflow compression, and ventricular contractility and filling status [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Diagnostic accuracy**: In another recent report, mediastinal mass was diagnosed intraoperatively using TEE; in that case, mass had been misdiagnosed preoperatively with transthoracic echocardiography as pericardial effusion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 4.4.6. ADDITIONAL NONINVASIVE MONITORING

#### 4.4.6.1. CEREBRAL OXIMETRY

Although data are presently limited, it has been reported that decreased cerebral oximetry values by absolute cerebral oximetry during OLV have been correlated with postoperative complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In subsequent study, larger decreases in cerebral oxygen saturation occurred in patients with better preoperative lung function [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. At this time, data are still too limited to recommend cerebral oximetry as routine monitor during thoracic surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.4.6.2. NONINVASIVE CARDIAC OUTPUT

A meta-analysis of the use of noninvasive cardiac output measurements during surgery revealed poor agreement with thermodilution [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Noninvasive cardiac output measurements are not commonly utilized during thoracic surgery and thus are not recommended at this time [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 4.5. OXYGENATION AND VENTILATION MONITORING
### 4.5.1. OXYGENATION MONITORING

#### 4.5.1.1. BASIC REQUIREMENTS

During administration of all thoracic surgical anesthetics, the concentration of inspired oxygen in the breathing system must be measured using oxygen analyzer with low oxygen concentration limit alarm: this is basic monitoring standard [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Such analyzers vary in sophistication from fuel cells to rapidly responding paramagnetic analyzers that monitor oxygen breath by breath and display oxygram (analogous to, and mirror image of, the capnogram) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.5.1.2. CLINICAL ASSESSMENT

Adequacy of blood oxygenation must be ensured, and adequate illumination and exposure of patient are helpful to assess the color of shed blood or presence of cyanosis of lips, nail beds, or mucous membranes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Most patients undergoing thoracic surgical or diagnostic procedures have arterial catheter in place for continuous monitoring of blood pressure and sampling of arterial blood for blood gas analyses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.5.1.3. PULSE OXIMETRY APPLICATIONS

Pulse oximetry is standard of care for noninvasive assessment of blood oxygenation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The use of pulse oximetry is especially important during OLV, when rapid assessment of oxygenation is critical [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. A low SpO₂ reading provides clinician with indication for blood gas sampling and laboratory analysis of arterial blood [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.5.1.4. ADVANCED PULSE OXIMETRY

* **Traditional limitations**: Two-wavelength pulse oximeter may display spurious readings of SpO₂ in presence of dyshemoglobins, methemoglobin, and carboxyhemoglobin [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Multiwavelength systems**: Multiwavelength (8 or 12 wavelengths) pulse oximeters are available that can measure carboxyhemoglobin, methemoglobin, deoxygenated hemoglobin, and oxygenated hemoglobin (HbO₂%) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Hemoglobin monitoring**: Continuous noninvasive monitoring of total hemoglobin concentration is also available [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 4.5.2. VENTILATION MONITORING

#### 4.5.2.1. QUALITATIVE ASSESSMENT

All patients must be continually monitored to ensure adequacy of ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Monitoring includes qualitative signs such as [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Chest excursion**: Visual observation of lungs when chest is open [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Auscultation**: Of breath sounds [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Specialized placement**: During OLV, stethoscope can be placed on chest wall under ventilated dependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 4.5.2.2. MECHANICAL MONITORING

During controlled ventilation, circuit lowand high-pressure alarms with audible signal must be used [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The following should be monitored [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Respiratory rate
* VT
* Minute volume
* Inflation pressures

#### 4.5.2.3. BLOOD GAS ANALYSIS

Adequacy of ventilation is confirmed by monitoring arterial blood gas analyses, and PaCO₂ in particular [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This may be estimated continuously and noninvasively by capnography [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 4.5.2.4. CAPNOGRAPHY APPLICATIONS

* **PaCO₂ estimation**: End-tidal CO₂ concentration represents alveolar CO₂ (PACO₂), which approximates PaCO₂ [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Normal gradient**: There is normally small arterial-to-alveolar CO₂ difference (4 to 6 mmHg), depending on alveolar dead space [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Diagnostic utility**: Capnogram waveform is also helpful in diagnosing airway obstruction, incomplete relaxation, and even malposition of DLT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 4.5.2.5. OLV-SPECIFIC CONSIDERATIONS

During OLV, systemic hypoxemia is usually greater problem than hypercarbia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This is because CO₂ is approximately 20 times more diffusible than oxygen and PaCO₂ is more dependent on ventilation, compared with PaO₂, which is more dependent on perfusion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Comprehensive intraoperative monitoring for thoracic surgery requires standard ASA monitoring plus specialized arterial access, careful hemodynamic assessment, and enhanced oxygenation/ventilation monitoring tailored to the unique physiological challenges of one-lung ventilation.

---

# 5. ONE-LUNG VENTILATION: PHYSIOLOGY AND MANAGEMENT

> **Syllabus**: [[mmed_anaes_part_a_syllabus#1.2 Respiratory System|Part A: Respiratory Physiology]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#Ventilation|Part B: Ventilation]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|Part B: Thoracic Surgery]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#1. Medical Knowledge|Part C: MK]]

> [!info]- Exam Context
> **Exam Weighting:** Very High (Core technique for thoracic surgery)

---

## 5.1. PHYSIOLOGICAL PRINCIPLES AND POSITIONING EFFECTS

**Understanding the physiology of lateral decubitus position and one-lung ventilation is fundamental to safe thoracic anesthesia.**

### 5.1.1. PHYSIOLOGY OF THE LATERAL DECUBITUS POSITION

Ventilation and blood flow under physiologically routine conditions are discussed in Chapter 15 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. These variables will now be considered as they pertain to the lateral decubitus position under six circumstances that are encountered during thoracic surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.1.1.1. LATERAL POSITION, AWAKE, BREATHING SPONTANEOUSLY, CHEST CLOSED

In the lateral decubitus position, the distribution of blood flow and ventilation is similar to that in the upright position, but turned by 90 degrees [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Blood flow and ventilation to the dependent lung are significantly greater than that to the nondependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Good matching at the level of the dependent lung results in adequate oxygenation in the awake patient who is breathing spontaneously [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Two important concepts**:
1. **Hydrostatic pressure gradient**: Because perfusion is gravity dependent, the vertical hydrostatic pressure gradient is smaller in the lateral than in the upright position; therefore, zone 1 is usually less extended [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
2. **Diaphragmatic function**: The dependent hemidiaphragm is pushed higher into the chest by the abdominal contents compared with the nondependent lung hemidiaphragm. During spontaneous ventilation, the conserved ability of the dependent diaphragm to contract results in an adequate distribution of VT to the dependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Key principle**: Because most of the perfusion is to the dependent lung, the matching in this position is maintained similar to that in the upright position [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.1.1.2. LATERAL POSITION, AWAKE, BREATHING SPONTANEOUSLY, CHEST OPEN

Controlled PPV is the most common way to provide adequate ventilation and ensure gas exchange in an open-chest situation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Frequently, thoracoscopy is performed using intercostal blocks with the patient breathing spontaneously to allow proper lung examination [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The thoracoscope provides an adequate seal of the open chest to prevent a "free" open-chest situation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Two complications can arise from spontaneous breathing with open chest**:

1. **Mediastinal shift**: Usually occurring during inspiration. The negative pressure in the intact hemithorax, compared with the less negative pressure of the open hemithorax, can cause the mediastinum to move vertically downward and push into the dependent hemithorax [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The mediastinal shift can create circulatory and reflex changes that may result in a clinical picture similar to that of shock and respiratory distress [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

⚠️ **Emergency conversion**: Sometimes, depending on the severity of the distress, it may be necessary to convert emergently to general anesthesia in order to intubate the patient and initiate PPV. The anesthesiologist must be prepared to intubate in lateral position without disturbing the surgical field [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

1. **Paradoxical breathing**: During inspiration, the relatively negative pressure in the intact hemithorax compared with atmospheric pressure in the open hemithorax can cause movement of air from the nondependent lung into the dependent lung. The opposite occurs during expiration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This gas movement reversal from one lung to the other represents wasted ventilation and can compromise the adequacy of gas exchange [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Paradoxical breathing is increased by a large thoracotomy or by an increase in airway resistance in the dependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. PPV or adequate sealing of the open chest eliminates paradoxical breathing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.1.1.3. LATERAL POSITION, ANESTHETIZED, BREATHING SPONTANEOUSLY, CHEST CLOSED

The induction of general anesthesia does not cause significant change in the distribution of blood flow, but it has an important impact on the distribution of ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Most of the VT enters the nondependent lung, and this results in a significant mismatch [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Mechanisms of ventilation-perfusion mismatch**:
* **FRC reduction**: Induction of general anesthesia causes reduction in volumes of both lungs secondary to reduction in FRC [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Greater dependent lung impact**: For several reasons, any reduction in volume in the dependent lung is of greater magnitude than that in the nondependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
  1. **Diaphragmatic displacement**: Cephalad displacement of dependent diaphragm by abdominal contents is more pronounced and is increased by paralysis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
  2. **Mediastinal compression**: Mediastinal structures pressing on dependent lung or poor positioning of dependent side on operating table prevents lung from expanding properly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Compliance curve effects**: These factors move the lungs to lower volume on S-shaped volume-pressure curve. The nondependent lung moves to steeper position on compliance curve and receives most of VT, whereas dependent lung is on flat (noncompliant) part of curve [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.1.1.4. LATERAL POSITION, ANESTHETIZED, BREATHING SPONTANEOUSLY, CHEST OPEN

Opening the chest has little impact on distribution of perfusion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. However, the upper lung is now no longer restricted by the chest wall and is free to expand, resulting in further increase in mismatch as the nondependent lung is preferentially ventilated, owing to now increased compliance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.1.1.5. LATERAL POSITION, ANESTHETIZED, PARALYZED, CHEST OPEN

During paralysis and PPV, diaphragmatic displacement is maximal over the nondependent lung, where there is the least amount of resistance to diaphragmatic movement caused by the abdominal contents [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This further compromises the ventilation to the dependent lung and increases the mismatch [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.1.1.6. OLV, ANESTHETIZED, PARALYZED, CHEST OPEN

During two-lung ventilation in the lateral position, the mean blood flow to the nondependent lung is assumed to be 40% of cardiac output, whereas 60% of cardiac output goes to the dependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Normally, venous admixture (shunt) in the lateral position is 10% of cardiac output and is equally divided as 5% in each lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Therefore, the average percentage of cardiac output participating in gas exchange is 35% in the nondependent lung and 55% in the dependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Shunt calculation during OLV**:  
OLV creates obligatory right-to-left transpulmonary shunt through the nonventilated, nondependent lung because the percentage of cardiac output participating in gas exchange in that lung is zero by definition [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In theory, an additional 35% should be added to the total shunt during OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. However, assuming active HPV, blood flow to the nondependent hypoxic lung will be decreased by 50% and therefore becomes (35/2) = 17.5% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. 5% must be added to this to account for the obligatory shunt through the nondependent lung, and therefore the shunt through the nondependent lung is 22.5% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Together with the 5% shunt in the dependent lung, the total shunt fraction during OLV is 22.5% + 5% = 27.5% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This results in PaO₂ of approximately 150 mmHg for an inspired oxygen fraction (FIO₂) of 1.0 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Critical matching considerations**: Because 72.5% of the perfusion is directed to the dependent lung during OLV, the matching of ventilation in this lung is important for adequate gas exchange [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The dependent lung is no longer on the steep (compliant) portion of the volume-pressure curve because of reduced lung volume and FRC [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Factors impairing dependent lung ventilation**:
* General anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Paralysis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Pressure from abdominal contents [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Compression by weight of mediastinal structures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Suboptimal positioning on operating table [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Absorption atelectasis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Accumulation of secretions [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Formation of transudate in dependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

All these create low V̇/Q̇ ratio and large P(A-a)O₂ gradient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 5.2. LUNG ISOLATION VS. SEPARATION INDICATIONS
**In any given case, the anesthesiologist must distinguish between the need for lung separation versus lung isolation.**

This distinction of lung isolation versus lung separation is important when selecting the method to provide OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. When lung protection is necessary, DLTs are preferable to endobronchial blockers (BBs) because the low-pressure high-volume cuff of the BB would not provide an adequate protective seal to prevent contamination of the dependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.2.1. LUNG ISOLATION REQUIREMENTS

**Whenever the nondiseased lung is threatened with contamination by blood or pus from the diseased lung, the nondiseased lung must be isolated.**

#### 5.2.1.1. LIFE-SAVING FUNCTION

Lung isolation may be lifesaving by simply preventing drowning or severe contamination of the ventilated lung from the nondependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Furthermore, the nondiseased lung must maintain adequate gas exchange [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Lung protection will prevent further deterioration of overall pulmonary function [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.2.1.2. CASES REQUIRING LUNG ISOLATION

* **Hemoptysis** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Empyema** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Contaminants**: Where any contaminant in the noninvolved lung could lead to severe atelectasis, pneumonia, sepsis, and inadequate ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.2.1.3. BRONCHOPLEURAL FISTULA CONSIDERATIONS

A large bronchopleural or bronchocutaneous fistula can result in little or no ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In this situation, the decreased resistance to flow in the affected lung results in most of the PPV being directed toward the diseased lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This results in minimal ventilation of the normal lung, resulting in inadequate gas exchange [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.2.1.4. TRANSPLANT LUNG CONSIDERATIONS

Conversely, a relatively noncompliant transplanted lung cannot compete with the better compliance of the native lung, and as a result, the healthy transplanted lung can be severely underventilated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.2.1.5. BULLOUS DISEASE AND TRAUMA

Yet another scenario involves a lung with bullous or cystic disease, or a lung with tracheobronchial disruption [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Tension pneumothorax or tension mediastinum could result during these scenarios from the increased airway pressures that are often observed with OLV in the lateral decubitus position [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.2.1.6. BRONCHOPULMONARY LAVAGE

Patients with alveolar proteinosis may require unilateral bronchopulmonary lavage, which involves multiple instillations of large fluid volumes into the target lung with subsequent drainage of the effluent fluid [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Lung isolation and protection are mandatory to avoid lung cross-contamination and drowning caused by the large volume of fluid required to perform the lavage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.2.2. LUNG SEPARATION APPLICATIONS

**Lung separation facilitates surgical exposure by providing a still operating field, avoiding lung trauma, and improving gas exchange.**

Operations that benefit from optimized surgical exposure afforded by lung separation and OLV include [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Repair of thoracic aneurysms
* Pneumonectomy
* Pulmonary lobectomies (especially upper lobe)
* Video-assisted thoracoscopic surgery (VATS)
* Esophageal surgery
* Anterior spinal surgery

#### 5.2.2.1. ADDITIONAL BENEFITS

* **Lung protection**: Further improves recovery by minimizing instrumentation and trauma to the operated, nonventilated, nondependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Improved oxygenation**: In cases of unilateral lung trauma, oxygenation and recovery may be optimized with OLV by improving V̇/Q̇ matching [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 5.2.3. INDICATIONS SUMMARY

**Table 38-1 Indications for One-lung Ventilation**

**Absolute Indications**:
1. **Isolation of each lung** to prevent contamination of healthy lung
   * Infection (abscess, infected cyst) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Massive hemorrhage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
2. **Control of distribution of ventilation** to only one lung
   * Bronchopleural fistula [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Bronchopleural cutaneous fistula [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Unilateral cyst or bullae [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Major bronchial disruption or trauma [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
3. **Unilateral lung lavage** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
4. **Video-assisted thoracoscopic surgery** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Relative Indications**:
1. **Surgical exposure—high priority**
   * Thoracic aortic aneurysm [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Pneumonectomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Lung volume reduction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Minimally invasive cardiac surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Upper lobectomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
2. **Surgical exposure—low priority**
   * Esophageal surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Middle and lower lobectomies [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Mediastinal mass resection, thymectomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Bilateral sympathectomies [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

## 5.3. DOUBLE-LUMEN TUBES AND BRONCHIAL BLOCKERS
### 5.3.1. DOUBLE-LUMEN ENDOBRONCHIAL TUBES

**Double-lumen endobronchial tubes are currently the most widely used means of achieving lung separation and OLV.**

#### 5.3.1.1. BASIC DESIGN PRINCIPLES

There are several different types of DLT, but all are essentially similar in design in that two endotracheal tubes are "bonded" together [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. One lumen is long enough to reach a main stem bronchus, and the second lumen ends with an opening in the distal trachea [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Lung separation is achieved by inflation of two cuffs: a proximal tracheal cuff and a distal bronchial cuff located in the main stem bronchus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.1.2. RIGHT-SIDED TUBE DESIGN

The endobronchial cuff of a right-sided tube is slotted or otherwise designed to allow ventilation of the right upper lobe because the right main stem bronchus is too short to accommodate both the right lumen tip and a right bronchial cuff [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.3.2. HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT

#### 5.3.2.1. CARLENS TUBE

The Carlens tube (which had a carinal hook) was the first clinically available DLT and was used by pulmonologists for split function spirometry testing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.2.2. ROBERTSHAW TUBE DESIGN

Subsequently, the Robertshaw-design DLT (which lacked a carinal hook) was developed to facilitate thoracic surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This DLT is available in leftand right-sided forms [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The absence of a carinal hook facilitates insertion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Design advantages**:
* **D-shaped, large-diameter lumens**: Allow easy passage of suction catheter [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Low resistance to gas flow** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Fixed curvature**: To facilitate proper positioning and reduce possibility of kinking [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.2.3. MODERN DISPOSABLE TUBES

* **Original**: Red rubber Robertshaw tubes were available in three sizes: small, medium, and large [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Current standard**: Clear, polyvinyl chloride (PVC) disposable Robertshaw-design DLTs have replaced red rubber tubes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Available sizes**: Both right-sided and left-sided versions for 35 French (Fr), 37 Fr, 39 Fr, and 41 Fr sizes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Special sizes**: 32-Fr left-sided DLT for small adults, and 28 Fr for pediatric cases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Advantages of disposable tubes**:
* Relative ease of insertion and proper positioning [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Easy recognition of blue color of endobronchial cuff when fiberoptic bronchoscopy is used [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Confirmation of position on chest radiograph using radiopaque lines in tube wall [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Continuous observation of tidal gas exchange and respiratory moisture through clear plastic [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.2.4. RIGHT-SIDED ENDOBRONCHIAL TUBE FEATURES

The right-sided endobronchial tube is designed to minimize occlusion of the opening of the right upper lobe bronchus, featuring a right endobronchial cuff that is doughnut shaped to allow the right upper lobe ventilation slot to ride over the opening of the right upper lobe bronchus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The tube is also suitable for use in long-term ventilation in the intensive care unit (ICU) because it has a high-volume, low-pressure cuff [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

💡 **Clinical recommendation**: These disposable PVC tubes are generally considered the tubes of choice for achieving lung separation and OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.3.3. NOVEL DOUBLE-LUMEN TUBES

#### 5.3.3.1. SILBRONCHO TUBE

The Silbroncho tube (Fuji Systems Corporation, Tokyo, Japan) is D shaped with a flexible wire-reinforced bronchial tip that allows the tube to be inserted at an angle of >50 degrees without kinking the bronchial lumen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It is useful in patients who require lung isolation after left upper lobectomy. In such patients, expansion of the left lower lobe causes the left main bronchus (LMB) to rotate upward and make a 90-degree angle from the trachea, which often prevents a conventional DLT from being placed in the LMB without kinking [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The tube is available only in a left-sided version [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.3.2. PAPWORTH BIVENT TUBE

The Papworth BiVent tube (P3 Medical, Bristol, UK) is a divided single-lumen tube with a forked tip that rests on the carina [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. An integrated bronchial blocker can then be advanced into the operative lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This tube eliminates the need for endobronchial intubation, and lung isolation can be achieved without the need for bronchoscope-guided blocker placement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.3.3. VIVASIGHT-DL

The VivaSight-DL (Ambu Inc., Columbia, MO, USA / ET View Ltd., Misgav, Israel) has recently been introduced to clinical practice [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It is a disposable DLT that incorporates a high-resolution camera embedded on the DLT between the tracheal cuff and the bronchial cuff [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This device allows continuous real-time viewing of the DLT position, which can be important in cases where the patient's head is away from the anesthesiologist, such as during robotic thoracic surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**VivaSight advantages**:
* **VivaSight-SL**: Also available as single-lumen tube, through which an independent blocker may be inserted without the need of a fiberoptic bronchoscope [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Reduced intubation time**: Duration of intubation with visual confirmation of tube position was significantly reduced using the VivaSight-DL compared with conventional DLT (51 vs. 264 seconds) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Reduced bronchoscopy need**: None of patients allocated to VivaSight-DL required fiberoptic bronchoscopy during intubation or surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Cost-effectiveness**: Studies found it can be cost-effective when compared to routine Robertshaw/fiberoptic bronchoscope approaches [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Limitations**:
* **Fixed camera position**: Position of camera is set at distal part of tracheal lumen and cannot be used to inspect either left or right main stem bronchus if deemed necessary [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Not complete replacement**: Will not completely eliminate use of fiberoptic bronchoscope in lung isolation procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.3.4. ECOM DLT

The ECOM DLT (ECOM Medical, CONMED Corporation, Utica, NY) has electrodes on the bronchial cuff that enable real-time monitoring of impedance changes from the ascending aorta and provides an estimate of cardiac output [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This tube can potentially be useful during lung transplantation or off-pump cardiac surgery where lung collapse is required, or for patients with cardiac comorbidities who undergo lung resection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.3.4. DLT SIZE SELECTION

#### 5.3.4.1. SIZE SELECTION CHALLENGES

In order to provide lung separation, an appropriately sized DLT must be selected [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Too large a tube may result in herniation of the bronchial cuff and/or cause tracheal or bronchial damage, while an undersized tube may dislocate intraoperatively with failure of lung separation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.4.2. OPTIMAL SIZE CRITERIA

The optimal size of DLT should allow the bronchial lumen to pass into the desired mainstem bronchus with minimal resistance and permit a bronchial seal when the bronchial cuff is inflated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.4.3. CLINICAL PRACTICE PATTERNS

* **Left-sided preference**: A left-sided DLT is preferred for both left and right-sided procedures. A left-sided DLT was selected for 1,166 of the 1,170 patients in one report, and was used successfully in over 98% of those patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Size selection variability**: No consensus exists as to how to best determine DLT size and most anesthesiologists choose the DLT size based on their personal experience [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.4.4. HEIGHT/GENDER DECISION CRITERIA

Use of the following height/gender decision criteria resulted in >90% incidence of successful positioning and similar rate of effective lung separation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Women <1.6 m (63 in) → 35 Fr
* Women >1.6 m → 37 Fr  
* Men <1.7 m (67 in) → 39 Fr
* Men >1.7 m → 41 Fr

💡 **Clinical pearl**: Typically, most women will need a 37-Fr DLT and most men can be adequately managed with a 39-Fr DLT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.4.5. SIZE SELECTION PHILOSOPHY

The authors recommended selecting the largest DLT that will safely fit the bronchus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This will provide [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Less resistance to ventilation
* Requires less air and pressure in bronchial cuff to form seal
* Less likely to dislocate or migrate distally

However, the common practice of fiberoptic bronchoscopy has decreased the risk of undetected distal placement or migration of the bronchial tip [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. One study showed that routine use of 35-Fr DLT in adults regardless of height was not associated with increase in hypoxemia or any other adverse clinical outcomes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.3.5. CHEST IMAGING AS GUIDE FOR DLT SELECTION

Although patient height is often used as basis for selecting DLT, the correlation between airway size and height is poor [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Tracheal and bronchial dimensions can be directly measured from the chest radiograph or chest CT scan [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.5.1. IMAGING-BASED MEASUREMENTS

* **LMB size relevance**: Size of LMB is relevant factor when sizing DLT but, unlike trachea, it is not clearly visible on chest x-ray in >50% of cases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Tracheal correlation**: Since tracheal width is closely correlated with LMB width, LMB width can be estimated as 0.68 × tracheal width [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Depth estimation**: Average depth of insertion from lips of left DLT placement in 170-cm tall man or woman has been estimated at 28 to 29 cm with approximately ± 1.0 cm for each 10-cm change in height [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.5.2. ULTRASOUND APPLICATIONS

Two-dimensional ultrasound measurements of tracheal size have also been used to estimate correct DLT size [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. When compared to measurements of LMB by CT scan, ultrasound estimates of tracheal size correlated with CT scan measurements and placement of DLTs chosen based on ultrasound measurement were uniformly successful [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Position confirmation**: Chest ultrasound has also been used to confirm correct DLT position and in comparative trials has demonstrated greater sensitivity to correct placement than auscultation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The use of ultrasound to verify DLT placement may represent alternative option if fiberoptic bronchoscopy is not available [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.3.6. DLT PLACEMENT TECHNIQUE

#### 5.3.6.1. PREPARATION PHASE

Before insertion, the DLT should be prepared and checked [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Tracheal cuff**: High volume, low pressure cuff can accommodate up to 20 mL of air [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Bronchial cuff**: Can be checked using 3-mL syringe [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Lubrication**: Tube should be coated liberally with water-soluble lubricant [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Stylet preparation**: Should be withdrawn, lubricated, and gently placed back into bronchial lumen without disturbing tube's preformed curvature [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.6.2. INTUBATION PROCESS

* **Laryngoscope choice**: Macintosh laryngoscope blade is preferred because it provides largest area through which to pass tube [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Initial insertion**: Insertion performed with distal concave curvature facing anteriorly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Rotation**: After tip is past vocal cords, stylet is removed and tube is rotated through 90 degrees (left-sided tube rotated 90 degrees to left, right-sided tube to right) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Final advancement**: Advancement ceases when moderate resistance to further passage is encountered, indicating tube tip has been firmly seated in main stem bronchus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

⚠️ **Critical safety points**:
* Remove stylet before rotating and advancing tube to avoid tracheal or bronchial laceration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Rotation and advancement should be performed gently and under continuous direct laryngoscopy to prevent hypopharyngeal structures from interfering with proper positioning [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.6.3. POSITION VERIFICATION SEQUENCE

Once tube is believed to be in proper position, a sequence of steps should be performed to check its location [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:

1. **Initial ventilation check**: Inflate tracheal cuff and establish equal ventilation of both lungs. If breath sounds are not equal, tube is probably too far down, and tracheal lumen opening is in main stem bronchus or lying at carina. Withdrawal by 2 to 3 cm usually restores equal breath sounds [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

2. **Bronchial cuff inflation**: Clamp right side (for left-sided tube) and remove right cap from connector. Slowly inflate bronchial cuff to prevent air leak from bronchial lumen around bronchial cuff into tracheal lumen. Inflation of bronchial cuff rarely requires greater than 2 mL of air [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

3. **Two-lung ventilation verification**: Remove clamp and check that both lungs are ventilated with both cuffs inflated. Ensures bronchial cuff is not obstructing contralateral hemithorax [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

4. **Selective ventilation test**: Clamp each side selectively and watch for absence of movement and breath sounds on ipsilateral (clamped) side. Ventilated side should have clear breath sounds, chest movement that feels compliant, respiratory gas moisture with each tidal ventilation, and no gas leak [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Pressure guideline**: If peak airway pressure during two-lung ventilation is 20 cmH₂O, it should not exceed 40 cmH₂O for the same VT during OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.6.4. ALTERNATIVE VERIFICATION METHODS

Other methods that have been used for ensuring correct placement include [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Fluoroscopy
* Chest radiography
* Selective capnography
* Use of underwater seal

### 5.3.7. FIBEROPTIC BRONCHOSCOPY FOR POSITION CONFIRMATION

**The introduction of the pediatric flexible fiberoptic bronchoscope has been the most important advance in checking for proper positioning of a DLT.**

#### 5.3.7.1. CLINICAL NECESSITY

Smith et al. showed that when the disposable DLT was believed to be in correct position by auscultation and physical examination, subsequent fiberoptic bronchoscopy showed that 48% of tubes were, in fact, malpositioned [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Such malpositions, however, are usually of no clinical significance [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.7.2. LEFT-SIDED DLT VERIFICATION

When using left-sided DLT, the bronchoscope is usually first introduced through the tracheal lumen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Carina visualization**: Carina is visualized, but no bronchial cuff herniation should be seen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Cuff position**: Upper surface of blue endobronchial cuff should be just below tracheal carina [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Bronchial lumen check**: Bronchoscope should then be passed through bronchial lumen, and left upper lobe bronchial orifice should be identified [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.7.3. RIGHT-SIDED DLT VERIFICATION

When right-sided DLT is used, carina should be visualized through tracheal lumen but, more importantly, orifice of right upper lobe bronchus should be identified when bronchoscope is passed through right upper lobe ventilating slot of DLT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.7.4. BRONCHOSCOPE SIZE SELECTION

Pediatric fiberoptic bronchoscopes are available in several sizes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **5.6 mm external diameter**
* **4.9 mm external diameter**
* **3.6 mm external diameter**: Easily passed through all sizes of DLT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **4.9 mm diameter**: Can be passed through DLTs of 37 Fr and larger [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Optimization principle**: It is recommended that the largest bronchoscope size that can pass through the lumen of the DLT be used because this provides better visualization and facilitates identification of the bronchial anatomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.3.8. PROBLEMS OF DLT MALPOSITION

**The use of a DLT is associated with a number of potential problems, the most important of which is malposition.**

#### 5.3.8.1. SIX MAIN MALPOSITION POSSIBILITIES

1. **Opposite side intubation**: DLT may be accidentally directed to side opposite desired main stem bronchus. In this case, lung opposite side of connector clamp will collapse [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Inadequate separation, increased airway pressures, and instability of DLT usually occur. Because of morphology of DLT curvatures, tracheal or bronchial lacerations may result [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

⚠️ **Critical recognition**: If left-sided DLT is inserted into right main stem bronchus, it obstructs ventilation to right upper lobe. Essential to recognize and correct such malposition as soon as possible [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

1. **Too far advancement**: DLT may be passed too far down into either right or left main stem bronchus. Breath sounds are very diminished or not audible over contralateral side. Corrected when tube is withdrawn and opening of tracheal lumen is above carina [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

2. **Insufficient insertion**: DLT may not be inserted far enough, leaving bronchial lumen opening above carina. In this position, good breath sounds are heard bilaterally when ventilating through bronchial lumen. No breath sounds audible when ventilating through tracheal lumen because inflated bronchial cuff obstructs gas flow arising from tracheal lumen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

3. **Right upper lobe obstruction**: Right-sided DLT may occlude right upper lobe orifice. Mean distance from carina to right upper lobe orifice is 2.3 ± 0.7 cm in men and 2.1 ± 0.7 cm in women [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. With right-sided DLTs, ventilatory slot in side of bronchial catheter must overlie right upper lobe orifice to permit ventilation of this lobe [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. However, margin of safety is extremely small, varying from 1 to 8 mm [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

4. **Left upper lobe obstruction**: Left upper lobe orifice may be obstructed by left-sided DLT. Mean distance between left upper lobe orifice and carina is 5.4 ± 0.7 cm in men and 5 ± 0.7 cm in women [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Average distance between openings of right and left lumens on left-sided disposable tubes is 6.9 cm [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Therefore, obstruction of left upper lobe bronchus is possible while tracheal lumen is still above carina [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. There is also 20% variation in location of blue endobronchial cuff on disposable tubes because this cuff is attached to tube at end of manufacturing process [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

5. **Bronchial cuff herniation**: May occur and obstruct bronchial lumen if excessive volumes are used to inflate cuff. Bronchial cuff has also been known to herniate over tracheal carina, and in case of left-sided DLT, to obstruct ventilation to right main stem bronchus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.3.9. COMPLICATIONS AND SAFETY CONSIDERATIONS

#### 5.3.9.1. TRACHEAL LACERATION

Tracheal laceration or rupture is rare complication with DLTs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Overinflation of bronchial cuff, inappropriate positioning, and trauma owing to intraoperative dislocation resulting in bronchial rupture have been described in association with the Robertshaw tube and disposable DLT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Prevention strategies**:
* **Pressure assessment**: Pressure in bronchial cuff should be assessed and decreased if cuff is found to be overinflated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Cuff deflation**: If absolute separation of lungs is not needed, bronchial cuff should be deflated and then reinflated slowly to avoid excessive pressure on bronchial walls [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Repositioning precautions**: Bronchial cuff should be deflated during any repositioning of patient unless lung separation is absolutely required during this time [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.9.2. POSTOPERATIVE COMPLICATIONS

In prospective trial, 60 patients were randomly assigned to two groups. OLV was achieved with either endobronchial blocker (blocker group) or DLT (double-lumen group) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Postoperative hoarseness and sore throat were assessed at 24, 48, and 72 hours following surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Findings**:
* **Postoperative hoarseness**: Occurred significantly more frequently in double-lumen group compared with blocker group (44% vs. 17%, respectively) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Vocal cord lesions**: Similar findings were observed for vocal cord lesions (44% vs. 17%) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Bronchial injuries**: Incidence was comparable between groups [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.9.3. META-ANALYSIS RESULTS

Clayton-Smith et al. performed review and meta-analysis of 39 randomized controlled trials comparing bronchial blockers with DLTs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. They concluded that while bronchial blockers are associated with lower incidence of airway injury, DLTs can be placed more rapidly and reliably [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.3.10. BRONCHIAL BLOCKERS

**Lung separation can be achieved with a reusable bronchial blocker.**

#### 5.3.10.1. HISTORICAL BACKGROUND

Magill described an endobronchial blocker that is placed using a bronchoscope and directed to the nonventilated lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Inflation of the cuff at the distal end of the blocker serves to block ventilation to that lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The lumen of the blocker permits suctioning of the airway distal to the catheter tip [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Depending on the clinical circumstance, oxygen can be insufflated through the catheter lumen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.10.2. TRADITIONAL LIMITATIONS

Because the blocker balloon requires high distending pressure, it may easily slip out of bronchus into trachea, obstructing ventilation and losing seal between two lungs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This displacement can be secondary to changes in position or surgical manipulation `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The loss of lung separation can be life-threatening if it was performed to prevent spillage of pus, blood, or fluid from bronchopulmonary lavage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. For this reason, bronchial blockers are rarely used for these types of cases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.10.3. CLINICAL APPLICATIONS

**Table 38-2 Indications for the Use of Endobronchial Blockers**

**The Difficult Airway**:
* Avoids need for tube exchange (DLT to SLT) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Following laryngeal surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Patient with tracheostomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Distorted bronchial anatomy due to compression by aneurysm or tumor [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Patient who requires nasotracheal intubation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Management**:
* Makes possible segmental blockade in patient who cannot tolerate OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Morbid obesity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Small size and pediatric patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Patients from ICU who arrive to OR tracheally intubated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Surgical Procedures not Involving the Lung**:
* Esophageal surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Spine surgery that requires transthoracic approach [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Minimally invasive cardiac surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 5.3.11. MODERN BRONCHIAL BLOCKER DEVICES

#### 5.3.11.1. UNIVENT TUBE

The Univent (Fuji Systems Corp., Tokyo, Japan) is single-lumen tracheal tube with movable endobronchial blocker [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In the Univent tube, the bronchial blocker is housed in small channel bored into wall of tube [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The blocker contains high-volume, low-pressure balloon, and is angled to permit external direction into desired bronchus under direct fiberoptic bronchoscopic vision [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Advantages**:
* **No tube change required**: Is SLT, and there is no need to change tube at end of procedure if postoperative ventilatory support is required [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Difficult airway applications**: Particularly important in cases of difficult intubation, altered anatomy of airway, or prolonged surgery with airway edema [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Therapeutic applications**: Possible to suction through blocker lumen or apply continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) to improve oxygenation in cases of hypoxemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Disadvantages**:
* **Positioning difficulty**: Correct positioning of blocker may be difficult to achieve or maintain [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Large external diameter**: Can make it difficult to pass tube between vocal cords [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Postoperative considerations**: Many anesthesiologists prefer to avoid postoperative ventilation with such large-diameter tube [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Displacement**: Blocker can dislocate during surgical manipulation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Seal challenges**: Satisfactory bronchial seal and lung separation are sometimes difficult to achieve [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Torque Control Blocker**: Second-generation Torque Control Blocker Univent was introduced to provide better control and facilitate direction of blocker into target main stem bronchus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.11.2. ARNDT BLOCKER

The Arndt snare-guided bronchial blocker (Cook Critical Care) was introduced to overcome potential problems with earlier devices [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It is wire-guided catheter with loop snare [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. A fiberscope is passed through loop of bronchial blocker and then guided into desired bronchus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The blocker is then slid distally over fiberscope and into selected bronchus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Features**:
* **Balloon options**: Available in spherical or elliptic shape [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Multiport adapter**: Allows uninterrupted ventilation during positioning of blocker [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Wire removal**: Wire may be removed, freeing 1.6-mm lumen for use as suction port or for oxygen insufflation to nondependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Wire reintroduction**: External reinforcement of wire now allows for its reintroduction through lumen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Size requirement**: External diameter necessitates large size SLT (at least 8 mm) to accommodate bronchial blocker [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Pediatric sizes**: Available in 7 Fr and 5 Fr pediatric size [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Disadvantage**: Advanced blindly over FB into desired main bronchus; occasionally, tip of blocker may get caught at carina or at Murphy eye of SLT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.11.3. COHEN BLOCKER

The Cohen Flexitip endobronchial blocker (Cook Critical Care) is designed for use as independent bronchial blocker [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It is inserted through SLT with aid of small-diameter (4 mm) fiberoptic bronchoscope [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Features**:
* **Rotating wheel**: Deflects soft tip by more than 90 degrees and easily directs it into desired bronchus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Pear-shaped cuff**: High-volume, low-pressure balloon inflated via 0.4-mm lumen inside wall of blocker [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Inflation volume**: Generally, inflation with between 6 mL and 8 mL of air provides adequate seal of bronchus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Visual guidance**: Best to inflate cuff under direct visualization via fiberoptic bronchoscope [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Blue cuff**: Distinctive blue color easily recognizable by fiberoptic bronchoscopy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Size**: Blocker is 9 Fr, with central main lumen (1.6 mm) allowing limited suctioning of secretions and insufflation of oxygen to collapsed lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Tube compatibility**: Can be used even with 7-mm endotracheal tube because blocker and fiberoptic bronchoscope do not have to pass through tracheal tube at same time [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.11.4. UNIBLOCKER

Fuji Systems introduced 9-Fr balloon-tipped, angled blocker with multiple port adapter that is essentially same design as Univent tube blocker, but can be used as independent blocker passed via special connector through standard tracheal tube [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.11.5. EZ-BLOCKER

The EZ Blocker (Teleflex, Morrisville, NC) is most recent addition to endobronchial blocker design [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It is 7-Fr, 4-lumen, 75-cm, disposable endobronchial blocker to facilitate selective lung ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Design features**:
* **Y-shaped bifurcation**: Has symmetric Y-shaped bifurcation resembling bifurcation of trachea [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Dual balloons**: Both branches have inflatable cuff and central lumen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Placement**: During insertion via standard tracheal tube, each of two distal ends is placed into main stem bronchus [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Bilateral procedures**: Should offer advantage during bilateral procedures because each lung can be deflated without need for repositioning blocker [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 5.3.12. COMPARATIVE EFFECTIVENESS STUDIES

#### 5.3.12.1. THREE-DEVICE COMPARISON

The effectiveness of lung isolation among three devices—left-sided DLT, Torque Control Blocker Univent, and wire-guided Arndt—has been compared in prospective randomized trial [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:

**Positioning time**:
* Arndt blocker: 3 minutes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Left-sided DLT: 2 minutes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Univent: 2 minutes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Lung collapse time** (excluding tube placement):
* Arndt group: 26 minutes (majority required suction to achieve lung collapse) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* DLT group: 17 minutes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Univent group: 19 minutes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Surgical exposure**: Once lung isolation was achieved, overall surgical exposure was rated excellent for all three groups [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

💡 **Clinical perspective**: One minute longer to position bronchial blocker or 6 minutes longer to collapse lung is insignificant when considering overall duration of thoracic procedure. Hence, patient safety and risk/benefit should be controlling factors when choosing methods for lung isolation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.12.2. FOUR-DEVICE STUDY

A further study evaluated the use of Cohen blocker, Arndt blocker, Uniblocker, and DLT (four groups of 26 patients in each group) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. No difference was found between groups in time taken to insert these lung isolation devices or in quality of lung collapse [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The grading was done by operating surgeons who were blinded as to which device was used [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The number of cuff dislocations was higher among the three bronchial blocker (BB) groups compared to DLT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.3.12.3. META-ANALYSIS CONCLUSIONS

A recent comprehensive meta-analysis and randomized controlled trials analysis was performed for comparison of efficacy and adverse effects of DLTs and BBs in thoracic surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The authors concluded that DLTs were quicker to place and less likely to be incorrectly positioned than BBs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. BBs were associated with lower incidence of postoperative sore throat, hoarseness, and fewer airway injuries than DLTs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Most importantly, no significant difference between BBs and DLT was shown for either time taken for adequate lung collapse or quality of lung collapse [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.3.13. DLT VS. BRONCHIAL BLOCKER DECISION-MAKING

**The decision whether to use a DLT or a BB is most commonly based on surgeon and anesthesiologist preference.**

#### 5.3.13.1. DLT INDICATIONS

* **Bilateral lung ventilation**: When both lungs need to be ventilated separately, DLT provides most control of ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Life-threatening contamination**: DLT use is particularly important when protection of dependent lung from life-threatening contamination from diseased lung is required. Isolation provided by low-pressure, high-volume BB cuff is less reliable under such circumstances [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Tumor in main bronchus**: If tumor is occupying main bronchus or an open sleeve resection is necessary, contralateral DLT should be used [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Bilateral procedures**: Such as double lung transplant, or bilateral sympathectomy are best managed using DLT because of need to ventilate both lungs independently [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.13.2. BB ADVANTAGES

* **Dislocation concerns**: Studies of BB use suggest they are more likely to dislodge intraoperatively than DLT. Proper locking of BB inside ET tube may decrease this likelihood, as may greater attention to securing ET tube [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Pressure limitations**: Since BBs have low-pressure, high-volume cuffs, peak airway pressure should be kept below 30 cmH₂O to prevent gas bypassing cuff [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.3.13.3. VERIFICATION METHODS

* **Direct visualization**: Adequate blockade can be confirmed by direct fiberoptic visualization of inflated cuff [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Volume monitoring**: Alternatively, lumen of BB can be intermittently connected to suction to assure no discrepancy exists between inspiratory and expiratory volumes due to suctioning of leaked gas [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Bottom Line:** DLTs remain the gold standard for lung separation with over 50 years of clinical use, but bronchial blockers offer valuable alternatives in specific clinical situations including difficult airways, tracheostomized patients, and cases where tube changes should be avoided.

## 5.4. VENTILATION MANAGEMENT STRATEGIES
**This section discusses the management of OLV in a paralyzed patient in the lateral decubitus position with an open chest.**

### 5.4.1. CONFIRMATION OF CORRECT POSITION

**Following intubation with a DLT or placement of a bronchial blocker, the correct position should be checked by multiple methods.**

#### 5.4.1.1. ASSESSMENT METHODS

* **Clinical assessment**: Visualization of chest movement, auscultation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Pressure/volume flow profile** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Standard practice**: In modern anesthesia practice, standard 4.0-mm fiberscope should be passed down tracheal lumen to check for correct depth, or alongside bronchial blocker that was passed through single-lumen tracheal tube [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.4.1.2. POSITION VERIFICATION

It is common practice to visualize tip of blue bronchial cuff at level of carina to ensure that left upper lobe orifice is not obstructed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Once patient is turned into lateral position, position of DLT should be rechecked to exclude dislocation of tube during positioning [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.4.2. INSPIRED OXYGEN FRACTION MANAGEMENT

**An FIO₂ of 1 is generally recommended during OLV.**

#### 5.4.2.1. RATIONALE FOR HIGH FIO₂

A high oxygen concentration serves to protect against hypoxemia during procedure and provides higher margin of safety `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.2.2. POTENTIAL DISADVANTAGES

A high FIO₂ may, however, cause absorption atelectasis and potentially further increase amount of shunt because of collapsed alveoli [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.2.3. ALTERNATIVE APPROACH

The use of FIO₂ less than 1 during OLV has potential benefits of decreasing risk of absorption atelectasis and, if N₂O is used, may allow potent inhaled anesthetics to be used in lower concentrations `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Some clinicians use O₂ 80%/N₂O 20% mixture as long that SpO₂ is maintained in safe range `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.2.4. LUNG COLLAPSE CONSIDERATIONS

The rate of lung collapse during OLV was addressed by Ko et al. [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. They compared effects of three different gas mixtures (air/oxygen, FIO₂ 0.4; N₂O/O₂, FIO₂ 0.4; and oxygen, FIO₂ 1) during OLV on lung collapse and oxygenation during subsequent OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. They found that deflation of nonventilated lung during thoracic surgery was delayed if air was used as part of anesthetic gas mixture during initial period of OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

💡 **Clinical recommendation**: For thoracic procedures in which delayed collapse of nonventilated lung will impede surgical exposure, optimal anesthetic technique should include thorough denitrogenation of lung by using FIO₂ of 1 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.4.3. CONTINUOUS POSITIVE AIRWAY PRESSURE TO THE NONDEPENDENT LUNG

**The single most effective maneuver to increase PaO₂ during OLV is the application of CPAP to the nondependent lung.**

#### 5.4.3.1. MECHANISM OF BENEFIT

The beneficial effects of CPAP 10 cmH₂O (CPAP10) are not attributable solely to effect of positive pressure in diverting blood flow away from collapsed lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In animal studies, hyperinflation of nitrogen into nondependent lung under 10 cmH₂O failed to improve PaO₂ [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

A lower level of CPAP (5 to 10 cmH₂O) maintains patency of nondependent lung alveoli, allowing some oxygen uptake to occur in distended alveoli `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. CPAP should be applied after delivering inspiratory VT to nondependent lung to keep it slightly expanded `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. CPAP, applied by insufflation of oxygen under positive pressure, keeps this lung "quiet" and prevents it from collapsing completely `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.3.2. IMPLEMENTATION REQUIREMENTS

* **Positive pressure essential**: Insufflation of oxygen without maintaining positive pressure failed to improve PaO₂ [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Intermittent reinflation**: Of collapsed (nondependent) lung with oxygen also resulted in significant improvement in PaO₂ [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.4.3.3. CONTEMPORARY PRACTICE LIMITATIONS

Unfortunately, in contemporary practice, most thoracic procedures are initiated thoracoscopically, such that application of CPAP to nondependent lung is generally not acceptable to most surgeons [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. During VATS, lung should be well collapsed to allow surgeon optimal view of surgical field and to palpate lesion in lung parenchyma `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In addition, it is difficult to place stapler on lung that is not completely collapsed, resulting in increased incidence of postoperative air leak [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.4.4. TIDAL VOLUME STRATEGY

**Over the past two decades, the incidence of hypoxemia during OLV has decreased from 25% to 4% to 5%.**

#### 5.4.4.1. REASONS FOR IMPROVEMENT

Most likely due to [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Routine use of fiberoptic bronchoscopy to confirm correct position of DLT/BB
* Use of protective lung ventilation strategies which include use of low VT, PEEP, and recruitment maneuvers (RMs)

#### 5.4.4.2. ACUTE LUNG INJURY RISK

Of greater concern is risk of ALI from stretching of lung parenchyma from delivery of large VTs `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. On the other hand, use of small VT of 6 mL/kg without PEEP can lead to formation of atelectasis in dependent lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.4.5. ARDS NETWORK EVIDENCE

The multicenter, prospective ARDS Network trial demonstrated that smaller VT of 6 mL/kg, rather than traditional 12 mL/kg, significantly decreased in-hospital mortality in ICU patients `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Several subsequent clinical studies have confirmed benefit of small VT in ARDS patients `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.5.1. "BABY LUNG" CONCEPT

These patients have decreased lung capacity due to presence of atelectatic areas that cannot be recruited despite application of high levels of PEEP `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. They should be treated as having "baby lung" and ventilated with small VT to avoid overdistension of rest of ventilated lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.5.2. APPLICABILITY TO OLV
