It is unclear, however, if this protective low VT strategy in ARDS patients is applicable to perioperative environment, specifically during OLV where, for most part, patients do not have unrecruited atelectasis `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In fact, in retrospective analysis of over 30,000 patients (not thoracic or cardiac), 30-day mortality rate was lower when VT of 8 to 9 mL/kg was used intraoperatively `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.5.3. THORACIC SURGERY EVIDENCE

Blank et al. analyzed data from Society of Thoracic Surgery that reported postthoracic procedure complications in 1,019 patients `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. They found that in large proportion of patients, use of large VT of 8 to 9 mL/kg during OLV was inversely proportional to incidence of respiratory complications `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.5.4. PATIENT POPULATION DIFFERENCES

The discrepancy between ICU data and thoracic surgical population can be explained by fact that, to date, there is no evidence to confirm that data derived from ARDS ICU patients are applicable to patients undergoing thoracic procedure `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Most thoracic surgery patients have lung lesion accidentally discovered during routine screening `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In addition, thoracic patients are exposed to PPV only for limited period of OR time compared to ICU patients who are prone to risk of parenchymal damage from prolonged ventilatory support `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.5.5. LATERAL POSITION CONSIDERATIONS

The application of protective lung ventilation may be more complex in lateral position `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Unlike in supine position where relative blood flow to each lung is basically unchanged, in lateral position, ventilated lung is subjected to increased blood flow distribution due to effect of gravity `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. During OLV, HPV in nondependent lung further increases proportion of blood that flows to dependent lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Consequently, dependent lung may receive more than 70% of total pulmonary blood flow, resulting in hyperemia that is injurious to pulmonary vasculature and may create additional shear stress, and possible damage to endothelial glycocalyx `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.4.6. POSITIVE END-EXPIRATORY PRESSURE MANAGEMENT

**The application of PEEP during thoracic surgery has an intraoperative effect on arterial oxygenation and on postoperative pulmonary complications.**

#### 5.4.6.1. MECHANISM OF BENEFIT

The beneficial effect of selective PEEP at 10 cmH₂O (PEEP10) to dependent lung is caused by increased lung volume at end-expiration (FRC), which improves ventilation/perfusion (V/Q) relationship in dependent lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. During OLV, PEEP also prevents alveolar closure at end expiration in dependent lung and thus prevents repetitive intratidal opening and collapse of airway `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.6.2. RESEARCH EVIDENCE CHALLENGES

Studies evaluating whether application of PEEP to dependent lung would improve arterial oxygenation were not encouraging and showed either no change, decrease, or slight increase in PaO₂ `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. High levels of PEEP can reduce regional blood flow and protect dependent lung from capillary overdistention `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.6.3. NEGATIVE EFFECTS MECHANISM

The negative effect of PEEP on PaO₂ during OLV is based on increased compression of small interalveolar vessels, which increases PVR accompanied by diversion of blood flow to nondependent lung, increasing transpulmonary shunt `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.6.4. IMPORTANT CLINICAL ROLES OF PEEP

1. **Atelectasis prevention**: Each time patient is anesthetized and mechanically ventilated, atelectatic areas will form in dependent part of lung, particularly when low VT is used for protective lung ventilation. PEEP will prevent or at least decrease extent of atelectatic areas in lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

2. **Study interpretation**: Studies that concluded that PEEP worsens PaO₂ during OLV need to be carefully analyzed. Almost 40 years ago, first report of application of PEEP during OLV found that it decreased PaO₂. However, in that study, mean PaO₂ during OLV when PEEP was applied was 150 mmHg; clearly there was no clinical need to improve oxygenation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.4.6.5. FRC-DEPENDENT EFFECTS

Whether application of PEEP will improve or worsen arterial oxygenation depends on FRC `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Low PaO₂**: Application of PEEP to patient with low PaO₂ may increase lung volume to normal FRC, which in turn results in lower PVR and improved V/Q ratio [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Higher PaO₂**: With higher PaO₂, patient already has adequate dependent lung FRC, so applied PEEP may negatively affect redistribution of blood flow away from dependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

The same concept was described by Slinger et al. who showed that patients would selectively benefit from application of PEEP during OLV when kept at FRC volume in dependent lung at which PVR was lowest `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.6.6. INDIVIDUALIZED PEEP CONCEPT

The same level of PEEP may not be universally applied to all patients. One size may not fit all, but rather PEEP should be individualized `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Each patient has individual level of PEEP that best fits their lung compliance at FRC lung volume `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Analysis across entire sample of patients, receiving same level of PEEP, may not truly represent each patient's ideal ventilatory parameters `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.6.7. DRIVING PRESSURE OPTIMIZATION

This concept was recently addressed using driving pressure (DP) as single best value for ideal ventilatory parameters `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The optimum respiratory system compliance is when PEEP is titrated to lowest DP and individualized for each patient `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.6.8. EIT-GUIDED PEEP

A recent study evaluated PEEP guided by electrical impedance tomography (EIT) during OLV in elderly patients undergoing VATS `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. EIT is noninvasive, medical imaging method that allows reconstruction of dynamic pulmonary images in real time `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The authors studied 100 patients assigned to either PEEP5 or to EIT group (PEEPEIT), and all were ventilated with TV of 6 mL/kg `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. At 1 hour into OLV, PaO₂/FIO₂ was 93 mmHg higher, lung compliance was 4.4 mL/cmH₂O higher, and DP was 4.9 cmH₂O lower in PEEPEIT group than in PEEP5 group `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.6.9. LARGE TRIAL RESULTS

The application of low PEEP (2 cmH₂O) versus high PEEP (12 cmH₂O) was evaluated in two prospective multicenter studies in noncardiothoracic patients: the PROVHILO trial and PROBESE study in obese population found no differences in postoperative pulmonary complications between these levels of PEEP `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. However, applying same level of PEEP across all patients in study group would most likely mask any beneficial effect of PEEP for individual patient by misidentifying ideal DP to achieve best compliance in ventilated lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

💡 **Key message**: Ultimately, takeaway message is that it is best to "open the lung and keep it open" which is what PEEP will accomplish [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.4.7. CPAP SYSTEMS AND IMPLEMENTATION

**CPAP can be applied to the nondependent lung using a number of simple systems.**

#### 5.4.7.1. SYSTEM COMPONENTS

All systems have essentially same features [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Oxygen source
* Tubing to connect oxygen source to nonventilated lung
* Pressure relief valve
* Pressure gauge

#### 5.4.7.2. IMPLEMENTATION METHOD

The catheter to nondependent lung is usually insufflated with 5 L/min of oxygen using modified Ayre's T-piece (pediatric) circuit, and valve on expiratory limb is adjusted to desired pressure as read on attached gauge `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Instead of pressure gauge or manometer inserted into circuit, spring-loaded adjustable PEEP valve can be used `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.7.3. TITRATION PRINCIPLES

The amount of CPAP applied should be titrated to clinical circumstances `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In most cases, even low levels of CPAP may be sufficient to increase PaO₂ to accepted safe level `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. CPAP greater than 10 cmH₂O is not beneficial because it creates overdistention that interferes with surgical exposure and may have undesirable hemodynamic consequences [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.7.4. ALTERNATIVE VENTILATION

High-frequency ventilation with oxygen to nondependent lung and conventional ventilation (CV) to dependent lung have also been used to improve PaO₂ during OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.4.8. RECRUITMENT MANEUVERS

**RMs have the purpose of opening atelectatic areas of the lung which, once opened, should continue to remain patent with the application of PEEP.**

#### 5.4.8.1. THEORETICAL RATIONALE

Many investigators believe that ventilator-induced lung injury (VILI) propagates from these atelectatic zones, rather than from continuously aerated alveoli that are repeatedly overstretched `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Eliminating these atelectatic interfaces with RM is primary objective of "open lung" approach to safe ventilation `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.8.2. IMPLEMENTATION METHODS

RM can be achieved by [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
1. **Sustained pressure**: Application of 35 to 40 cmH₂O sustained airway pressure for 30 to 40 seconds
2. **Incremental PEEP**: 5 cmH₂O incremental increases in PEEP maintained for five breaths until reaching final PEEP of 20 cmH₂O, plateauing at pressure of 40 cmH₂O

#### 5.4.8.3. CONTROVERSY AND CHALLENGES

RM is most controversial part of protective ventilation (PV), with conflicting literature regarding its clinical value in both ARDS, anesthesia with two-lung ventilation, and OLV in particular `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Performing RM during lung resection carries significant challenges `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Leak concerns**: Overpressure leaks past bronchial cuff securing operative lung may create unwanted stress on bronchial stumps and anastomoses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Cardiovascular effects**: Impeded venous return during RM can create cardiovascular depression and worsen hypoxia by reducing blood flow through ventilated lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 5.4.8.4. EXPERT CRITIQUE

Arnal et al. reported that "during recruiting effort, incremental gain achieved by sustained inflation wanes progressively as risk for adverse consequences builds" `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Although most hypotensive episodes caused by RM are rather transient, efficacy of RM with sustained high pressure has been questioned `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

Marini stated that "although sustained inflation is commonly deployed and is still actively researched as recruiting technique, there are good reasons to retire it from clinical practice. Stepwise approaches to maximum applied pressure are perhaps more effective than abrupt applications of same peak pressure, and graded rise of mean pressure is generally better tolerated" `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.4.9. DRIVING PRESSURE OPTIMIZATION

**DP is defined as the difference between plateau pressure and PEEP (Plat-PEEP), and can also be expressed as the ratio of VT to respiratory system compliance.**

#### 5.4.9.1. PHYSIOLOGICAL BASIS

It depends on VT as well as on relative balance between amount of aerated lung at end expiration and end inspiration at different levels of PEEP `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. DP is derived from interaction between ventilatory parameters set by anesthesiologist and patient's total thoracic compliance `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.9.2. ARDS EVIDENCE

In randomized controlled trials of mechanical ventilation in ARDS patients, DP is variable that was most strongly associated with mortality `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Amato et al. conducted multilevel analysis of data from 3,562 patients with ARDS which concluded that, among ventilation parameters, DP was most strongly associated with survival even with "protective" lung ventilation `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Individual changes in VT or PEEP were not independently associated with survival unless changes led to reductions in DP `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.9.3. APPLICATION TO OLV

Park et al. confirmed significance of DP during OLV `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In double-blind, randomized study of 292 patients undergoing thoracic surgery, they compared DP-guided ventilation with conventional PV `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:

**PV group (n = 147)**: Received conventional PV during OLV: VT 6 mL/kg, PEEP 5 cmH₂O, and RM [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**DP group (n = 145)**: Received same VT and recruitment, but with titrated individualized PEEP to produce lowest DP during OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Concept**: Ventilating lungs using lowest DP is equivalent to ventilating according to patient's "functional lung size," which will avoid underdistention (atelectasis) or overdistension (barotrauma) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Results**: Postoperatively, number of patients who developed pneumonia or ARDS was smaller in DP group [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.4.10. MECHANICAL POWER CONSIDERATIONS

**Mechanical Power (MP) is the energy delivered from the ventilator to the respiratory system per unit of time.**

#### 5.4.10.1. COMPONENTS AND CALCULATION

The energy delivered to respiratory system is composed of static component, due to PEEP, and dynamic cyclic component, due to DP and VT above PEEP, plus additional pressure required to generate gas flow `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. MP is expressed in Joules/minute (J/min) `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

For volume-controlled ventilation, it is calculated as [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:  
**Mechanical Power = 0.098 × RR × VT × [Ppeak − ½ × (PPlat − PEEP)]**

MP is single variable that combines volume, pressure, flow, and RR. Clinical changes in respiratory rate have potential to affect MP strongly `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.10.2. CLINICAL EVIDENCE

The extent of lung injury depends on total amount of mechanical energy delivered by ventilator per unit of time `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Serpa Neto et al. analyzed multicenter data from 8,207 critically ill patients, with primary outcome of in-hospital mortality `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. They concluded that high MP was independently associated with in-hospital mortality, ICU mortality, 30-day mortality, ventilator-free days, and ICU and hospital length of stay, regardless of VT used `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.10.3. THORACIC SURGERY APPLICATIONS

The role of MP during thoracic surgery has been investigated `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In lateral decubitus position, weight of contralateral hemithorax adds to restriction of dependent chest wall `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The MP generated to ventilate dependent lung is therefore expected to increase `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

Chiumello et al. evaluated MP, lung elastance, chest wall elastance, and esophageal pressure in supine and lateral positions, with two and OLV, with both closed and open chest, before and after surgery `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The authors found that MP delivered to dependent lung and respiratory system elastance increased during OLV in lateral position compared to supine position `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The chest wall elastance increased in lateral position with two-lung ventilation as compared to supine, while opening chest was associated with decrease in respiratory system and chest wall elastance `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Important finding**: Reducing VT did not result in decrease in MP `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The authors concluded that, "Although we cannot suggest any clinical mechanical power threshold for possible injury, our data suggest that only decrease of VT from 8 to 5 mL/kg of ideal body weight did not reduce mechanical power. Thus, dependent lung might undergo underappreciated amount of injury" `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.10.4. CLINICAL IMPLEMENTATION

The MP equation could be easily implemented and displayed by ventilator's software `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Rather than following intraoperative parameters such as VT or peak airway pressure, monitoring MP may help estimate contribution of ventilator-related causes of lung injury `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.4.11. THERAPEUTIC HYPERCARBIA

**OLV is known to cause an inflammatory response and ALI. Hypercapnia has been shown to have beneficial effects in patients with lung injuries such as ARDS.**

#### 5.4.11.1. RESEARCH EVIDENCE

Gao et al. studied effect of deliberate hypercapnia on inflammatory responses to OLV in patients undergoing lobectomy `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Fifty patients having lobectomy under intravenous anesthesia were randomly assigned to ventilation with either oxygen/air mixture (goal PaCO₂ of 35 to 45 mmHg) or oxygen/air/CO₂ mixture (goal PaCO₂ of 60 to 70 mmHg) `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. FIO₂ was maintained at greater than 0.8 during OLV `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The duration of OLV was not significantly different between two groups (means ∼ 180 minutes) `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.11.2. RESULTS

The authors found that induced hypercapnia during OLV period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Inhibited local inflammatory response
* Decreased airway pressure
* Increased lung compliance
* Improved PaO₂/FIO₂ following surgery

No severe adverse effects were reported in relation to therapeutic hypercarbia `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.4.11.3. FUTURE DIRECTIONS

The potential beneficial effect of inhalation of CO₂ during OLV merits further investigation in patients receiving potent inhaled anesthetics, which have already been shown to inhibit inflammatory response during lung surgery `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 5.5. HYPOXEMIA MANAGEMENT AND TROUBLESHOOTING
**Once the patient is in the lateral decubitus position, the placement of the DLT should be rechecked.**

### 5.5.1. CLINICAL APPROACH TO OLV MANAGEMENT

#### 5.5.1.1. INITIAL MANAGEMENT PRINCIPLES

Two-lung ventilation should be maintained for as long as possible `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. When OLV needs to be instituted, it is generally recommended that FIO₂ of 1 be used `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The lung should be ventilated using VT that results in plateau airway pressure less than 25 cmH₂O at rate adjusted to maintain PaCO₂ at 35 ± 3 mmHg `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This is usually monitored indirectly with use of capnometer or other multigas analyzer `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.5.1.2. RECOMMENDED MEASURES DURING OLV

* **Protective ventilation**: Use of PV with low VT 6 to 7 mL/kg [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **PEEP application**: To dependent lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Recruitment maneuvers**: Frequent RMs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Fluid restriction**: Of volume of fluid administered [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 5.5.2. OXYGENATION MONITORING AND RESPONSE

#### 5.5.2.1. EXPECTED CHANGES

After initiation of OLV, depending on lung pathology and intensity of HPV, PaO₂ can continue to decrease for up to 45 minutes `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Frequent monitoring of arterial blood gases and use of pulse oximeter continue throughout operative period `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.5.2.2. SURGICAL COORDINATION

It is also essential to work closely with surgeon in case reinsufflation of lung is necessary `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.5.3. SYSTEMATIC HYPOXEMIA MANAGEMENT

#### 5.5.3.1. PRIMARY ASSESSMENT

If hypoxemia occurs during OLV, position of DLT should be rechecked using fiberoptic bronchoscope `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. If dependent lung is not severely diseased, satisfactory PaO₂ on two-lung ventilation should not decrease to dangerously hypoxic levels on OLV `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.5.3.2. SPECIFIC TUBE CONSIDERATIONS

If left thoracotomy is being performed using right-sided DLT, ventilation to right upper lobe should be ensured `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.5.3.3. FIRST-LINE INTERVENTION

After tube position has been confirmed as correct, CPAP10 should be applied to nondependent lung after VT that expands lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In most cases, this is sufficient to increase PaO₂ to safe level `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.5.3.4. VATS LIMITATIONS

During thoracoscopy, application of CPAP is usually not possible because it impedes surgeon `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This is especially so during VATS procedures `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In this case, PEEP to ventilated lung may be tried `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.5.3.5. ADVANCED INTERVENTIONS

In very rare case in which PaO₂ remains low despite these maneuvers, intermittent two-lung ventilation can be reinstituted with surgeon's cooperation `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Also, if pneumonectomy is being performed, depending on stage of surgical dissection, ligation of PA eliminates shunt `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.5.4. INTRAOPERATIVE MONITORING PARAMETERS

**Table 38-4 Clinical Approach to One-lung Ventilation (OLV) Management**

1. **Use FIO₂ of 1** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
2. **Ventilate with VT of 6–8 mL/kg with PEEP 5 cmH₂O** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
3. **Respiratory rate to maintain PaCO₂ between 35 and 40 mmHg** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
4. **Check DLT/endobronchial blocker position subsequent to lateral decubitus positioning** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
5. **If peak airway pressure exceeds 40 cmH₂O during OLV, DLT/endobronchial blocker malposition should be excluded** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
6. **For hypoxemia, apply CPAP 10 cmH₂O to nondependent lung (not during VATS)** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
7. **If additional correction of hypoxemia is necessary, add PEEP 5–10 cmH₂O to ventilated lung** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
8. **Frequent recruiting maneuvers** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
9. **Avoid fluid overload** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
10. **TIVA may be preferable to inhalation anesthetics** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
11. **If necessary, intermittently inflate and deflate the operated lung** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 5.5.5. CONTINUOUS MONITORING REQUIREMENTS

During OLV, the following should be checked continuously [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Peak airway pressure**
* **Actual VT delivered** (measured by spirometer)
* **Shape of capnogram**
* **Pressure-volume loop** (if available)

#### 5.5.5.1. PRESSURE CHANGE EVALUATION

A sudden increase in peak airway pressure may be secondary to tube dislocation because of surgical manipulation, resulting in impaired ventilation `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In addition, ability to auscultate by stethoscope over dependent lung is extremely important `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.5.6. EMERGENCY PROTOCOLS

#### 5.5.6.1. INSTABILITY MANAGEMENT

If there is any doubt about stability of patient, or if patient becomes hypotensive, dusky, or tachycardic, two-lung ventilation should be resumed until problem has been resolved `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.5.6.2. CARDIOVASCULAR CONSIDERATIONS

Because of pericardial manipulation (during left thoracotomy in particular) and pulling on great vessels, cardiac dysrhythmias and hypotension are not uncommon `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Cardiotonic drugs should be prepared and kept available for use during any thoracic surgical procedure `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.5.6.3. RISK-BENEFIT ASSESSMENT

Most thoracic surgical procedures represent only relative indications for OLV, and benefits of OLV should always be weighed against risks to patient `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.5.7. LUNG PROTECTION STRATEGIES

#### 5.5.7.1. DEPENDENT LUNG PROTECTION

Attention should be directed toward protection of ventilated lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. PV should be used with low VT and lowest peak airway pressure (e.g., with I:E ratio of 1:1), with high respiratory rate or using pressure control ventilation `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.5.7.2. COPD CONSIDERATIONS

Patients with COPD are of particular concern because application of PEEP may cause dynamic hyperinflation secondary to increase in respiratory rate to maintain PaCO₂ `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.5.7.3. RECRUITMENT AND FLUID MANAGEMENT

* **Frequent recruiting maneuvers**: Should be applied to reduce amount of atelectasis in dependent lung. They should be applied with sustained peak pressure of 40 cmH₂O to be effective [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Fluid limitation**: Must be limited to avoid fluid overload that could increase pulmonary capillary permeability [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Risk correlation**: The risk of ALI and fluid overload increases in proportion to amount of lung parenchyma resected [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.5.8. ANESTHETIC CHOICE CONSIDERATIONS

**A balanced anesthetic technique using inhalational agents with low rates of propofol infusion alone or in combination with remifentanil is the technique of choice during OLV.**

It should have least inhibitory effect on HPV and decrease transpulmonary shunt through nonventilated lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Karzai et al. published an excellent review on prediction, prevention, and treatment of hypoxemia during OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 5.5.8.1. PROTECTIVE VS. CONVENTIONAL VENTILATION STUDY

Yang et al. compared PV with CV strategies in two groups of 50 patients each undergoing OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:

**CV strategy**:
* FIO₂ 1 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* VT 10 mL/kg [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Zero end-expiratory pressure (ZEEP) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Volume-controlled ventilation (VCV) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**PV strategy**:
* FIO₂ 0.5 [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* VT 6 mL/kg [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* PEEP 5 cmH₂O [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Pressure-controlled ventilation (PCV) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Results**: During OLV, although 58% of PV group needed increased FIO₂ to maintain SpO₂ greater than 95%, peak airway pressure was significantly lower than in CV group, whereas mean PaCO₂ values remained at 35 to 40 mmHg in both groups `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Importantly, in PV group, incidence of primary end point of pulmonary dysfunction was significantly lower than in CV group (incidence of PaO₂/FIO₂ <300 mmHg, lung infiltration, or atelectasis: 4% vs. 22%) `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 5.5.9. EVIDENCE-BASED CONCLUSIONS

Walsh et al. concluded that recent evidence from prospective randomized controlled trials of ventilation of ICU patients without ARDS suggests that clinically important outcomes do not differ between patients who are ventilated with low or higher VT, or with low versus higher PEEP `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. During OLV, while protective lung ventilation (via manipulation of VT, PEEP, and DP) may be prudent, there is limited evidence that it reduces pulmonary complications `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Successful OLV management requires systematic approach to positioning confirmation, oxygenation optimization, protective ventilation strategies, and prompt recognition and treatment of hypoxemia through a hierarchical algorithm of interventions from CPAP application to emergency two-lung ventilation resumption.

---

# 6. ANESTHETIC CONSIDERATIONS AND TECHNIQUES

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C2 Sedation and General Anaesthesia|Part B: General Anaesthesia]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|Part B: Thoracic Surgery]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|Part C: PC]]

> [!info]- Exam Context
> **Exam Weighting:** High (Critical for optimal thoracic surgery outcomes)

---

## 6.1. CHOICE OF ANESTHETIC AGENTS
**The choice of anesthetic technique for OLV must take into consideration the effects on oxygenation and therefore on hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction.**

### 6.1.1. HISTORICAL PERSPECTIVE

Traditionally, potent inhaled anesthetics were the agents of choice for OLV since they decrease airway reactivity by direct action on the airway musculature of the bronchial tree [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Of concern was their potential inhibitory effect on HPV with an increase in the transpulmonary shunt during OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This effect, however, was not supported by clinical trials that compared inhaled agents with TIVA [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 6.1.2. COMPARATIVE STUDIES: INHALED VS. INTRAVENOUS AGENTS

#### 6.1.2.1. INFLAMMATORY RESPONSE STUDIES

**De Conno et al.** compared the effect of propofol and sevoflurane on the pulmonary inflammatory response before and after OLV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. There was an attenuated inflammatory reaction with sevoflurane associated with significantly fewer pulmonary complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Schilling et al.** compared the effects of desflurane, sevoflurane, and propofol on pulmonary and systemic inflammation in patients undergoing open thoracic surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. They found that the increase in alveolar concentrations of proinflammatory mediators in the ventilated lung was suppressed by desflurane and sevoflurane, while it was enhanced during propofol anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. However, that increase was not associated with higher incidence of postoperative pulmonary complications [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 6.1.2.2. CLINICAL OUTCOMES STUDY

In a prospective study, **Beck-Schimmer et al.** compared desflurane with propofol in 240 patients undergoing OLV and found no difference in complications during hospital stay and within a 6-month postoperative period [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

💡 **Evidence summary**: To date, there is a lack of solid evidence that the use of TIVA overall necessarily benefits the patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 6.1.3. PRECONDITIONING WITH INHALED AGENTS

#### 6.1.3.1. LUNG PROTECTIVE MECHANISMS

Preconditioning with inhaled agents has a lung protective effect due to inhibition of proinflammatory mediators [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The proposed mechanism whereby inhaled anesthetics protect the lung is by maintaining the tight junctions between epithelial cells and preventing the leakage of inflammatory factors from the capillaries into the alveoli [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 6.1.3.2. ZONA OCCLUDENS 1 (OZ-1) PROTECTION

Zona Occludens 1 (OZ-1) is a tight junction protein that acts as a scaffold which anchors the cells to prevent capillary leakage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Englert et al. demonstrated in an animal lung model, with endotoxin-induced inflammation and injured epithelium, that the normally observed decrease in tight junction protein OZ-1 was prevented by preconditioning with isoflurane [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 6.1.3.3. GLYCOCALYX PRESERVATION

Another mechanism by which inhaled anesthetics protect the lung is the preservation of glycocalyx in the epithelial cells [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The glycocalyx is a carpet of microtubules that lines the inner surface of the capillary endothelial cells, and it regulates the normal patterns of fluid fluxes across semipermeable membranes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. An intact glycocalyx layer tends to decrease leukocyte and platelet adhesion to the endothelium [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Ischemia-reperfusion protection**: Deterioration of the glycocalyx during ischemia-reperfusion injury and the presence of inflammatory mediators such as cytokines contribute to increased vascular permeability [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It has been demonstrated that sevoflurane stabilizes and reduces glycocalyx shedding in postischemic vessels, which prevents the postischemic adhesion of leukocytes and platelets in a dose-dependent manner `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This may explain the beneficial outcomes associated with the use of volatile anesthetics after ischemia-reperfusion [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 6.2. TUBELESS THORACIC SURGERY
**Tubeless thoracic procedures are procedures that are performed without an endotracheal tube while the patient is spontaneously breathing.**

### 6.2.1. DEFINITION EVOLUTION

Initially defined as an "awake" procedure, the term was updated to "tubeless" since these procedures are performed with sedation/regional block while maintaining spontaneous ventilation `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Avoiding PPV fulfills the goal of performing a less traumatic, safe operation with decreased postoperative pain and an expeditious postoperative recovery `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 6.2.2. HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT

In 2004, **Pompeo et al.** reported the resection of pulmonary nodules by conventional VATS under intravenous anesthesia without endotracheal intubation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Under direct thoracoscopic vision, the vagus nerve was blocked using lidocaine sprayed to effectively abolish the cough reflex [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 6.2.2.1. CLINICAL EXPERIENCE

A report of 40 consecutive patients, who underwent uniportal tubeless VATS pulmonary resection using multilevel intercostal nerve blocks and intrathoracic vagal block with no in-hospital mortality, concluded that nonintubated uniportal VATS appears to be a feasible and valid surgical option for appropriately selected patients, depending on the surgeon's experience [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 6.2.3. INSTITUTIONAL EXPERIENCE

The Tor Vergata Hospital in Rome, Italy, has formed the "Awake Thoracic Surgery Research Group" dedicated to performing thoracic operations in nonintubated patients `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. They report more than 1,000 various tubeless procedures successfully performed with a low conversion rate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Some of these procedures were performed uniportal tubeless [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 6.2.3.1. META-ANALYSIS RESULTS

In a large meta-analysis by **Zhang et al.**, tubeless VATS was compared with conventional VATS `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Tubeless VATS patients had [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Lower postoperative complication rate
* Shorter global in-OR time
* Shorter hospital stay
* Shorter anesthesia time
* Shorter chest-tube placement time
* Reduced chest pain
* Lower perioperative mortality rate

### 6.2.4. QUALITY OF LIFE OUTCOMES

The quality of life after tubeless versus intubated VATS pleurodesis for malignant pleural effusion was evaluated in a study of 198 tubeless and 193 intubated pleurodesis patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The tubeless group had [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Shorter total OR time
* Shorter duration of postoperative pleural fluid drainage
* Shorter postoperative hospital stay
* Lower mortality and morbidity
* Lower costs
* Similar quality of life between groups
* Significantly better dyspnea and early postoperative improvement in physical function

### 6.2.5. PHYSIOLOGICAL ADVANTAGES OF TUBELESS APPROACH

#### 6.2.5.1. AVOIDANCE OF PPV SIDE EFFECTS

Exposure to positive pressure is not beneficial to the lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. There is a clear advantage for the lung when it is not subjected to positive pressure ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Mechanical ventilation has a number of potential side-effects such as [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Pressure-induced injury
* Lung overdistension from volutrauma
* Release of a variety of proinflammatory mediators
* Atelectasis
* Potential laryngeal or tracheal injuries

#### 6.2.5.2. MUSCLE FUNCTION PRESERVATION

Avoidance of muscle relaxants will reduce the degree of atelectasis in the dependent zone of the lung from diaphragmatic dysfunction, and will maintain ventilation–perfusion matching [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Eliminating the potential for residual neuromuscular blockade is particularly important in the thoracic surgical patient with compromised lung mechanics due to hyperinflation, or in MG [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 6.2.5.3. PATIENT COMFORT AND SATISFACTION

Avoidance of airway irritation, chest tube, or urinary catheter insertion ultimately will improve patient satisfaction `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Finally, tubeless procedures offer the potential for surgery to patients who may otherwise be considered too high risk for general endotracheal anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 6.2.6. REGIONAL ANESTHESIA TECHNIQUES

#### 6.2.6.1. AVAILABLE REGIONAL BLOCKS

Several regional techniques have been used, including [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Thoracic epidural
* Paravertebral block
* Intercostal block
* Serratus anterior plane block
* Transversus thoracic plane block

#### 6.2.6.2. TECHNIQUE SELECTION CONSIDERATIONS

* **Thoracic epidural**: Can provide analgesia for the procedure and the postoperative period, but it would be hard to justify placing an epidural for procedures of short duration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Alternative blocks**: Such as serratus anterior plane block, or transverse thoracic plane block depend on the patient, the procedure and the anesthesiologist's comfort and preference [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 6.2.6.3. SURGICAL SUPPLEMENTATION

The surgeon can supplement with vagal nerve blockade and local anesthetic spray of the pleura to block the cough reflex [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 6.2.7. SEDATION MANAGEMENT

Sedation can be managed with [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Propofol infusion
* Fentanyl
* Remifentanil
* Dexmedetomidine
* Ketamine

### 6.2.8. OXYGENATION STRATEGIES

Oxygen saturation should be maintained using [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Nonrebreather oxygen facemask
* High-flow nasal cannula
* Positive-pressure nasal oxygen mask
* LMA

### 6.2.9. PATIENT SELECTION AND CONSIDERATIONS

#### 6.2.9.1. PATIENT REQUIREMENTS

A number of issues must be considered for tubeless procedures. The patient must [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Be able to cooperate
* Have a BMI <40
* Should not have a difficult airway in case an emergency intubation is needed

#### 6.2.9.2. PROCEDURE AND TEAM REQUIREMENTS

* **Careful procedure selection**: Required [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Surgeon experience**: Surgeon should be comfortable and skilled in tubeless procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Communication**: Close communication and collaboration between the surgeon and the anesthesiologist are crucial [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 6.2.10. PHYSIOLOGICAL CHALLENGES AND MANAGEMENT

#### 6.2.10.1. HEMODYNAMIC ISSUES

**Hypotension**: Can result from mediastinal shift [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 6.2.10.2. RESPIRATORY ISSUES

**Paradoxical breathing**: Can occur, in which air from the nondependent lung moves into the dependent lung in inspiration, and in the opposite direction during exhalation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This wasted ventilation results in hypoxemia and hypercarbia that can be treated by applying a slight positive pressure with the face mask, which will usually stabilize the patient immediately [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 6.2.11. CURRENT STATUS AND GUIDELINES

At the present time, there are no clear guidelines for tubeless procedures and the extent of the procedures performed varies from country to country [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 6.3. ROBOTIC-ASSISTED THORACOSCOPIC SURGERY
**Robotic-assisted thoracoscopic surgery (RATS) offers the surgeon three-dimensional depth perception and allows fine motor movements and significant degrees of freedom at the tip of the robotic instruments.**

### 6.3.1. CURRENT TECHNOLOGY

Currently, the Da Vinci surgical robotic system (Intuitive Surgical, Sunnyvale, CA, USA) is the only available system with a cost of around $2 million [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 6.3.2. PROCEDURE APPLICATIONS

A variety of thoracic procedures are performed using RATS, including [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Wedge resection
* Lobectomy or pneumonectomy
* Thymectomy
* Resection of mediastinal masses
* Esophageal procedures

### 6.3.3. COST-EFFECTIVENESS CONTROVERSIES

The use of robotic procedures ignited controversies regarding the role and cost of this approach [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Comparative study**: A retrospective study compared 184 patients undergoing RATS versus VATS lung resection and found no improvement in any clinical outcomes, while RATS procedures took longer and cost significantly more [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Cost comparison**: Another study found that a RATS lobectomy cost more than twice that of VATS lobectomy, but less than half of an open lobectomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 6.3.4. ANESTHETIC IMPLICATIONS OF RATS

#### 6.3.4.1. INCREASED PORT REQUIREMENTS

First, these procedures are relatively invasive and require the establishment of at least four ports, which is in contrast to the trend of performing minimally invasive surgery such as a uniportal approach [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The increase in the number of ports will require diligent management of postprocedure pain and an epidural or paravertebral block should be considered `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Pain considerations**:
* Potential intercostal nerve damage from the ports may lead to chronic postoperative pain [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* However, the combination of a smaller port size and a different angulation of the instruments in the incisions is believed to make RATS surgery less painful than VATS [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 6.3.4.2. CO₂ INSUFFLATION MANAGEMENT

Second, in many RATS procedures, the surgeon insufflates the hemithorax with CO₂ to enhance lung collapse on the operative side [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Ventilation strategy**: Pressure-controlled ventilation may provide superior PaO₂ and lower peak airway pressures during CO₂ insufflation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Hemodynamic effects**: CO₂ insufflation often causes hypotension, so the anesthesiologist must remain in close communication with the surgeon to reduce the insufflation pressure, if necessary [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Vigilance requirements**: The anesthesiologist must be vigilant to the possibility of DLT/BB displacement from movement of the tracheobronchial tree and frequent variation in the delivered VT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 6.3.4.3. PATIENT POSITIONING CHALLENGES

Third, during RATS, the patient may be turned 90 degrees away from the anesthesia workstation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This orientation makes it difficult for the anesthesiologist to perform fiberoptic bronchoscopy during the procedure to monitor the correct positioning of the DLT `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Solution**: The use of a DLT with an embedded video camera provides continuous visualization of the carina and potentially earlier indication of impending displacement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 6.3.5. COMPARATIVE EFFECTIVENESS

#### 6.3.5.1. ONGOING DEBATE

Whether RATS is superior to VATS for lobectomy remains the subject of debate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. There are only a few reports comparing RATS lobectomy with conventional VATS lobectomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 6.3.5.2. LARGE-SCALE COMPARISON STUDY

One study compared in-hospital mortality, complications, length of stay, and cost for patients undergoing RATS lobectomy or VATS lobectomy; a total of 2,498 robotic-assisted and 37,595 thoracoscopic lobectomies were analyzed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. RATS lobectomy was found to be associated with [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Higher rate of intraoperative injury and bleeding than VATS lobectomy
* Significantly greater cost

### 6.3.6. MARKET TRENDS AND FUTURE CONSIDERATIONS

Despite these issues with robotic surgery, the number of RATS procedures is increasing worldwide, possibly due to the perception that robotic surgery is cutting-edge technology that may improve the reputation of an institution `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It is unclear at this time how much the availability of robotic surgery will attract patients `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

💡 **Future outlook**: Hopefully, when additional devices become available, commercial competition will drive the cost down [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Anesthetic choice for thoracic surgery should consider the protective effects of volatile agents, the expanding role of tubeless techniques in selected patients, and the unique challenges posed by robotic surgery including positioning, CO₂ insufflation effects, and the need for specialized monitoring approaches.

---

# 7. DIAGNOSTIC PROCEDURES AND INTERVENTIONS

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|Part B: Thoracic Surgery]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C1 Airway Oxygenation Ventilation|Part B: Airway Management]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|Part C: PC]]

> [!info]- Exam Context
> **Exam Weighting:** High (Essential diagnostic and therapeutic procedures)

---

## 7.1. BRONCHOSCOPY TECHNIQUES AND MANAGEMENT
### 7.1.1. HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT

Early bronchoscopes were rigid tubes, but in 1966, the Machida and Olympus Companies introduced the first practical fiberoptic bronchoscopes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Since then, they have been improved dramatically and have simplified many otherwise complicated bronchoscopies [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.1.2. INDICATIONS FOR BRONCHOSCOPY

**Table 38-5 Indications for Bronchoscopy**

#### 7.1.2.1. DIAGNOSTIC INDICATIONS

* **Symptoms**: Cough, hemoptysis, wheeze [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Imaging abnormalities**: Atelectasis, unresolved pneumonia, abnormal chest radiograph [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Disease assessment**: Diffuse lung disease, acute inhalation injury [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Preoperative evaluation**: Rule out metastases, assess local disease recurrence [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Neurological complications**: Recurrent laryngeal nerve palsy, diaphragm paralysis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Special conditions**: Exclude tracheoesophageal fistula, during mechanical ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Imaging procedures**: Selective bronchography [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 7.1.2.2. THERAPEUTIC INDICATIONS

* **Foreign body removal** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Secretion management**: Accumulated secretions, atelectasis, aspiration [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Infection treatment**: Lung abscess [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Airway management**: Reposition endotracheal tubes, placement of endobronchial tubes, positioning of double-lumen tubes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Surgical procedures**: Laser surgery of the airway [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 7.1.3. INSTRUMENT SELECTION

**Table 38-6 Instruments of Choice for Bronchoscopy**

#### 7.1.3.1. RIGID BRONCHOSCOPY INDICATIONS

* **Foreign bodies** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Massive hemoptysis** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Vascular tumors** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Small children** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Endobronchial resections** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 7.1.3.2. FIBEROPTIC/FLEXIBLE BRONCHOSCOPY INDICATIONS

* **Anatomical challenges**: Mechanical problems of neck [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Lesion location**: Upper lobe and peripheral lesions [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Bleeding considerations**: Limited hemoptysis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Mechanical ventilation**: During mechanical ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Infection assessment**: Pneumonia, for selective cultures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Airway management applications**: Positioning of double-lumen tubes, difficult intubation, checking position of endotracheal tube, bronchial blockade [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Selection factors**: Operator preferences and experience may play a major role in the choice of instrument [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.1.4. PREOPERATIVE ASSESSMENT

Before bronchoscopy is performed, the patient must be evaluated for [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Pulmonary conditions**: Chronic lung disease, respiratory obstruction, bronchospasm, coughing, hemoptysis
* **Infection status**: Infectivity of secretions
* **Medication review**: Current medications should be reviewed
* **Procedure planning**: Need for a more major procedure should always be anticipated: bronchoscopy may lead to thoracotomy or sternotomy

### 7.1.5. ANESTHETIC TECHNIQUES

#### 7.1.5.1. LOCAL ANESTHESIA APPROACH

**Patient preparation**:
* **Antisialagogue**: Patient should first be pretreated with a drying agent such as glycopyrrolate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Agent selection**: Local anesthetics most commonly used are lidocaine and tetracaine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ **Dosage consideration**: In all cases, total dose of anesthetic must be considered and potential for toxicity recognized [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Application methods**:
1. **Oropharyngeal anesthesia**:
   * Nebulizer can be used to spray oropharynx and base of tongue [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Patient may gargle with viscous (2%) lidocaine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Tongue held forward, and pledgets soaked in local anesthetic held in each piriform fossa using Krause forceps to achieve block of internal branch of superior laryngeal nerve [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

2. **Tracheal anesthesia**:
   * Transtracheal injection of local anesthetic [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Spraying vocal cords and trachea under direct vision using laryngoscope [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Spraying through suction channel of fiberoptic bronchoscope [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Superior laryngeal nerve block by external approach [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
   * Glossopharyngeal block to depress gag reflex [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

3. **Nasal preparation**: 
   * If fiberoptic bronchoscopy is to be performed transnasally, nasal mucosa should be pretreated topically with 4% cocaine, or viscous lidocaine may be administered through the nares [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Advantages and disadvantages**:
* **Advantages**: Patient who is awake, cooperative, and breathing spontaneously. Sedatives may be added to make patient more comfortable [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Disadvantages**: Poor tolerance of any bleeding by patient and occasional lack of patient cooperation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

⚠️ **Important safety consideration**: These blocks cause depression of airway reflexes, so patients must be kept on nothing-by-mouth status for several hours after the examination [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.1.5.2. GENERAL ANESTHESIA APPROACH

**Combined technique**: General anesthesia for bronchoscopy is often combined with topical laryngeal anesthesia so less general anesthesia is needed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Balanced technique options**:
* N₂O/O₂, incremental doses of an intravenous drug such as propofol, an opioid, and a neuromuscular blocking drug [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Potent inhalational anesthesia technique is also satisfactory [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Intravenous-based technique may be used to avoid contamination of the OR atmosphere by potent inhaled agents [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Scavenging**: If desired, limited scavenging of waste gases may be achieved by placing a suction catheter in the patient's oropharynx [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Ventilation**: Unless there is some contraindication, ventilation of the lungs is usually controlled [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

⚠️ **Myasthenic syndrome consideration**: In any patient undergoing a thoracic diagnostic procedure for a suspected malignancy, the possibility of the myasthenic syndrome with sensitivity to nondepolarizing muscle relaxants must always be considered. The doses of neuromuscular blocking drugs should be titrated to effect using a neuromuscular monitoring system [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.1.6. RIGID BRONCHOSCOPY TECHNIQUES

#### 7.1.6.1. EQUIPMENT DESCRIPTION

A modern rigid ventilating bronchoscope is essentially a hollow tube with a blunted, beveled tip [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Various sizes and designs are available; however, in all of them, a side arm is provided for connection to an anesthetic gas source [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.1.6.2. VENTILATION TECHNIQUES

**1. Apneic Oxygenation**:
* After preoxygenation and induction of general anesthesia, skeletal muscle paralysis, and cessation of intermittent PPV, the PaCO₂ increases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **CO₂ increase rate**: During the first minute, the increase is approximately 6 mmHg. Subsequently, the average rate of increase is 3 mmHg/min [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Oxygen delivery**: Oxygen is insufflated at 10 to 15 L/min through a small catheter placed above the carina [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ **Limitations**: The apneic period should be kept to the minimum necessary, particularly in high-risk patients, because the technique is limited by buildup of CO₂, respiratory acidosis, and cardiac dysrhythmias [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**2. Apnea and Intermittent Ventilation**:
* Oxygen and anesthesia gases are delivered to the bronchoscope via the anesthesia circuit [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Limitation**: Ventilation is possible only when the eyepiece is in place, which limits the period for instrumentation by the surgeon [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Technique**: Intermittent ventilation of the lungs is achieved by squeezing the reservoir bag [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Advantages**: Assuming a good bronchoscope fit in the airway, compliance is constantly monitored, the risk of barotrauma is reduced, and VT may be estimated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Disadvantages**: There may be a leak around the bronchoscope, which could lead to hypoventilation and hypercarbia. Packing of the oropharynx can reduce the leak [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**3. Sanders Injection System**:
* **Equipment**: Oxygen from a high-pressure source (50 psig) is delivered, using a controllable pressure-reducing valve and toggle switch, to a 2.5to 3.5-cm 18or 16-gauge needle inside and parallel to the long axis of the bronchoscope [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Mechanism**: When the toggle switch is depressed, the jet of oxygen entering the bronchoscope entrains room air, and the air-oxygen mixture resulting at the distal tip of the bronchoscope emerges at a pressure to provide adequate ventilation and oxygenation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Pressure characteristics**:
* The intraluminal tracheal pressure depends on the DP from the reducing valve, the size of the needle jet, the length, internal diameter, and the design of the bronchoscope [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* For each combination of gas-driving pressure, jet orifice, and bronchoscope diameter, only one inflation pressure can be attained, regardless of the volume or compliance of the lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* As long as the proximal end of the bronchoscope is open, the system is strictly pressure limited [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Advantages**: Because continuous ventilation is possible (because the presence of an eyepiece is not necessary for ventilation of the lungs), the duration of the bronchoscopy procedure is minimized, but the efficiency also permits extended bronchoscopy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Disadvantages**: Entrainment of air by the oxygen jet results in a variable FIO₂ at the distal end of the bronchoscope, ventilation of the lungs may be inadequate if compliance is poor, and adequacy of ventilation may be difficult to assess [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**4. Mechanical Ventilator**:
* Ventilation of the lungs may be achieved by connecting a mechanical ventilator to an anesthesia circuit that is connected to the bronchoscope side arm [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Disadvantage**: Presence of a leak of anesthesia gases, and consequentially, light anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**5. High-Frequency Positive-Pressure Ventilation (HFPPV)**:
* HFPPV has been used in conjunction with rigid bronchoscopy and has been compared with the Sanders injector in patients with tracheobronchial stenosis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Effectiveness**: With HFPPV of up to 150 breaths/min, blood gases were identical with both techniques. At a frequency of 500 breaths/min, oxygenation deteriorated and CO₂ was not removed effectively [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Advantage**: HFPPV has the advantage that the tracheobronchial wall remains immobilized during ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 7.1.7. FIBEROPTIC BRONCHOSCOPY

#### 7.1.7.1. TECHNOLOGICAL ADVANCEMENT

New generations of fiberscopes, with their improved optics and smaller diameters, have revolutionized bronchoscopy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The flexibility has also been applied in preoperative assessment of the airway, management of difficult tracheal intubations, endotracheal tube positioning and change, bronchial toilet, correct positioning of DLTs, bronchial blockade, and evaluation of the larynx and trachea [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.1.7.2. PATIENT PREPARATION

* **Nasal approach**: Nasal fiberoptic bronchoscopy under topical anesthesia is well tolerated by most awake patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Antisialagogue**: The administration of an antisialagogue such as glycopyrrolate is useful in reducing secretions [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Oral insertion**: Also possible in both awake and asleep patients and should be performed with a bite block in place to prevent damage to the bronchoscope [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 7.1.7.3. PHYSIOLOGICAL CHANGES ASSOCIATED WITH FIBEROPTIC BRONCHOSCOPY

**Hypoxemia effects**:
* In all patients, insertion of the fiberoptic bronchoscope is associated with hypoxemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* The average decline in PaO₂ is 20 mmHg and lasts for 1 to 4 hours after the procedure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* By 24 hours, the blood gas tensions are usually back to normal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Clinical recommendation**: It is therefore recommended that if the initial PaO₂ is 70 mmHg (FIO₂ = 0.21), bronchoscopy should be performed only with the administration of supplemental oxygen [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Oxygen delivery methods**:
* Mouth-held nasal prongs [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Special face mask with a diaphragm through which the fiberscope can be passed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Endotracheal tube with a T-piece diaphragm adapter [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Airway obstruction effects**:  
During and after fiberoptic bronchoscopy, patients experience increased airway obstruction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In a study of 35 patients, insertion of the bronchoscope was associated with [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Increase in FRC (17% to 30%)
* Decreases in PaO₂, vital capacity, FEV₁, and forced inspiratory flow
* All returned to baseline by 24 hours

These changes are believed to be secondary to direct mechanical activation of irritative reflexes in the airway and, possibly, to mucosal edema `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. They may be avoided if atropine, either intramuscular or aerosolized into the airway, is administered before the procedure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.1.7.4. EQUIPMENT SPECIFICATIONS AND CONSIDERATIONS

**Standard specifications**:
* **External diameter**: 5.7 mm [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Suction channel**: 2-mm diameter [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Air removal rate**: If suction at 1 atm is applied to the fiberscope, air is removed at a rate of 14 L/min [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Clinical implications**:
* If the fiberscope is in the airway, this causes decreases in FIO₂, PaO₂, and FRC, leading to decreased PaO₂ [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* ⚠️ **Important practice point**: Suctioning should therefore be kept brief [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Tube compatibility**: The adult fiberscope can be passed through endotracheal tubes of 7 mm or greater internal diameter [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Optimization strategy**: If fibroscopy is planned, an endotracheal tube of the largest possible diameter should be used [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.1.7.5. PEEP EFFECTS AND SAFETY CONSIDERATIONS

**Pressure effects**:
* Insertion of the bronchoscope also causes a significant PEEP effect that may result in barotrauma in ventilated patients [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* If PEEP is already being used, it should be discontinued before passage of the fiberscope [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Post-procedure monitoring**: A postendoscopy chest radiograph is advisable to exclude the presence of mediastinal emphysema or pneumothorax [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Small tube considerations**:  
In patients whose tracheas are intubated with endotracheal tubes of less than 7 mm internal diameter, the use of pediatric fiberscopes, which have smaller diameters, would be more appropriate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.1.7.6. JET VENTILATION THROUGH FIBERSCOPE

The suction channel of the adult fiberoptic bronchoscope has been used to oxygenate and ventilate the lungs of patients `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. By attaching a jet ventilation system (similar to that used to drive the Sanders injector for rigid bronchoscopy) to the suction connection at the head of a fiberoptic bronchoscope, successful ventilation of the lungs of patients undergoing gynecologic procedures was achieved `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Technical specifications**:
* **Driving pressure**: 50 psig of oxygen was used with a ventilatory rate of 18 to 20 breaths/min [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Patient selection**: This technique permitted adequate ventilation of patients with normally compliant lungs and chest walls [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

⚠️ **Safety consideration**: Ventilation of the lungs should be performed only with the tip of the instrument in the trachea because a more peripheral location may produce barotrauma [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.1.7.7. LASER APPLICATIONS

Neodymium-yttrium-aluminum garnet (Nd-YAG) lasers are used for the resection of obstructing and endobronchial lesions `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This procedure is performed under general anesthesia `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The lasers may be introduced into the bronchial tree through a fiberoptic bundle passed via the suction port of the fiberoptic bronchoscope `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Safety protocols**:
* **Oxygen management**: During laser resection, FIO₂ should be kept to a minimum and titrated to oxygen saturation (as continuously monitored by pulse oximeter) to make endotracheal fire less likely [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Rigid bronchoscope option**: Laser therapy of bronchial tumors is also possible using a rigid bronchoscope [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **HFPPV advantage**: HFPPV through a rigid bronchoscope provides satisfactory operating conditions for laser resection of tracheal tumors and has the advantage of producing airway immobility [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 7.1.8. COMPLICATIONS OF BRONCHOSCOPY

#### 7.1.8.1. RIGID BRONCHOSCOPY COMPLICATIONS

* **Mechanical trauma**: To teeth, bronchial or tracheal perforation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Hemorrhage** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Bronchospasm** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Foreign body complications**: Loss of surgical sponge into bronchial tree [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Airway complications**: Subglottic edema, barotrauma [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 7.1.8.2. FIBEROPTIC BRONCHOSCOPY COMPLICATIONS

The incidence of complications is much lower with fiberoptic bronchoscopy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Nevertheless, complications may arise owing to [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Drug-related**: Overdose with topical anesthetic [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Trauma**: Insertion trauma, local trauma, hemorrhage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Airway obstruction**: Related to passage of the instrument through an area of tracheal stenosis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Physiological**: Hypoxemia, bronchospasm [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 7.1.8.3. POST-PROCEDURE MANAGEMENT

In most cases, it is best to intubate the trachea with an endotracheal tube after bronchoscopy under general anesthesia `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This permits avoidance or treatment of some of these problems, particularly the increased airway irritability `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Intubation also facilitates effective suctioning of the trachea and bronchi, and allows the patient to recover more gradually from general anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.1.9. MONITORING DURING BRONCHOSCOPY

Monitoring during bronchoscopy should include [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Electrocardiogram
* Blood pressure cuff
* Precordial stethoscope
* Pulse oximeter

If thoracotomy is planned, an arterial cannula should also be placed, as well as other monitors (e.g., PA or CVP catheters) that may be indicated by the patient's condition [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 7.2. MEDIASTINAL MASS ASSESSMENT
**Patients with an anterior mediastinal mass may present a special problem for the anesthesiologist.**

### 7.2.1. CLINICAL CHALLENGE

Although such masses may cause obvious superior vena cava obstruction, they may also cause obstruction of major airways and cardiac compression, which are less obvious and may become apparent only on induction of anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.2.2. CASE REPORTS AND CLINICAL EVIDENCE

Many cases of anesthetic-related airway compression from anterior mediastinal mass have been reported [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:

**Case 1**: Total occlusion of the trachea starting 2 to 3 cm above the carina and extending to both main stem bronchi was observed, and a bronchoscope was passed through the obstruction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Case 2**: Extrinsic compression of the left main stem bronchus occurred on inspiration during recovery from anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Case 3**: Flow-volume studies were performed with the patient in the upright and supine positions, with marked reductions in FEV₁ and peak expiratory flow in the latter position. These findings suggested potential obstruction with onset of anesthesia; radiation therapy to the mediastinum was commenced, after which the flow-volume studies showed improved function [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 7.2.3. LARGE SERIES DATA

In a subsequent series of 105 patients with mediastinal masses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Intraoperative cardiorespiratory complications**: 38% incidence
* **Postoperative respiratory complications**: 11% incidence
* **No airway collapse**: No cases of airway collapse were reported during anesthesia

**Risk factors for increased complications**:
* Preoperative cardiorespiratory signs and symptoms [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Obstructive and restrictive dysfunction on pulmonary function tests [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Greater than 50% tracheal compression on CT scan [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 7.2.4. CONTRADICTORY EVIDENCE
