In another series of patients with mediastinal mass, four patients had abnormal spirometry but underwent general anesthesia without sequelae [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.2.5. EMERGENCY PREPAREDNESS

In severe cases of airway compression, the femoral vessels should be cannulated prior to induction of anesthesia so that if the airway is lost completely, cardiopulmonary bypass can be instituted immediately [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.2.6. PREOPERATIVE INTERVENTIONS

#### 7.2.6.1. RADIATION THERAPY CONSIDERATIONS

If the mass is sensitive to radiation therapy, this may shrink the tumor and make an induction of general anesthesia less hazardous `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. However, a serious potential disadvantage of preoperative radiation therapy is that it may affect tissue histologic appearance, thereby preventing an accurate diagnosis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.2.6.2. PEDIATRIC CONSIDERATIONS

If the patient is a child, it may be difficult to obtain tissue samples under local anesthesia alone, and there may be no adequate alternative to general anesthesia `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Pediatric safety data**:
* **Series of 44 patients** aged 18 years or younger with anterior mediastinal masses who underwent general anesthesia before radiation or chemotherapy: No fatalities occurred, but seven patients did have airway compromise [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Safety criteria**: In another report on a series of children, it was found to be safe to induce general anesthesia if the CT scan revealed that the tracheal cross-sectional area and peak expiratory flow rates were at least 50% of predicted [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 7.2.7. PATHOPHYSIOLOGY OF OBSTRUCTION

Airway obstruction caused by an anterior mediastinal mass has been attributed to changes in lung and chest wall mechanics associated with changes in position or with onset of paralysis in muscles that previously maintained airway patency [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.2.8. PREOPERATIVE EVALUATION PROTOCOL

It is important to determine in the history if the patient has dyspnea in the supine position and to examine the CT scan to determine the extent of the tumor and its effect on surrounding structures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.2.9. MANAGEMENT OF AIRWAY OBSTRUCTION

If such obstruction occurs, it may be relieved by [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Passage of a rigid bronchoscope or reinforced anode tube past the obstruction
* Direct laryngoscopy
* Changing the position of the patient

### 7.2.10. ALTERNATIVE ANESTHETIC APPROACHES

#### 7.2.10.1. AWAKE FIBEROPTIC INTUBATION

In a situation in which the biopsy procedure cannot be performed under local anesthesia and there is concern that muscle paralysis may result in airway compression, fiberoptic intubation of the awake patient followed by general anesthesia with spontaneous ventilation has been described `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Physiological rationale**: During spontaneous inspiration, the normal transpulmonary pressure gradient distends the airways and helps maintain their patency, even in the presence of extrinsic compression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.2.10.2. CASE REPORT OF COMPLICATIONS

Airway collapse and inability to ventilate have been reported in a previously asymptomatic patient with a mediastinal mass despite spontaneous ventilation with an inhaled anesthetic and an endotracheal tube `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This resulted in sudden cardiopulmonary collapse `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. PPV was impossible, a rigid bronchoscopy was requested and the surgeons began to prepare femoral vessel access for emergent cardiopulmonary bypass `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Fortunately, the airway patency was re-established when the patient's spontaneous respiratory efforts improved as he awoke from general anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.2.11. EMERGENCY MANAGEMENT PROTOCOL

The authors emphasize the need for immediate availability of a rigid bronchoscope and that if a patient is at high risk, then serious consideration should be given to insertion of femoral cannulae with cardiopulmonary bypass standing by before general anesthesia is induced `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

⚠️ **Critical timing consideration**: Cardiopulmonary bypass is not a suitable rescue modality unless the cannulae have been placed before induction because severe neurologic damage is likely to occur within the time required to achieve cannulation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 7.3. MEDIASTINOSCOPY PROCEDURES
**Mediastinoscopy was introduced as a means of assessing the spread of bronchial carcinoma.**

### 7.3.1. ANATOMICAL AND CLINICAL RATIONALE

The lymphatics of the lung drain first to the subcarinal and paratracheal areas, and then to the sides of the trachea, the supraclavicular areas, and the thoracic duct [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Examination of these nodes can provide a tissue diagnosis and greater selectivity of patients for thoracotomy `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.3.1.1. DIAGNOSTIC UTILITY

* **Right lung tumors**: Mediastinoscopy is most useful in right lung tumors because left lung cancers tend to spread to subaortic nodes that are more accessible by an anterior mediastinoscopy in the second or third interspace (Chamberlain procedure) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Thymus access**: The transcervical approach to the thymus is an adaptation of mediastinoscopy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 7.3.2. ANATOMICAL CONSIDERATIONS AND PROCEDURE

#### 7.3.2.1. PATIENT POSITIONING AND APPROACH

For cervical mediastinoscopy, the patient is placed in a reverse Trendelenburg (i.e., head-up) position, and the mediastinoscope is inserted into the superior mediastinum through a transverse incision just above the suprasternal notch `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The instrument is advanced along the anterior aspect of the trachea and passes behind the innominate vessels and the aortic arch `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.3.2.2. VULNERABLE STRUCTURES

The left recurrent laryngeal nerve is vulnerable as it loops around the aortic arch, and any of these structures may be traumatized `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.3.3. CONTRAINDICATIONS

#### 7.3.3.1. RELATIVE CONTRAINDICATIONS

* **Previous procedure**: Because of scarring, previous mediastinoscopy may be considered a contraindication to a repeat examination [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Vascular complications**: Superior vena cava obstruction, aneurysm of the thoracic aorta [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Anatomical distortion**: Tracheal deviation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 7.3.4. PREOPERATIVE EVALUATION

#### 7.3.4.1. ASSESSMENT REQUIREMENTS

Preoperative evaluation should include a search for [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Airway complications**: Airway obstruction or distortion
* **Imaging review**: Review of a CT scan is very helpful in this regard
* **Neurological assessment**: Evidence of impaired cerebral circulation, history of stroke
* **Paraneoplastic syndromes**: Signs of the Eaton-Lambert syndrome resulting from oat cell carcinoma

⚠️ **Blood availability**: Blood must be available for the procedure because hemorrhage from major vessels is a real risk and may be life-threatening [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.3.5. ANESTHETIC MANAGEMENT

#### 7.3.5.1. PREFERRED TECHNIQUE

Most surgeons and anesthesiologists prefer general anesthesia using an endotracheal tube and continuous ventilation because this offers a more controlled situation and greater flexibility in terms of surgical manipulation `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The anesthetic technique should include a muscle relaxant to prevent the patient from coughing because this may produce venous engorgement in the chest or trauma by the mediastinoscope to surrounding structures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.3.6. COMPLICATIONS AND MORBIDITY

#### 7.3.6.1. OVERALL INCIDENCE

* **Morbidity**: 1.5% to 3.0% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Mortality**: 0.09% [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 7.3.6.2. SPECIFIC COMPLICATIONS

**1. Hemorrhage (0.73%)**:
* **Most common complication** due to proximity of major vessels and vascularity of certain tumors [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Management**: Tamponade may be the only recourse, and thoracotomy or median sternotomy may be required to achieve hemostasis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Prevention**: Needle aspiration of any structure is essential before any biopsy is taken [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Hemodynamic management**: If severe bleeding occurs, induced arterial hypotension may be helpful in reducing the size of the tear in a vessel [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Venous bleeding considerations**: If bleeding is venous, fluids given via an upper limb vein may enter the mediastinum, in which case a large-bore catheter should be placed in a lower limb vein [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Air embolism risk**: A venous laceration may also result in air embolism, particularly if the patient is breathing spontaneously. Some recommend the use of a precordial Doppler probe if the risk of air embolism is likely [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**2. Pneumothorax (0.66%)**:
* **Second most common complication** [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Characteristics**: Usually right sided, often recognized at the time of the occurrence [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Management**: Treated according to the size. A symptomatic pneumothorax should be treated by chest tube decompression [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**3. Recurrent Laryngeal Nerve Injury (0.34%)**:
* **Permanence**: Permanent in 50% of these cases [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Mechanism**: The nerve may be damaged by the mediastinoscope or be involved in tumor [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Clinical consequences**:
  * **Unilateral injury**: May result in hoarseness [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
  * **Bilateral injury**: May result in upper airway obstruction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 7.3.6.3. PHYSIOLOGICAL COMPLICATIONS

**Autonomic Reflexes**:
* May be triggered by manipulation of the trachea or the aorta, the latter having pressor receptors located in the arch [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Vagally mediated reflexes may be blocked by atropine [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Innominate Artery Compression**:  
The mediastinoscope can inadvertently compress the innominate artery, causing reduced blood flow in [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Right subclavian artery
* Right vertebral artery
* Right common carotid artery

This can cause the appearance of a "factitious cardiac arrest" in which the right radial pulse disappears and no pulse oximetry reading can be obtained from the right hand, all in the presence of a normal electrocardiogram `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. A normal right radial pulse should return once the pressure on the innominate artery from the mediastinoscope is removed `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.3.6.4. CEREBROVASCULAR COMPLICATIONS

Compression of the innominate can be of much greater clinical severity if it causes reduced blood flow to the right hemisphere of the brain `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. There is a greater risk of right-sided cerebral ischemia if there is a history of impaired cerebral circulation or transient ischemic attacks or if a carotid bruit is present `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Transient left hemiparesis may occur after mediastinoscopy `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.3.7. MONITORING RECOMMENDATIONS

It is therefore recommended that [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Blood pressure be monitored in the left arm
* The right radial pulse be monitored continuously during mediastinoscopy
* A decrease in the right radial pulse amplitude is an indication for repositioning the mediastinoscope, especially in a patient with a history of cerebrovascular disease

### 7.3.8. ADDITIONAL RARE COMPLICATIONS

Other reported complications include [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Acute tracheal collapse
* Tension pneumomediastinum
* Mediastinitis
* Hemothorax
* Chylothorax

### 7.3.9. POST-PROCEDURE CARE

A chest radiograph taken in the immediate postoperative period is a useful precaution in all patients after mediastinoscopy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 7.4. VIDEO-ASSISTED THORACOSCOPIC SURGERY
### 7.4.1. DISTINCTION FROM MEDICAL THORACOSCOPY

#### 7.4.1.1. THORACOSCOPY (MEDICAL THORACOSCOPY)

Thoracoscopy (medical thoracoscopy) involves the insertion of an endoscope into the thoracic cavity and pleural space `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. It is used for [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Diagnosis of pleural disease, effusions, and infectious disease (especially in immunosuppressed patients and those with acquired immunodeficiency syndrome)
* Staging procedures
* Chemical pleurodesis
* Lung biopsy

**Practice setting**: Usually performed by the pulmonary physician in the clinic, under local anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Therapeutic applications**: Also used in therapeutic procedures such as CO₂ laser treatment of spontaneous pneumothorax or bullous emphysema and Nd-YAG laser vaporization of malignant pleural tumors `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.4.1.2. VATS (SURGICAL THORACOSCOPY)

VATS entails making small incisions in the chest wall, which allows the introduction of a video camera and surgical instruments into the thoracic cavity `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In general, it is performed by a thoracic surgeon in the OR under general anesthesia `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.4.2. HISTORICAL DEVELOPMENT

Although the first thoracoscopy was performed by Jacobeus in 1910, using what was at that time a cystoscope, in more recent years, the surgical techniques, instruments, and video technology have been improved to permit a wide variety of procedures to be performed using VATS `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.4.3. VATS PROCEDURE APPLICATIONS

#### 7.4.3.1. DIAGNOSTIC PROCEDURES

* Evaluation of pleural disease and effusions [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Staging of lung cancer [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Identification of parenchymal disease, including nodules, mediastinal tumors, and pericardial disease [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 7.4.3.2. THERAPEUTIC PROCEDURES

* Operations for pleural disease, including pleurodesis, decortication and drainage of empyema [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Resection of lung tissue or bullae [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Pericardial window or stripping [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Esophageal surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Major resections**: Lung lobectomies are now usually performed by VATS, and an increasing number are being performed robotically [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 7.4.4. ANESTHESIA CONSIDERATIONS FOR VATS

#### 7.4.4.1. PATIENT POSITIONING AND LUNG ISOLATION

**As with a traditional thoracotomy, the patient needs to be in the lateral decubitus position, and lung collapse is needed for adequate surgical exposure.**

This generally mandates the use of a lung-separation technique `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. VATS is most commonly performed under general anesthesia with OLV `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.4.4.2. ENHANCED NEED FOR OLV

**The need for OLV is much greater with video-assisted thoracoscopic surgery than with open thoracotomy because it is not possible to retract the lung during VATS, unlike during open thoracotomy.**

This is because during VATS, it is not possible to retract the lung, whereas during open thoracotomy lung retraction is possible `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.4.4.3. TIMING AND OPTIMIZATION OF LUNG DEFLATION

* **Early initiation**: Deflation of the nondependent (operated) lung should begin as soon as possible following tracheal intubation and patient positioning because it can take more than 30 minutes to achieve complete collapse of the lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Surgical timing**: Also, the surgeon enters the thoracic cavity much sooner during VATS than with open thoracotomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Facilitation techniques**: Suction applied to the airway can help facilitate a more rapid deflation of the lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 7.4.4.4. CO₂ INSUFFLATION MANAGEMENT

In some cases, carbon dioxide is insufflated into the pleural cavity to facilitate visualization `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Safety parameters**:
* **Pressure limitations**: Insufflation pressures should be maintained as low as possible and the CO₂ inflow rate kept less than 2 L/min [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Hemodynamic complications**: Higher pressures can cause mediastinal shift, hemodynamic compromise, increases in airway pressure, and increases in end-tidal CO₂ [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Clinical presentation**: The clinical picture of hemodynamic compromise is similar to that seen with tension pneumothorax [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

⚠️ **Critical threshold**: Significant hemodynamic changes can be produced when pressures as little as 5 mmHg are used to insufflate CO₂ into the chest cavity [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.4.4.5. OXYGENATION MANAGEMENT DURING VATS

* **CPAP limitations**: CPAP is commonly used for the treatment of hypoxemia during OLV for thoracotomy and is usually very effective. However, during VATS, CPAP interferes with the surgical exposure and is therefore best avoided [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Alternative strategy**: It is preferable to use PEEP to the nonoperated (dependent lung) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Tolerance considerations**: It may be necessary to tolerate a lower PaO₂ during VATS compared to an open thoracotomy [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 7.4.5. MEDICAL THORACOSCOPY ANESTHETIC OPTIONS

#### 7.4.5.1. ANESTHESIA CHOICES

This procedure may be performed using local, regional, or general anesthesia; the choice depending on the expected duration of the procedure and the physical status of the patient `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.4.5.2. REGIONAL ANESTHESIA CONSIDERATIONS

* **Pneumothorax risk**: Pneumothorax is a potential complication of an intercostal block, but it would not have clinical sequelae during a thoracoscopy because it is created as part of the surgical procedure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Surgical advantages**: The collapse of the lung provides the surgeon with a working space, and a chest tube is placed at the conclusion of the surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Additional blocks**: The addition of a stellate ganglion block helps suppress the cough reflex that is sometimes provoked during manipulation of the hilum of the lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 7.4.5.3. PHYSIOLOGICAL TOLERANCE

When air enters the pleural cavity under inspection, a partial pneumothorax occurs, permitting good surgical visualization `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Changes in PaO₂, PaCO₂, and cardiac rhythm are usually minimal when the procedure is performed using local or regional anesthesia `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

With local anesthesia, the spontaneous pneumothorax is usually well tolerated because the skin and chest wall form a seal around the thoracoscope and limit the degree of lung collapse `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Occasionally, however, the procedure is poorly tolerated, and general anesthesia must be induced `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.4.5.4. CONVERSION TO GENERAL ANESTHESIA

The insertion of a DLT with the patient in the lateral position may be difficult, in which case the patient may be temporarily placed in the supine position for the intubation `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

If general anesthesia is required, a DLT is preferable to an SLT because PPV via an SLT would interfere with endoscopic visualization `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In addition, if pleurodesis is being performed, general anesthesia through a DLT allows for re-expansion of the lung and avoids the pain associated with instillation of talc for recurrent pneumothorax `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 7.4.6. POSTOPERATIVE CONCERNS

#### 7.4.6.1. PAIN MANAGEMENT

There is less pain after VATS than after open thoracotomy, and an epidural catheter is usually placed before surgery only if there is a likelihood that a thoracotomy may need to be performed `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.4.6.2. CONVERSION CONSIDERATIONS

Although a lobectomy can be performed by VATS, sometimes, conversion to an open thoracotomy may be required `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.4.6.3. RECOVERY ADVANTAGES

The patient's respiratory function is better preserved after VATS, and their recovery is faster `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 7.4.6.4. POTENTIAL COMPLICATIONS

However, postoperative dysrhythmias, which commonly occur after thoracotomy, have also been reported after VATS `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Other complications that may occur include bleeding, pulmonary edema, and pneumonia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Bottom Line:** Diagnostic procedures and VATS represent essential components of thoracic anesthesia practice, requiring specialized knowledge of bronchoscopy techniques, understanding of mediastinal mass physiology, expertise in managing the unique challenges of minimally invasive surgery, and the ability to adapt anesthetic management to the specific requirements of each procedure type.

---

# 8. SPECIAL SITUATIONS AND COMPLEX CASES

> **Syllabus**: [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|Part B: Thoracic Surgery]] | [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#C4 Trauma Resuscitation and Crisis Management|Part B: Crisis Management]] | [[mmed_anaes_part_c_syllabus#2. Patient Care and Procedural Skills|Part C: PC]]

> [!info]- Exam Context
> **Exam Weighting:** High (Complex scenarios requiring specialized management)

## 8.1. HIGH-FREQUENCY VENTILATION APPLICATIONS
**Management of patients with BPF, empyema, cysts, and bullae, as well as those requiring tracheal reconstruction, is considered here.**

Many of these patients are appropriately managed using high-frequency ventilatory techniques; therefore, these techniques are described first [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.1.1. PHYSIOLOGICAL PRINCIPLES

#### 8.1.1.1. GAS TRANSPORT MECHANISMS

With conventional PPV, VT and respiratory rates usually exceed or approach those in the normal, spontaneously breathing patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Gas transport to the alveoli occurs by convection in the larger airways, and then by convection and molecular diffusion in the more distal airways and alveoli [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

High-frequency ventilation differs from conventional PPV in that smaller VT and more rapid rates are used [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Gas transport may depend more on [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Molecular diffusion
* High-velocity flow
* Coaxial gas flow in the airways, with gas in the center moving distally and that in the periphery moving proximally

### 8.1.2. TYPES OF HIGH-FREQUENCY VENTILATION

#### 8.1.2.1. HIGH-FREQUENCY POSITIVE-PRESSURE VENTILATION (HFPPV)

* **Rate**: 60 to 120 breaths/min (1 to 2 Hz) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Tidal volume**: Small VT at rapid rates [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Ventilator characteristics**: Uses negligible internal compliance so the VT generated, which usually approximates the dead space volume, equals the volume set on the ventilator and represents all fresh gas [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Gas flow advantage**: The high instantaneous gas flows that are generated facilitate gas exchange and movement in the conducting airways [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**System configurations**:

*Open system example*:
* Percutaneous placement of a transtracheal catheter [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Placement of a catheter through the nose or mouth with its distal end above the carina [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Inflow is intraluminal and outflow is extraluminal [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Applications**: Used during bronchoscopy, tracheal resection, and reconstructive surgery [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Limitations**: Gas outflow pathway is not established mechanically and depends on natural airway patency, subject to compromise. Also, aspiration is a potential complication [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

*Closed system (superior)*:
* Integrates both airway patency and outflow protection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Example**: Catheter placed in short segment of endotracheal tube for delivery of HFPPV, with remainder of tube lumen representing exit pathway for gas [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Specialized equipment**: A quadruple-lumen endotracheal tube (Hi-Lo Jet Tracheal Tube, Mallinckrodt, Inc.) has been designed specifically for delivery of HFPPV with one lumen for HFPPV delivery, one for gas outflow, one for cuff inflation, and one for measuring airway pressures at distal end of tube [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **PEEP capability**: Use of closed system also permits application of PEEP, not possible with open arrangement [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 8.1.2.2. HIGH-FREQUENCY JET VENTILATION (HFJV)

* **Mechanism**: Uses a pulse of a small jet of fresh gas introduced from a high-pressure source (50 psig) into the airway through a small catheter or additional lumen in an endotracheal tube [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Rate**: Usually 100 to 400 breaths/min [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Gas delivery**: The fresh gas jet entrains gas from an injection cannula side-port reservoir [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Similarity**: This system is somewhat analogous to the Sanders injector system described in the "Bronchoscopy" section, and FIO₂ is similarly variable [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Gas movement**: The jet and entrained gas flows cause forward motion of the mass of gas in the airways [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **System options**: HFJV can be used with an open system or with a closed arrangement. In the latter, PEEP may be added to enhance oxygenation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Anesthetic delivery**: With use of high fresh gas flows from an anesthesia circuit, inhaled anesthetics may be delivered as an entrained gas mixture [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 8.1.2.3. HIGH-FREQUENCY OSCILLATION VENTILATION

* **Mechanism**: Uses a mechanism that oscillates gas at rates of 400 to 2,400 breaths/min [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Application**: Has not been described in association with thoracic surgical procedures [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Tidal volume**: VT is small (50 to 80 mL) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Gas exchange**: Occurs through enhanced molecular diffusion and coaxial airway flow [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 8.1.3. ADVANTAGES OF HIGH-FREQUENCY VENTILATION IN THORACIC ANESTHESIA

**The potential advantages offered by HFPPV during thoracic anesthesia are that lower tidal volume and inspiratory pressures result in a quiet lung field for the surgeon, with minimal movements of airway, lung tissue, and mediastinum.**

HFPPV has been used to ventilate both the nondependent and the dependent lung during thoracic surgical procedures, with adequate arterial blood gas measurements obtained throughout `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. At high frequencies (>6 Hz), however, CO₂ retention may become a problem `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.1.3.1. APPLICATIONS IN ONE-LUNG VENTILATION

HFJV has been used to ventilate the nondependent lung to improve PaO₂ during one-lung anesthesia, whereas the dependent lung is ventilated with conventional intermittent PPV `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. PaO₂ increased compared with that obtained during simple collapse of the nondependent lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

A study comparing HFJV with CPAP to the nondependent lung during conventional intermittent PPV to the dependent lung found that both improved PaO₂ significantly during closed and open stages of the surgery `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Open chest**: HFJV maintained satisfactory cardiac output, whereas CPAP usually decreased cardiac output [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **No significant differences**: In PaCO₂ between HFJV and CPAP [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

💡 **Clinical consideration**: Because similar increases in PaO₂ may be obtained using selective CPAP to the nondependent lung and with much simpler equipment than that necessary to deliver high-frequency ventilation, the use of CPAP would seem preferable to high-frequency ventilation to increase PaO₂ during most one-lung anesthesia situations [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.1.4. SPECIFIC APPLICATIONS

#### 8.1.4.1. BRONCHOPLEURAL FISTULA MANAGEMENT

The lower pressures and VT associated with high-frequency ventilation result in only a small leak through BPFs, and HFJV is now generally considered the conservative treatment of choice in this condition `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.1.4.2. SURGICAL APPLICATIONS

Another advantage of high-frequency ventilation is that the rapid-rate small VT can be delivered through small tubes or catheters so if an airway has to be divided, the passage of a small tube across the surgical field permits ventilation of the distal airway and lung tissue `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This use has been applied during [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Sleeve resection of the lung
* Tracheal reconstruction
* Surgery for tracheal stenosis

In all three situations, the surgeon is able to work easily around the small catheter used to provide the high-frequency ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 8.2. BRONCHOPLEURAL FISTULA AND EMPYEMA MANAGEMENT
**A BPF is an abnormal communication between the bronchial tree and the pleural cavity.**

### 8.2.1. DEFINITION AND ETIOLOGY

Occasionally, there is an additional communication to the surface of the chest, a cutaneous BPF [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. 

#### 8.2.1.1. COMMON CAUSES

* **Most common**: BPF occurs most commonly after pulmonary resection for carcinoma [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Other causes**: Traumatic rupture of a bronchus or bulla (sometimes caused by barotrauma or PEEP), penetrating chest wound, or spontaneous drainage into the bronchial tree of an empyema cavity or lung cyst [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Incidence**: The incidence of BPF is higher after pneumonectomy than following other types of lung resection [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 8.2.2. PATHOPHYSIOLOGICAL PROBLEMS

The problems associated with BPF and empyema are that PPV may result in [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Contamination of healthy lung
* Loss of air
* Decreased alveolar ventilation leading to CO₂ retention
* Development of a tension pneumothorax

### 8.2.3. PREOPERATIVE MANAGEMENT

#### 8.2.3.1. EMPYEMA DRAINAGE

If an empyema is present, it should be drained under local anesthesia before any surgery to close the BPF `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Patient positioning**: Drainage is performed with the patient sitting up and leaning toward the affected side [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Drainage challenges**: Empyemas are often loculated, and complete drainage is not always possible [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Drainage system**: A drain to an underwater seal system is left in the cavity before administration of anesthesia for surgery of the BPF [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Post-drainage assessment**: After drainage of an empyema, a chest radiograph should be obtained to determine the efficacy of the procedure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 8.2.4. ANESTHETIC MANAGEMENT PRIORITIES

**The priorities in the anesthetic management of BPF are the isolation of the affected side in terms of contamination and ventilation.**

#### 8.2.4.1. IDEAL APPROACH

The ideal approach is intubation of the trachea while the patient is awake using a DLT with the patient breathing spontaneously `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Supplemental oxygen**: Should be administered [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Patient comfort**: The patient should be constantly reassured [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Sedation**: Neuroleptanalgesia is satisfactory in providing a suitably cooperative patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Airway preparation**: The airway is then pretreated with topical anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 8.2.4.2. DLT SELECTION STRATEGY

The endobronchial tube selected should be such that the bronchial lumen is on the side opposite the BPF `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Selection of the largest possible tube provides a close fit in the trachea, which helps stabilize the tube `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.2.4.3. POST-INTUBATION MANAGEMENT

Once the tube is adequately positioned in the trachea, there may be a considerable outpouring of pus from the tracheal lumen if an empyema is present; therefore, this lumen should be immediately suctioned using a large-bore suction catheter `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The healthy and possibly the affected lung may then be ventilated; adequacy of oxygenation and ventilation is assessed by pulse oximetry and arterial blood gas analysis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.2.5. ALTERNATIVE ANESTHETIC TECHNIQUES

#### 8.2.5.1. GENERAL ANESTHESIA WITH SPONTANEOUS VENTILATION

An alternative technique is to insert the DLT under general anesthesia, with the patient breathing spontaneously to avoid a tension pneumothorax `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.2.5.2. SINGLE-LUMEN TUBE APPROACH

In patients who do not have an empyema, use of an SLT has been described and may be satisfactory if the BPF and air leak are small `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.2.5.3. HIGH-RISK RAPID SEQUENCE (NOT RECOMMENDED)

A rapid-sequence induction with ketamine or propofol followed by a relaxant has also been described, but is associated with considerable risk of contamination and tension pneumothorax [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.2.6. CRITICAL SAFETY CONSIDERATIONS

With either technique, the chest drainage tube must be left unclamped to avoid any bouts of coughing and to prevent the buildup of a tension pneumothorax in the event that a predisposing valvular mechanism exists [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.2.7. CONSERVATIVE MANAGEMENT TECHNIQUES

#### 8.2.7.1. SELECTIVE BRONCHIAL INTUBATION

BPF may also be treated conservatively using various ventilatory techniques. Thus, the bronchus of the normal lung may be intubated and ventilated, allowing the BPF the opportunity to rest and heal `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This approach may result in an intolerable shunt, however, and PEEP may be necessary to maintain PaO₂ [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.2.7.2. DIFFERENTIAL LUNG VENTILATION

Differential lung ventilation using a DLT has also been described, the healthy lung being ventilated with normal VT, while the affected lung is exposed to a smaller VT or to CPAP with oxygen at pressures just below the critical opening pressure of the fistula `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Critical opening pressure assessment**: The critical opening pressure of the BPF can be assessed by determining the lowest level of CPAP that must be applied to the bronchus on the affected side to produce continuous bubbling through the underwater seal chest drain [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.2.7.3. HIGH-FREQUENCY JET VENTILATION

For a large BPF, HFJV may be the nonsurgical treatment of choice `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Mechanism of benefit**: The use of small VT results in minimal gas loss through the fistula, which may heal more quickly [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Additional advantages**: Hemodynamic effects are usually minimal and spontaneous efforts at ventilation are usually abolished, thereby decreasing the work of breathing and eliminating the need for relaxants or excessive sedation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

## 8.3. LUNG CYSTS AND BULLAE
**Air-filled cysts of the lung are usually bronchogenic, postinfective, infantile, or emphysematous.**

### 8.3.1. CLASSIFICATION AND PATHOPHYSIOLOGY

They may be associated with COPD or be an isolated finding [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. A bulla is a thin-walled space filled with air that results from the destruction of alveolar tissue `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The walls are, therefore, composed of visceral pleura, connective tissue septa, or compressed lung tissue `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In general, bullae represent an area of end-stage emphysematous destruction of the lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.3.2. SURGICAL INDICATIONS

Patients may be considered for surgical bullectomy when [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* Dyspnea is incapacitating
* The bullae are expanding
* There are repeated pneumothoraces owing to rupture of bullae
* The bullae compress a large area of normal lung

### 8.3.3. PATIENT POPULATION CHARACTERISTICS

Most of these patients have severe COPD and CO₂ retention, and little functional respiratory reserve [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.3.4. ANESTHETIC MANAGEMENT CONSIDERATIONS

#### 8.3.4.1. FUNDAMENTAL PRINCIPLE

The first consideration in management is maintenance of a high FIO₂ [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.3.4.2. PATHOPHYSIOLOGICAL RISKS

If the bulla or cyst communicates with the bronchial tree, PPV may cause it to expand or even to rupture, if it is compliant, producing a situation analogous to tension pneumothorax `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. If the bulla is very compliant, most of the applied VT may be wasted in this additional dead space `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

⚠️ **Nitrous oxide contraindication**: Nitrous oxide should be avoided because it causes expansion of any air spaces in the body, including bullae [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.3.4.3. OPEN CHEST CONSIDERATIONS

Once the chest is open, even more of the VT may enter the compliant bulla, which is no longer limited by chest wall integrity, and an increase in ventilation is needed until the bulla is controlled [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.3.5. ANESTHETIC APPROACH

#### 8.3.5.1. IDEAL MANAGEMENT

Ideally, a DLT is inserted with the patient awake or under general anesthesia but breathing spontaneously `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The avoidance of PPV (when possible) helps decrease the likelihood of the potential problems described previously, although oxygenation may be precarious with spontaneous ventilation `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

Once the endotracheal tube is in place, each lung may be controlled separately, and adequate ventilation can be applied to the healthy lung if bilateral disease is not present `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.3.5.2. GENTLE PPV PROTOCOL

Gentle PPV with rapid, small VT and pressures not to exceed 10 cmH₂O may be used during the induction and maintenance of anesthesia, especially if the bullae have been shown to have no or only poor bronchial communication by preoperative ventilation scanning `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.3.5.3. INTRAOPERATIVE MANAGEMENT

While the surgery is being performed, as each bulla is resected, the operated lung can be separately ventilated to check for air leaks and the presence of additional bullae [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.3.6. EMERGENCY PREPAREDNESS

#### 8.3.6.1. TENSION PNEUMOTHORAX MANAGEMENT

If PPV is to be applied before the chest is opened, the possibility of a tension pneumothorax must be kept in mind, and treatment should be readily available `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Diagnosis**:
* Unilateral decrease in breath sounds (this may be difficult to distinguish in a patient with bullous disease) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Increase in ventilatory pressure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Progressive tracheal deviation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Wheezing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* Cardiovascular changes [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Treatment**: Involves the rapid placement of a chest tube [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. An added risk of chest tube placement is the creation of a cutaneous BPF, which causes problems for ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.3.6.2. ALTERNATIVE EMERGENCY APPROACH

Alternatively, general anesthesia is induced only after the surgeon has prepared the operative field and draped the patient [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In the event of sudden deterioration in the patient's condition during induction, the surgeon may perform an immediate median sternotomy `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In any event, the time from induction of anesthesia to sternotomy must be kept to a minimum [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.3.7. HIGH-FREQUENCY VENTILATION APPLICATIONS

To avoid these problems in a patient with known bullae, HFJV has been used in a patient with a large bulla undergoing coronary artery bypass graft and in another patient undergoing bilateral bullectomy `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.3.8. BILATERAL BULLECTOMY MANAGEMENT

If bilateral bullectomy is to be performed, a median sternotomy is usually used `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Benumof described the use of sequential OLV using a DLT in the management of a patient needing bilateral bullectomy `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Surgical sequence**: The side with the largest bulla and least lung function, as assessed before surgery by ventilation and perfusion scans, should be operated on first `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In this way, the lung with the better function should support gas exchange first `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

**Hypoxemia management**: If hypoxemia develops during this one-lung situation, application of CPAP to the nonventilated lung during the deflation phase of a tidal breath should increase PaO₂ [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.3.9. POSTOPERATIVE CONSIDERATIONS

#### 8.3.9.1. IMPROVED LUNG FUNCTION

Unlike most cases of pulmonary resection, patients after bullectomy are left with a greater amount of functional lung tissue than was previously available to them, and the mechanics of respiration are improved [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.3.9.2. RECOVERY MANAGEMENT

At the end of the procedure, the DLT is replaced by an SLT, and the patients generally require several days to be weaned from the ventilator `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. During this time, the positive airway pressure used should be minimized to avoid causing a pneumothorax owing to rupture of suture or staple lines or of residual bullae [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 8.4. TRACHEAL RESECTION AND RECONSTRUCTION
**Tracheal resection and reconstruction are technically difficult for the surgeon and challenging for the anesthesiologist.**

### 8.4.1. INDICATIONS

Indications for this type of procedure include [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* **Congenital lesions**: Agenesis, stenosis
* **Neoplasia**: Primary or secondary
* **Injuries**: Direct or indirect
* **Infections**
* **Postintubation injuries**: Caused by an endotracheal tube or tracheotomy

### 8.4.2. MAJOR CHALLENGES

For the surgical team, the major problems are maintenance of ventilation to the lungs while the airway is being operated on and postoperative integrity of the anastomoses `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In this respect, the presence of lung disease sufficiently severe to require postoperative ventilatory support is a relative contraindication to tracheal resection or reconstruction [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.4.3. MONITORING REQUIREMENTS

Monitoring of these patients should include placement of an arterial cannula in the left radial artery to permit continuous measurement of blood pressure during periods of innominate artery compression `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.4.4. PREOPERATIVE PREPARATION

* **Steroids**: Should be administered to help reduce any tracheal edema [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **High FIO₂**: Should be used throughout the procedure to ensure an adequate oxygen reserve at all times in the FRC so that temporary interruptions of ventilation are less likely to produce hypoxemia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 8.4.5. VENTILATION METHODS

#### 8.4.5.1. SMALL-BORE TUBE TECHNIQUE

A small-bore reinforced anode tube may be placed through and distal to an upper tracheal lesion so resection may occur around the tube `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This technique is useful only in mild stenoses [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.4.5.2. CROSS-FIELD TECHNIQUE

Alternatively, an endotracheal tube may be passed through the glottis to above the stenosis, and a sterile endotracheal or bronchial tube may later be inserted into the trachea opened distal to the site of stenosis, with the sterile anesthesia tubing being led across the surgical field `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. After resection of the lesion, the sterile and distally placed endotracheal tube is withdrawn, and the upper tube (originally passed through the glottis) is advanced across the anastomosis [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.4.5.3. ENDOBRONCHIAL APPROACH

With low tracheal or bronchial lesions, resection and reconstruction may be performed around an endobronchial or DLT `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.4.5.4. PATIENT POSITIONING

During these procedures, the patient is kept in a head-down position to minimize aspiration of blood and debris into the alveoli, and ventilation must be carefully monitored throughout the procedure [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.4.6. HIGH-FREQUENCY VENTILATION APPLICATIONS

Clearly, the presence of a large-bore tube in the airway may make these resections technically difficult, and the use of high-frequency ventilation techniques may improve surgical access `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Thus, a small-diameter catheter or catheters may be placed across or through the stenotic lesion or transected airway(s) and ventilation to the distal airways and lungs maintained using HFPPV or HFJV [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.4.6.1. POTENTIAL DISADVANTAGES

Potential disadvantages of these high-frequency ventilation techniques are that, by necessity, the system is "open," and egress of gas during exhalation may be compromised if the stenosis is tight `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Also, the catheter may become occluded by blood and become displaced, and distal aspiration of debris or blood may occur `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.4.6.2. COMPLEX RESECTION MANAGEMENT

With complex resections, two anesthesia teams with two machines and anesthesia circuits or sets of ventilating equipment may be necessary to ensure adequate ventilation of the two distal airway segments `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. During carinal resections, HFPPV to the left lung alone usually provides adequate oxygenation and ventilation [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.4.7. POSTOPERATIVE CARE

#### 8.4.7.1. POSITIONING

After tracheal resection or reconstructive surgery, patients should be positioned with the neck and head flexed to reduce tension on the anastomotic suture lines `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In some cases, this is maintained by using sutures between the chin and the anterior chest wall [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.4.7.2. EARLY EXTUBATION

Extubation of the trachea is performed as early as possible to minimize tracheal trauma due to the endotracheal tube and cuff [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 8.5. BRONCHOPULMONARY LAVAGE
**This procedure involves irrigation of the lung and bronchial tree, and is used as a treatment for alveolar proteinosis, radioactive dust inhalation, cystic fibrosis, bronchiectasis, and asthmatic bronchitis.**

Lung lavage is performed under general anesthesia using a DLT so one lung may be ventilated while the other is being treated with lavage fluid [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.5.1. PREOPERATIVE ASSESSMENT

The preoperative assessment of these patients should include ventilation–perfusion scans so lavage can be performed first on the more severely affected lung (i.e., the one with the least ventilation) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. If involvement is equal, the left lung is generally lavaged first because gas exchange should be better through the larger, right lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Patients are premedicated and supplied with supplemental oxygen en route to the OR [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.5.2. ANESTHETIC MANAGEMENT

* **Induction**: Anesthesia is induced with an intravenous drug and maintained with an inhaled agent in oxygen to maintain the highest possible FIO₂ [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Muscle relaxation**: Facilitates placement of the DLT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Seal verification**: The cuff seal should be checked to maintain perfect separation at a pressure of 50 cmH₂O to prevent leakage of lavage fluid around the cuff [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Position confirmation**: A fiberoptic bronchoscope is useful to check the position of the bronchial cuff of the DLT [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

### 8.5.3. MONITORING REQUIREMENTS

Monitoring should include [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]:
* An arterial catheter
* A stethoscope placed over the ventilated lung to check for rales, the presence of which may indicate leakage of lavage fluid into this lung

### 8.5.4. PROCEDURE PREPARATION

The patient is maintained on an FIO₂ of 1 throughout the procedure `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Before lavage, this serves to denitrogenate the lungs so only oxygen and carbon dioxide remain `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Instillation of fluid then allows these gases to be absorbed, resulting in greater access by the fluid to the alveolar spaces than if the more insoluble nitrogen bubbles remained [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.5.5. LAVAGE PROCEDURE

Once the trachea is intubated, the patient is turned so the side to be lavaged is lowermost, and the DLT position and seal are checked once again `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. With the patient in a head-up position, warmed heparinized isotonic saline is infused by gravity from a reservoir 30 cm above the midaxillary line into the catheter to the dependent lung, while the nondependent lung is ventilated [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.5.5.1. FLUID MANAGEMENT

* **Inflow**: When fluid ceases to flow in (usually after 700 to 1,000 mL in an adult), the patient is placed in a head-down position and fluid is allowed to drain out [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Continuation criteria**: The lavage is continued until the effluent is clear (as opposed to the milky fluid that drains initially when lavage is being performed for alveolar proteinosis) [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Post-lavage management**: At which point the lung is suctioned and ventilation is re-established with large VT (and pressures) because compliance is decreased owing to loss of surfactant [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 8.5.5.2. CRITICAL MONITORING

With each lavage, inflow and outflow volumes are monitored so the patient is not "drowned" in fluid, and there is no excessive absorption or leakage to the ventilated side `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. At least 90% of the saline volume should be recovered with each lavage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.5.6. RECOVERY PROCESS

Two-lung ventilation is re-established and, as compliance improves, an air–oxygen mixture (i.e., addition of nitrogen) may be introduced to help maintain alveolar patency `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. After a further period of ventilation, in most patients, the trachea can be extubated in the OR `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In the posttreatment period, patients are encouraged to cough and engage in breathing exercises to fully re-expand the treated lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. From 3 days to 1 week after lavage of the first lung, the patient may return to the OR for lavage of the other lung [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.5.7. COMPLICATIONS AND MANAGEMENT

#### 8.5.7.1. SPILLAGE MANAGEMENT

Problems sometimes encountered with this procedure include spillage of lavage fluid from the treated to the ventilated lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This must be managed by stopping the lavage and ensuring functional separation of the lungs before continuing `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. DLT positioning is critical `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Spillage may cause profound decreases in oxygenation, which may necessitate terminating the procedure and maintaining two-lung ventilation with oxygen and PEEP [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.5.7.2. OXYGENATION CHANGES

During periods when lavage fluid is being instilled into the dependent lung, oxygenation usually improves because the increased intra-alveolar pressure caused by the fluid produces diversion of the pulmonary blood flow to the nondependent, ventilated lung `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Conversely, when the fluid is drained out of the dependent lung, hypoxemia may occur [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.5.7.3. ADVANCED MANAGEMENT FOR HIGH-RISK CASES

In some cases in which severe hypoxemia was anticipated during right lung lavage, the risk has been reduced by passing a balloon-tipped catheter into the right main PA (checked by radiography) and inflating the balloon during periods of right lung drainage `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In this way, blood flow to the dependent, right, nonventilated lung is minimized during periods of drainage `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. This technique is not without risk (e.g., PA rupture) and is reserved for those patients considered to be at greatest risk for hypoxemia during lavage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

### 8.5.8. SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS

#### 8.5.8.1. BRONCHOPLEURAL FISTULA

If the patient has recently had a diagnostic open lung biopsy, a BPF may be present `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. If this is a possibility, a chest tube should be inserted on the side of the BPF, and this side should be lavaged first `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The chest drain is removed several days later [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.5.8.2. PEDIATRIC LIMITATIONS

Limitations in the sizes of available DLTs preclude their use for lavage in patients weighing less than 40 kg `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. In such cases, cardiopulmonary bypass may be required to provide oxygenation during lavage [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

## 8.6. MYASTHENIA GRAVIS AND MYASTHENIC SYNDROME
### 8.6.1. MYASTHENIA GRAVIS

**The thoracic anesthesiologist will most likely have to manage patients with MG for thymectomy, which is now considered the treatment of choice in most cases of MG.**

#### 8.6.1.1. CLINICAL OVERVIEW

MG is a disorder of the neuromuscular junction, the function of which is altered routinely in the modern practice of anesthesia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The incidence of MG appears to be increasing `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The most accurate estimate of incidence of MG was around 30/1,000,000 per year `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. The incidence in children and adolescents aged 0 to 19 years was found to be between 1 and 5/1,000,000 per year [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. These rates may well be an underestimate of the true incidence rates, as mild cases will have been missed and cases in the elderly will have been misdiagnosed [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.6.1.2. DEMOGRAPHICS AND CLINICAL COURSE

People of any age may be affected, but peaks of incidence occur in the third decade for women and the fifth decade for men `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. MG is a chronic disorder characterized by a clinical course of fluctuating painless weakness and fatigability of voluntary muscles with improvement following rest `[[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]. Onset is usually slow and insidious, any skeletal muscle or group of muscles may be affected, and the condition is associated with relapses and remissions [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]].

#### 8.6.1.3. CLINICAL PRESENTATION

* **Most common onset**: Ocular; if the disease remains localized to the eyes for 2 years, the likelihood of progression to generalized MG is low [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Generalized disease**: In some cases, the disease is generalized and may involve the bulbar musculature, causing problems with breathing and swallowing [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Peripheral involvement**: May cause weakness, clumsiness, and difficulty in holding up the head or in walking [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

#### 8.6.1.4. CLINICAL CLASSIFICATION

**Table 38-7 Clinical Classification of Myasthenia Gravis (MG)**

**Class I**: Ocular myasthenia—involvement of ocular muscles only. Mild with ptosis and diplopia. Electrophysiologic testing of other musculature is negative for MG [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Class IA**: Ocular myasthenia with peripheral muscles showing no clinical symptoms but showing a positive electromyogram for MG [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]

**Class II**: Generalized myasthenia [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Class IIA**: Mild—slow onset, usually ocular, spreading to skeletal and bulbar muscles. No respiratory involvement. Good response to drug therapy. Low mortality rate [[MMed_Anaesthesiology_Part_B_Syllabus#S7 Thoracic surgery|ⓘ]]
* **Class IIB**: Moderate—as IIA but progressing to more severe involvement of skeletal and bulbar muscles. Dysarthria, dysphagia, difficulty ch
